PREVIEW 

The Individual Income Tax Act of 1944 is confined to the simplification of the individual income tax and according to the official report of the Senate Finance Committee accomplishes the following objectives: (a) Relieves the great majority of taxpayers from the necessity of computing their income tax. (b) Reduces the number of tax computations. (c) Simplifies the return forms. (d) Decreases the number of persons required to file declarations of estimated tax and eliminates difficulties and uncertainties in making such estimates.

The Act accomplishes these objectives without substantially changing the number of taxpayers or the revenue yield under existing law.

In accomplishing these objectives several important changes were made in the income tax law. 

First, in computing the surtax a uniform exemption of $500 per person is allowed as a credit for the taxpayer, the taxpayer's spouse, and each dependent. A dependent need no longer be under 18 or incapable of self-support. Instead the taxpayer may claim as a dependent anyone for whom he furnishes more than half the support, provided the person claimed as a dependent is closely related to the taxpayer and does not have a gross income of $500 or over.

Second, the victory tax is repealed and the former normal tax and surtax are combined into a single surtax. A new normal tax of 3 per cent is imposed on each person whose net income exceeds $500. In computing the new normal tax no credit is allowed for dependents.

Third, taxpayers with adjusted gross incomes of less than $5,000, regardless of the source of their income, may use an optional tax table in ascertaining their tax. Under this method, the taxpayer is allowed a standard deduction of approximately 10 per cent of his adjusted gross income. 

Fourth, taxpayers with adjusted gross incomes of $5,000 or more may elect to claim, in lieu of their actual deductions, a standard deduction of $500. 5

PREVIEW 

Fifth, beginning January 1, 1945, employers are required to withhold approximately the full tax liability on wages up to at least $5,000. 

Sixth, taxpayers filing declarations of estimated tax may amend their declarations on or before January 15 next following the close of the taxable year. This is a change trom December 15 in prior law.

Seventh, charitable contributions not exceeding 15 per cent of the adjusted gross income may be claimed as a deduction. 

Under prior law the deduction was limited to 15 per cent of net income. This change in the law simplifies the computation of the amount that may be deducted and also has the effect of increasing the maximum allowance.

Eighth, medical expenses are deductible to the extent that they exceed 5 per cent of adjusted gross income. Under prior law the deduction was limited to the excess of 5 per cent of net income. This change in the law results in a slight reduction in the medical expense allowance. 

Ninth, the term "adjusted gross income" means, in general, gross income minus business deductions. The term is used principally in connection with the use of the optional tax table. 

Tenth, anyone deriving more than two-thirds of his gross income from farming is considered a farmer. This is a change from 80 per cent or more under prior law. If a farmer files his final return on or before January 15 next following the close of the calendar year, he is not required to file a declaration of estimated tax. If he is unable to make a final return by January 15 he is permitted to make a declaration of estimated tax on or before January 15 and may file a final return on or before 

March 15. 

J. F. S. 

C. R. N.

CHAPTER ONE INTRODUCTION 1. Early Income Tax Laws 2. Withholding Income Taxes from Wages 

3. Quarterly Payment of Estimated Taxes Not Withheld

The Federal income tax is based upon net income, which may be defined as gross income less deductions, computed according to law. The amount of net income, as determined for tax purposes, is not the same as the amount determined by adherence to generally accepted principles of accounting.. A large proportion of the problems that arise in the preparation of income tax returns is concerned with the determination of net income. Some items of gross income are exempt from tax and hence need not be included in income. There are certain business expenses that are not deductible and, on the other hand, certain personal and family expenses are allowable deductions.

The income tax, unlike a real estate tax, is self-assessed. 

The responsibility for preparing the return rests upon the taxpayer. He must either prepare it himself or employ another to prepare it for him. It is the purpose of this volume to acquaint the student with the framework of the income tax law, to assist him in the reading and interpretation of the statutes, and to provide practice in the preparation of tax returns. 

Early Income Tax Laws. Income taxation in this country is not a recent development. It dates back to 1646 when the 

Massachusetts Bay Company enacted a law taxing the produce of estates. The first Federal income tax law was enacted in 

1861. It provided for a tax of 3% on the annual income of every person residing within the United States, who had a net income from all sources in excess of $800.00, and 5% upon income derived from sources within the United States by citizens residing outside of the United States. No tax was payable until June 30, 1862. Before the tax could be collected in the spring of 1862, the law was superseded by the Revenue Act of

[Ch. 1 

INTRODUCTION 

1862. It imposed a tax of 5% on the entire annual income of citizens residing abroad, and 3% on the income from all sources whatsoever of persons residing in the United States. On incomes in excess of $10,000.00 the rate was 5%. An exemption of $600.00 was allowed.

In 1864 the tax rates were graduated from 5% to 10%, the highest rate applying to incomes in excess of $10,000.00. 

These Civil War income tax laws continued in effect, with other minor amendments, until 1873.

The next attempt on the part of the Federal Government to levy an income tax was in 1894. Provision was made for an income tax in the Wilson Tariff Bill. In the following year the 

U. S. Supreme Court held it to be unconstitutional (Pollock vs. 

Farmers' Loan and Trust Company, 157 U. S. 429). The Con stitution provided that "no capitation or other direct tax shall be laid, unless in proportion to the census or enumeration hereinbefore directed to be taken." Thus there were two questions at issue was this a direct tax, and if so was it levied in proportion to population. A portion of the decision follows: "The tax ... so far as it falls on the income of real estate, and of personal property, being a direct tax, within the meaning of the Constitution, and therefore unconstitutional and void, because not apportioned according to representation, all these sections constituting one entire scheme of taxation, are necessarily invalid.”

In 1909 Congress enacted a third income tax law imposing a tax on corporations. This law was worded somewhat differently from the law of 1894, and the Supreme Court upheld it on the ground that it imposed an excise tax instead of a direct tax. 

In order to remove any doubt as to its power to levy taxes on incomes, Congress passed the Sixteenth Amendment to the Constitution on July 12, 1909. It was ratified by the required number of states and became effective on February 25, 1913. 

The amendment provides that "Congress shall have power to levy and collect taxes on incomes from whatever source derived, without apportionment among the several states, and without regard to any census or enumeration." On October 3, 1913, Congress passed a law known as The Revenue Act of 1913, which imposed a tax on the incomes of both individuals and corporations accrued after March 1, 1913. It provided for a normal tax of 1% and a surtax graduated from 1% to 6%.

Since March 1, 1913, a series of internal revenue acts have been enacted with a result that the present income tax statutes bear little resemblance to the parent law of 1913.

Ch. 1] 

INTRODUCTION 

Prior to January 1, 1943, income taxes were payable in the year following the year in which the income was received or earned. In other words, out of the current year's income the taxpayer was required to pay the income taxes incurred in the preceding year. This plan proved satisfactory when the imposition of taxes was limited to persons of relatively large incomes. 

With the beginning of World War II, the need on the part of the government for greatly increased revenue resulted in the enactment of tax laws imposing taxes on millions of persons with small incomes derived primarily from wages. With this development came a persistent demand from taxpayers that some provision be made for paying the tax out of current income in the same year in which the liability is incurred. Eventually this led to a law providing for the current payment of income taxes-- (a) by requiring employers to withhold a percentage of the wages paid to their employees; and (b) by requiring individuals to make quarterly payments of that portion of their estimated taxes which is not withheld from their wages. 

Withholding Taxes at the Source. Since January 1, 1943, employers have been required to withhold a percentage of the wages paid to their employees in excess of the withholding exemption for income tax purposes. Prior to July 1, 1943, the withholding tax amounted to 5% of the wages paid in excess of the exemption. This was intended to cover the amount of the victory tax imposed under the Revenue Act of 1942. Under that law a victory tax of 5% was imposed on the income in excess of $624 of every individual other than nonresident aliens. 

Beginning July 1, 1943, employers were required to withhold a tax of 20% of the amount paid in excess of the exemption. 

This was intended to cover the victory tax and at least a part of the income tax. 

Beginning January 1, 1945, employers are required to withhold from the wages of their employees a tax equal to the sum of the following: (a) 2.7% of the excess of the total wages over the amount of one withholding exemption as shown in the first column of the following table. (b) 18% of the excess of the total wages over the amount of one withholding exemption, multiplied by the number of withholding exemptions claimed, but not in excess of the amount shown in the last column of the following table.

[Ch. 1 

INTRODUCTION 

(c) 19.8% of the excess of the total wages over the amount of one withholding exemption, multiplied by the number of withholding exemptions claimed, plus the amount shown in the last column of the following table.

The purpose of this complex method of arriving at the amounts to be withheld from the wages of employees is to come as close as possible to collecting the actual tax on incomes up to the $5,000 level.

Withholding Exemptions. Each employee is entitled to the following withholding exemptions: (a) An exemption for himself. (b) If married, an exemption for his spouse, unless his spouse claims a withholding exemption in behalf of herself. (c) An exemption for each dependent. 

A dependent is one who meets the following requirements: (a) Receives over one-half of his support from the taxpayer. (b) Has less than $500 of gross income of his own during the year. (c) Is closely related to the taxpayer. "Closely related" means that the dependent is- Your son, daughter, or their descendants; stepson, stepdaughter, son-in-law, or daughter-in-law; 

Your father, mother, or ancester of either; stepfather, stepmother, father-in-law, or mother-in-law; 

Your brother, sister, stepbrother, stepsister, half brother, half sister, brother-in-law, or sister-in-law; 

Your uncle, aunt, nephew, or niece (but not if related only by marriage).

Ch. 1] 

INTRODUCTION 

The above relationships apply to a legally adopted child the same as though he or she were a child by blood. A citizen of a foreign country cannot be claimed as a dependent unless he or she is a resident of the United States, Canada, or Mexico. 

A single person, without dependents, may claim one withholding exemption. A married couple is entitled to two withholding exemptions; if both are employed these exemptions may be claimed by either spouse or each may claim one exemption. 

Withholding Exemption Certificate. Every employee is required to furnish his employer with a signed withholding exemption certificate setting forth the number of withholding exemptions which he claims, which shall in no event exceed the number to which he is entitled. The withholding certificates must be retained by the employer as a supporting record of the withholding exemptions allowed to his employees. 

Every employee is required to furnish his employer with a new withholding exemption certificate on or before December 1, 1944. On this certificate the employee should state the number of withholding exemptions which he is entitled to claim for 1945 determined as of January 1, 1945. These new certificates will become effective with respect to the first payment of wages made on or after January 1, 1945.

If an employee who began working before December 1, 1944, fails to furnish his employer with a new withholding certificate on December I but furnishes it later, his employer may put it into effect immediately or he may wait until the first payment of wages is made on or after July 1, 1945. In the latter case the employee will have no withholding exemption at all for the first half of 1945 except the one exemption allowed for the 2.7% rate. 

In the case of an employee who commences employment after December 1, 1944, a withholding exemption certificate should be furnished to his employer on or before the date he commences such employment. This certificate becomes effective with respect to the first payment of wages on or after the date the certificate is furnished.

Should there be a change in the status of an employee with respect to the number of withholding exemptions to which he is entitled, a new certificate should be filed with the employer immediately. In case there is a decrease in the number of withholding exemptions, the employee is required to furnish his employer with a new certificate within 10 days. Should there be an increase in the number of withholding exemptions to which the employee is entitled, he should file a new certificate although

[Ch. 1 

INTRODUCTION 

he is not required to do so as the claiming of additional exemptions is optional with the employee. When new certificates are filed as a result of a change in the status of an employee the employer may put them into effect immediately or he may wait until the next status determination date-July I or January 1. 

In other words, employers may elect to put into effect any changes in the status of their employees on January I and July 1 of each year only. However, it is advisable for each employee to notify his employer promptly of any change in his status as to the number of withholding exemptions to which he is entitled. 

The death of a dependent during the year does not affect the dependency credit; hence, no change in withholding exemptions is required if a dependent dies during the year. Should an employee's spouse die or a divorce be obtained during the year, a new withholding certificate should be filed within ten days. 

Form W-4 is the official form to be used for the withholding exemption certificate. A model filled-in copy of this form is reproduced on the following page.

Methods of Withholding. Employers are permitted to use either of the following methods in computing the amount of tax to be withheld from the wages of each employee: (a) Percentage method. (b) Wage-bracket method.

The use of the percentage method involves a separate computation for each wage payment, while the wage-bracket method involves the use of tables in ascertaining the amounts to be withheld from the wages of each employee on each payday. The tables are so constructed that in many cases the amount withheld will more nearly approximate the actual tax liability of the employee than if the percentage method were used. Inasmuch as withholding tables are provided only for weekly, biweekly, semimonthly, monthly, and daily or miscellaneous pay-roll periods, the percentage method of withholding must be used where other pay-roll periods are involved. Thus, where wages are paid on the basis of quarterly, semiannual, or annual payroll periods it is necessary to use the percentage method of computing amounts to be withheld. An employer may change from one method to another at will or he may use one method for one group of employees and the other for another group.

Ch. 1] 

INTRODUCTION 

Wage Bracket Withholding. In lieu of using the percentage method of withholding an employer may elect to use the so-called "wage bracket method" of withholding. The wage bracket method involves the use of tables in determining the amount to be withheld.

It will be noted that the wage bracket tables cover weekly, biweekly, semimonthly, monthly, and daily or miscellaneous pay-roll periods. A pay-roll period is the period for which a payment of wages is ordinarily made to the employee. Thus, if wages are paid weekly, the employer may elect to use the wage bracket table for the weekly pay-roll period. 

If, instead of using the percentage method in computing the amount to be withheld from the semimonthly wages of Robert Jordan, the employer had elected to use the wage bracket table for the semimonthly pay-roll period, he would have ascertained that $9.10 should be withheld each payday instead of $9.21.

If, instead of using the percentage method in computing the amount to be withheld from the monthly wages of Henry B. 

Nolan, the employer had elected to use the wage bracket table for the monthly pay-roll period, he would have ascertained that $51.60 should be withheld each payday instead of $52.49.

In using the wage bracket method to determine the amounts to be withheld from the wages earned by employees, the following factors need to be considered: (a) The length of the pay-roll period. (b) The amount of the wages earned during the pay-roll period. (c) The number of withholding exemptions claimed by the employee.

With this information it is necessary only to refer to the proper table to ascertain the amount to be withheld from the wages of each employee on each payday. 

Regardless of whether the percentage method or the wage bracket method is used in determining the amount to be withheld, the amount of tax withheld only approximates the amount of the tax liability on incomes up to $5,000 a year derived from wages only. Under certain conditions the total amount withheld during the taxable year may be more or less than the actual amount of the tax liability. If too much is withheld, the ememployee will be entitled to a refund; if too little is withheld the employee will be required to pay the difference.

Ch. 1] 

INTRODUCTION Supplementary Compensation. Any compensation re ceived in addition to the regular wages, such as commissions, bonuses, royalties, fees, etc., are subject to the withholding tax. 

Such supplementary compensation may be added to the regular compensation and the taxes to be withheld may be computed on the aggregate amount under either the percentage method or the wage bracket method. 

If the employer so elects, the regular wages and supplementary compensation need not be aggregated in computing the amount to be withheld. If the percentage method is used, it will make no difference whether the regular wages and supplementary compensation are aggregated or whether the amount to be withheld is computed on the separate payments. If the wage bracket method is used, however, the amount to be withheld may be computed by reference to the appropriate table with respect to the amount of the regular wages and by using either the percentage method or the wage bracket method with respect to the supplementary wages. If the wage bracket method is used with respect to the supplementary wages, the employee must be classified arbitrarily as a person claiming no withholding exemptions.

Statement To Be Furnished Employee. Employers are required to furnish each employee with a written statement showing the wages paid during the calendar year and the amount of the tax withheld during the year. This statement must be furnished to the employee on or before January 31 of the succeeding year; or, if the employment is terminated before the close of the year, on the day on which the last payment of wages is made.

Employers. Any person or corporation for whom one or more individuals perform any service as employees is an employer. A sole proprietor is an employer regardless of whether he has one or more employees. Partners are also employers regardless of the number of their employees and regardless of whether or not they draw compensation for services rendered.

The officers of a corporation including the president, vicepresident, secretary, and treasurer are employees. Corporations, which are themselves exempt from income tax, are employers and are subject to the withholding requirements. Such corporations include religious and charitable organizations, educational institutions, clubs, social organizations, and societies.

22 [Ch. 1 

INTRODUCTION 

Employees. Every individual is an employee if the relationship between him and the person for whom he performs services is the legal relation of employer and employee. In some cases it is rather difficult to determine whether the relationship of employer and employee exists. If such relationship exists, the designation or description of the relationship by the parties as anything other than that of employer and employee is immaterial. Thus, if such relationship exists, it is of no consequence that the employee is designated as a partner, agent, or independent contractor. Generally, physicians, lawyers, dentists, veterinarians, public stenographers, public accountants, auctioneers, and others who follow an independent trade, business, or profession, in which they offer their services to the public, are independent contractors and not employees. 

Amounts paid in advance or reimbursements made to employees specifically for traveling or other bona fide ordinary and necessary expenses incurred or reasonably expected to be incurred in the business of the employer are not subject to withholding. Any reasonable segregation of wages paid will be acceptable; as, for example, where an employer issues one check indicating thereon the amount thereof which represents wages and the amount which represents expenses, or issues a separate check for the expenses.

Payment of Tax Withheld by Employers. The amount withheld during each calendar quarter must be paid to the Collector of Internal Revenue at the time of filing the quarterly return, except that where the amount withheld during any month is more than $100, it must be paid to a U. S. depositary within ten days after the close of the month. However, if desired, the taxes withheld during the last month of the quarter may be remitted direct to the collector at the time of filing the quarterly return. Thus, if an employer withholds more than $100 from the wages paid to his employees during the first month of the calendar quarter, he is required to pay the amount withheld to a U. S. depositary not later than the tenth of the following month; if the amount withheld during the second month of the calendar quarter is more than $100, it must likewise be paid to the depositary by the tenth of the following month. The amount withheld during the third month of the calendar quarter, even though it exceeds $100 may be paid to the collector at the time of filing the quarterly return on or before the last day of the month following the close of the quarter. Any errors made by an employer either in the withholding or the payment of the tax

Ch. 1] 

INTRODUCTION 

for any quarter of the calendar year may be corrected in any subsequent quarter of the same year. 

Quarterly Payment of Estimated Taxes not Withheld.

Inasmuch as wages are the only type of income on which collection of tax at the source is provided, many individuals are required to make quarterly payments of that portion of their estimated taxes which is not withheld at the source. In order to determine the amount of such required payments certain individuals are required to file declarations of their estimated taxes on or before the fifteenth day of the third month of the taxable year, with certain exceptions.

Persons Required to File Declarations. Every individ ual, other than a nonresident alien, is required to file a declaration of his estimated tax for each taxable year if— (1) his gross income from wages is expected to exceed $5,000 plus $500 for each surtax exemption, except his own, or (2) his gross income from sources other than wages is expected to exceed $100 and his total gross income is expected to be $500 or more.

The reason a declaration need not be filed by individuals whose gross income from wages is not expected to exceed $5,000 is because, under the withholding provisions of the income tax law, employers are required to withhold from the wages of employees an amount approximating the actual tax on incomes up to the $5,000 level. However, when an individual estimates that his gross income from wages will exceed $5,000 the amount withheld by his employer will not be sufficient to cover the entire tax liability, hence he is required to file a declaration showing the unpaid balance of his estimated tax which serves as a basis for determining the amount of his quarterly payments. 

Marital status of individuals and dependents are no factor in determining their liability to file declarations of their estimated tax. However, husband and wife may unite in filing a joint declaration if desired. Whether husband and wife unite in filing a joint declaration or whether they file separate declarations, they are not obligated to follow the same procedure in filing their final returns. In other words, even though they may unite in filing a joint declaration they still have the right to file separate final returns, or vice versa. However, no joint declaration may be made if either husband or wife is a nonresident alien. If a joint declaration is made by husband and wife and they decide

[Ch. 1 

INTRODUCTION 

to file separate final returns, the estimated tax paid in quarterly installments may be applied as a credit in the final return of either spouse, or may be divided between them. 

Time for Filing Declarations. The declaration shall be filed on or before March 15 of the taxable year except in the following cases: (a) If the requirements are first met after March I and before June 2 of the taxable year, the declaration shall be filed on or before June 15. (b) If the requirements are first met after June I and before September 2 of the taxable year, the declaration shall be filed on or before September 15. (c) If the requirements are met after September 1 of the taxable year, the declaration shall be filed on or before January 15 of the succeeding taxable year.

Special provisions apply to taxpayers who report on a fiscal year basis.

Amended Declarations. A taxpayer may amend his declaration once in each calendar quarter after the quarter in which he first files, except that the last amendment may be made on or before January 15 of the succeeding year. This gives those individuals who receive unexpected income after December 15 of the calendar year a chance to avoid the penalty imposed for underestimating their tax by filing an amended declaration after the close of the calendar year. 

If desired, a taxpayer may file his final return on or before January 15 and pay the full tax shown as due. This will eliminate the need for filing an amended declaration on January 15. 

Thus, if by January 15 of the succeeding year a taxpayer finds that he has underestimated his tax in a previous declaration, he may file either an amended declaration and wait until March 15 to file his final return, or he may file his final return and pay the tax due immediately. In either case he avoids any penalty imposed for underestimating his tax.

Content of Declaration. Briefly the declaration consists of (a) the estimated tax for the year, (b) the estimated tax to be withheld at the source during the year, and (c) the unpaid balance of the estimated tax. Only the reasonably expected income for the current year need be considered in preparing the declaration.

Ch. 1] 

INTRODUCTION 

Payment of Estimated Tax. The unpaid balance of the tax set forth in the declaration may be paid in four equal installments due on March 15, June 15, September 15, and January 15 of the succeeding year. At the election of the taxpayer any installment of the estimated tax may be paid prior to the date prescribed for its payment. Thus, the entire amount of the estimated unpaid balance may be paid at the time of filing the declaration if desired. If a taxpayer is delinquent in filing his declaration, any installments which would have been due if a declaration had been filed on time must be paid when the declation is filed. Thus, if a declaration is required to be filed on or before March 15, but it is not filed until December 15, threefourths of the estimated tax must be paid at the time of filing the declaration and one-fourth must be paid on or before January 15 of the succeeding year.

To illustrate the application of the law governing quarterly payments, it will be assumed that Henry B. Nolan had sufficient income from sources other than wages that it was necessary for him to file a declaration on March 15. This declaration showed that he estimated the unpaid balance of his income tax to be $240 computed as follows: (a) Estimated tax for the year. (b) Estimated tax to be withheld during the year... (c) Unpaid balance of estimated tax.. $859.20 619.20 $240.00

At the time of filing his declaration he paid one quarter of the tax, or $60. This left a balance of $180 to be paid in three subsequent quarterly payments. Under the law he is required to pay one-third of the unpaid balance, or $60, on June 15; onehalf of the unpaid balance, or $60, on September 15; and the remaining $60 on January 15 of the secceeding year. 

Farmers. Anyone whose estimated gross income from farming for the taxable year is at least two-thirds of his total estimated gross income from all sources may wait until January 15 of the succeeding year to file a declaration of his estimated tax. Under prior law, farmers were required to file their declaration on or before December 15 of the taxable year but this proved to be a hardship on many farmers who were unable to estimate their income before the close of the taxable year. Payment of the entire estimated tax is due at the time of filing the declaration. However, if desired a farmer may file a declaration sooner and make current payments of his estimated tax. If a

[Ch. 1 

INTRODUCTION 

farmer files his final return for the taxable year on or before January 15 of the succeeding year, he is relieved from filing a declaration of his estimated tax. On this basis a farmer need file but one return for each year and that is his final return, which must be filed on or before January 15, at which time the entire amount of the tax must be paid.

Fiscal Year Taxpayers. The foregoing discussion applied specifically to calendar year taxpayers. In the case of fiscal year taxpayers, the original declaration must be made on or before the fifteenth day of the third month of the fiscal year. 

Installments of the tax are due on or before the fifteenth days of the third, sixth, and ninth months of the taxable year with the final installment being due on or before the fifteenth day of the first month of the succeeding fiscal year. 

Penalties. Penalties may be imposed for the following: 

(a) Failure to file a declaration within the time prescribed. (b) Failure to pay an installment of the estimated tax within the time prescribed. (c) Substantial understatement of estimated tax. 

The penalty provided by law for failure to file a declaration of estimated tax within the prescribed time is 5% of each installment due but unpaid, with an additional 1% of the unpaid amount for each month or fraction thereof during which such failure continues, not to exceed 10% in the aggregate. 

The penalty provided by law for failure to pay an installment of the estimated tax within the time prescribed, where a declaration has been filed within the time prescribed, is 5% of the unpaid amount of such installment, with an additional 1% of such unpaid amount for each month or fraction thereof during which such amount remains unpaid, not to exceed 10% in the aggregate.

A penalty is provided by law for substantial understatement of the estimated tax. The penalty is imposed when the estimated tax with certain adjustments is less than 80% (66%% in the case of farmers) of the actual tax shown to be due in the final return. This penalty may not exceed 6% of the excess of the tax shown on the final return over the amount of the estimated tax with certain adjustments.

Ch. 1]

CHAPTER TWO  1. Conditions Under Which Returns Must be Filed 

(a) Individuals (b) Fiduciaries (c) Nonresident aliens 

2. Bases Upon Which Returns May be Filed 

(a) Cash (b) Accrual (c) Calendar year (d) Fiscal year 

3. Returns (a) Time and place for filing (b) Verification of 

(c) Penalties 

(d) Publicity 

(e) Official forms 

For taxable years beginning after December 31, 1943, individuals who are required to file income tax returns are divided into two principal groups as follows: (1) Those individuals who qualify and elect to have the collector of internal revenue determine their tax. (2) Those individuals who determine their own tax. 

Individuals whose gross income is less than $5,000 and whose income not subject to withholding does not exceed $100, may choose to have their tax determined by the collector if their income consists entirely of compensation for personal services, dividends, or interest. In certain cases the Commissioner may by regulation extend this privilege to individuals whose gross income does not exceed $5,200 and whose income not subject to withholding does not exceed $200. This group of individuals is permitted to use a simple form for their returns setting forth (a) the total amount of wages received, (b) the total amount of taxes withheld by their employers, and (c) the total amount of their other income, if any. It has been estimated that this provision will relieve approximately 30,000,000 taxpayers of the necessity of computing their tax. 29

[Ch. 2 

All other individuals who are required to file returns belong to the second group. These individuals may be subdivided into three classes as follows: 

(a) Individuals whose adjusted gross income (generally, gross income less business deductions) is less than $5,000 and whose nonbusiness deductions do not exceed 10% of adjusted gross income, are permitted to use a short form for their return and may ascertain their tax by reference to a table. (b) Individuals whose adjusted gross income is $5,000 or over and whose nonbusiness deductions do not exceed $500, may elect to use a standard deduction of $500 in lieu of itemizing their nonbusiness deductions. However, their taxes may not be ascertained by reference to a table; instead, their taxes must be computed by applying the prescribed rates of normal tax and surtax. (c) Individuals whose adjusted gross income is less than $5,000 and whose nonbusiness deductions exceed 10% of their adjusted gross income, and individuals whose adjusted gross income is $5,000 or over and whose actual nonbusiness deductions are in excess of $500, in order to secure the full benefit of their nonbusiness deductions and credits, are required to list them in detail and their taxes must be computed by applying the prescribed rates of normal tax and surtax. Individuals classified as (2b) or (2c) above are required to use a long form for their return. 

Individual Returns. (a) RequiremenT.-Every individual having for the taxable year a gross income of $500 or more shall make a return, which shall contain or be verified by a written declaration that it is made under the penalties of perjury. Such return shall set forth in such cases, and to such extent, and in such detail, as the Commissioner with the approval of the Secretary may by regulations prescribe, the items of gross income and the deductions and credits allowed under this chapter and such other information for the purpose of carrying out the provisions of this chapter as may be prescribed by such regulations. (b) HUSBAND AND WIFE.-A husband and wife may make a single return jointly. Such a return may be made even though one of the spouses has neither gross income nor deductions. If a joint return is made the tax shall be computed on the aggregate income and the liability with respect to the tax shall be joint and several. No joint return may be made if either the husband or wife is a nonresident alien or if the husband and wife have different taxable years. The status of individuals as husband and wife shall be determined as of the last day of the taxable year. 

(c) PERSONS UNDER DISABILITY.-If the taxpayer is unable to make his own return, the return shall be made by a duly authorized agent or by the guardian or other person charged with the care of the person or property of such taxpayer. 

(d) SIGNATURE PRESUMED CORRECT. The fact that an individual's name is signed to a filed return shall be prima facie evidence for all purposes that the return was actually signed by him. 

(Section 51-I. R. C.)

Ch. 2] 

Every individual who has a gross income of $500 or more for the taxable year must file a return regardless of the following: (a) Age. (b) Whether single or married. (c) Amount of net income. (d) Whether any tax is payable.

The Commissioner of Internal Revenue with the approval of the Secretary of the Treasury may prescribe regulations governing the kind of returns to be filed by different categories of taxpayers, the nature of the information to be shown therein as to items of gross income and the deductions and credits, and other information bearing upon the tax liability of the taxpayer. 

Minors. A minor is one who has not attained the statutory age of majority. The Internal Revenue Code does not stipulate the age of majority; instead, this is determined by state laws and the state laws are not uniform. Under the laws of some states the age of majority is stipulated as 21 while in other states it is 18.

The return of a minor must be made by him or for him by his duly authorized agent, guardian, or other person charged with the care of his person or property. Thus, the return of a minor may be filed by himself or by someone else as his legal representative. 

Married Persons. A husband and wife may make a single return jointly even though one of them has no gross income and even though they may be voluntarily separated. When a joint return is filed, the tax is computed on the aggregate income and the liability of the husband and wife for the tax is joint and several. Thus, they are individually and collectively liable for the tax.

Where husband and wife have separate incomes each may file a separate return or they may unite in filing a single return jointly. An election made in one year is not binding as to subsequent years, however. Thus, separate returns may be filed in one year and a joint return the next year or vice versa. The filing of a joint return by husband and wife constitutes an election as to the basis to be used in computing the tax and they cannot subsequently file separate returns even though they propose to do so prior to the expiration of the period specified for the filing of returns.

[Ch. 2. 

The status of husband and wife is determined as of the last day of the taxable year. Assuming that the taxable year is the calendar year ending on December 31, individuals who are married on or before December 31 are thus entitled to file a single return jointly. On the other hand, if they are divorced on or before December 31 they have the status of single persons and must file separate returns.

Married persons may not file a single return jointly if either is a nonresident alien or if they have different taxable years. A nonresident alien is an individual whose residence is not within the United States and who is not a citizen of the United States. 

If the taxable year of the husband is the calendar year ending on December 31 while the taxable year of the wife is a fiscal year ending on the last day of any month other than December, they may not avail themselves of the right to file a single return jointly.

In states having community property laws, the husband and wife may treat one-half of their community income as their respective incomes. States having such laws include Arizona, California, Idaho, Louisiana, Nevada, New Mexico, Texas, and

Washington. Where the husband and wife elect to make a single return jointly, community property laws will have no effect. 

However, if the husband and wife wish to take advantage of the community property laws in the state in which they reside they may do so even in cases where the community income consists entirely of the earnings of the husband.

Disabled Persons. The return of a taxpayer who is unable to make his own return because of illness may be made for him by his duly authorized agent, guardian, or other person charged with the care of his person or property. The return may also be made by an agent if the taxpayer is unable to make the return by reason of continuous absence from the United States for a period of at least 60 days prior to the date prescribed by law for making the return.

The return of an individual must be signed by him or by his duly authorized agent. The signature to a return is presumed to be correct in the absence of proof to the contrary. If the return is signed by an agent, he assumes the responsibility for making the return and incurs liability to the specific penalties provided for erroneous, false, or fraudulent returns.

Ch. 2] 3333 

 WAGE EARNERS RETURNS.— (f) TAX COMPUTED BY COLLECTOR IN Case of Wage Earners.— (1) RETURN REQUIREMENTS.-An individual entitled to elect to pay the tax imposed by Supplement T whose gross income is less than $5,000 and is entirely from one or more of the following sources: Remuneration for services performed by him as an employee, dividends, or interest; and whose gross income from sources other than wages, as defined in section 1621 (a), does not exceed $100, shall at his election be relieved, by using the form prescribed as the form for the return for the purposes of this subsection, from showing on the return the tax imposed by this chapter. In such case the tax shall be computed by the collector. (2) RESULT OF COMPUTATION. After the collector has computed the tax, he shall mail to the taxpayer a notice stating the amount determined by the collector as payable and making demand therefor. 

(3) REGULATIONS.-The Commissioner with the approval of the Secre tary shall prescribe regulations for carrying out this subsection, and such regulations may provide for the application of the rules of this subsection to cases where the gross income includes items other than those enumerated in paragraph (1), to cases where the gross income from sources other than wages on which the tax has been withheld at the source is more than $100 but not more than $200, and to cases where the gross income is $5,000 or more but not more than $5,200. Such regulations shall provide (A) for the application of this subsection in the case of husband and wife, including provisions determining when a joint return under this subsection may be permitted or required and what constitutes a joint return, whether the liability shall be joint and several, and whether one spouse may make return under this subsection and the other without regard to this subsection, and (B) whether and the extent to which the benefits of this subsection may be availed of, in the case of taxable years beginning in the calendar year 1944, by persons required to make or making payments of estimated tax with respect to any such taxable year. (4) METHOD OF ELECTION.-The election to have the benefits of this subsection shall be made by making return on the form prescribed as the form for the return for the purposes of this subsection. An election so made shall constitute an election to pay the tax imposed by Supplement T. 

(Section 51-I. R. C.) 

This is a new provision included in the Individual Income Tax Act of 1944. The administrative details are left entirely to the Commissioner to prescribe by regulation. The objective is to enable wage earners at their election to file a very simple statement showing the essential facts for tax determination by the collector. The collector is authorized to use the optional table in the determination of the tax, hence only those taxpayers who are entitled to use the optional table may take advantage of this provision.

The collector will determine the tax from the statement filed by the taxpayer and by reference to the optional table. If any balance remains unpaid after taking into consideration amounts withheld at the source the collector will mail to the taxpayer a notice and demand for the balance due which is payable within thirty days after the mailing of the notice and demand. If the tax liability is less than the amount withheld at the source, the taxpayer will be entitled to a refund of the difference.

[Ch. 2 

 Fiduciary Returns. (a) REQUIREMENT OF RETURN.-Every fiduciary 

(except a receiver appointed by authority of law in possession of part only of the property of an individual) shall make under oath a return for any of the following individuals, estates, or trusts for which he acts, stating specifically the items of gross income thereof and the deductions and credits allowed under this chapter and such other information for the purpose of carrying out the provisions of this chapter as the Commissioner with the approval of the Secretary may by regulations prescribe— (1) Every individual having a gross income for the taxable year of $500 or over; (2) Every estate the gross income of which for the taxable year is $500 or over; (3) Every trust the net income of which for the taxable year is $100 or over, or the gross income of which for the taxable year is $500 or over, regardless of the amount of the net income; (4) Every estate or trust of which any beneficiary is a nonresident alien. 

(b) JOINT FIDUCIARIES.-Under such regulations as the Commissioner with the approval of the Secretary may prescribe a return made by one of two or more joint fiduciaries and filed in the office of the collector of the district where such fiduciary resides shall be sufficient compliance with the above requirement. Such fiduciary shall make oath (1) that he has sufficent knowledge of the affairs of the individual, estate, or trust for which the return is made, to enable him to make the return, and (2) that the return is, to the best of his knowledge and belief, true and correct. 

(c) LAW APPLICABLE TO FIDUCIARIES.-Any fiduciary required to make a return under this chapter shall be subject to all the provisions of law which apply to individuals. 

(Section 142-I. R. C.) 

(6) The term "fiduciary" means a guardian, trustee, executor, administrator, receiver, conservator, or any person acting in any fiduciary capacity for any person. 

(Section 3797-I. R. C.) 

A fiduciary is a person who is empowered to act as the agent of another person. He may be a guardian, trustee, executor, administrator, or a receiver.

Anyone who has the right to act as the duly authorized agent for an incapable individual, for an estate, or for a trust must file an income tax return for such individual, estate, or trust for each taxable year in which the gross income is $500 or over. An incapable individual for whom a fiduciary acts may be a minor, or an individual who is incapable of filing his own return because he is physically or mentally ill or because he is absent from the United States at the time prescribed for making the return.

If the net income of a trust for the taxable year amounts to $100 or over, a return must be filed by the fiduciary regardless of the amount of the gross income. In other words, if the gross income of a trust for the taxable year is $500 or over or the net income is $100 or over, a return must be filed by the fiduciary. 

If any beneficiary of an estate or trust is a nonresident alien, a return must be filed by the fiduciary regardless of the

Ch. 2] 

amount of the gross income or regardless of the amount of the net income. 

In determining the responsibility of fiduciaries to file returns for individuals, estates, or trusts it should be noted that the amount of the tax liability is not a factor; hence, returns must be filed under the conditions set forth above regardless of whether or not any tax is payable.

In the case of a person dying within the year whose gross income prior to his death amounted to $500 or over, a return must be filed for him by the executor or administrator. This return is in addition to the return required of the fiduciary for the estate covering the portion of the year subsequent to the death. In other words, the fiduciary should file two returnsone for the decedent covering that portion of the year elapsed before death and the other for the estate covering that portion of the year subsequent to death.

Bases Upon Which Returns May be Filed. The law provides that returns may be filed upon the basis of either a calendar or fiscal year, and upon either the cash or accrual basis. 

The net income must be computed upon the basis of a fixed period which usually is twelve months and is known as a taxable year. The year in which any item of income or deduction is to be accounted for must be determined in the light of the fundamental rule that the computation shall be made in such a manner as clearly reflects the taxpayer's income. (a) TAXABLE YEAR.-"Taxable year" means the calendar year, or the fiscal year ending during such calendar year, upon the basis of which the net income is computed under this Part. "Taxable year" means, in the case of a return made for a fractional part of a year under the provisions of this chapter or under regulations prescribed by the Commissioner with the approval of the Secretary, the period for which such return is made. (b) FISCAL YEAR.-"Fiscal year" means an accounting period of twelve months ending on the last day of any month other than December. 

(Section 48-I. R. C.) 

In business, a fiscal year is an accounting period of twelve months ending on any date, but for income tax purposes, a fiscal year is an accounting period of twelve months ending on the last day of any month other than December. If the period ends on the last day of December, it constitutes a calendar year. 

A taxable year may be either a calendar year or a fiscal year depending upon the basis on which the net income of the taxpayer is computed. If a taxpayer computes his net income on a basis of an accounting period of twelve months ending on 

June 30 or the last day of any other month excepting December, his taxable year is a fiscal year. If, however, he computes his

[Ch. 2 

net income on a basis of an accounting period of twelve months ending on the last day of December, his taxable year is a calendar year.

CHANGE OF ACCOUNTING PERIOD.-If a taxpayer changes his accounting period from fiscal year to calendar year, from calendar year to fiscal year, or from one fiscal year to another, the net income shall, with the approval of the Commissioner, be computed on the basis of such new accounting period, subject to the provisions of section 47. 

(Section 46-I. R. C.) (a) RETURNS FOR SHORT PERIOD RESULTING FROM CHANGE OF AC- COUNTING PERIOD.-If a taxpayer, with the approval of the Commissioner, changes the basis of computing net income from fiscal year to calendar year a separate return shall be made for the period between the close of the last fiscal year for which return was made and the following December 31. If the change is from calendar year to fiscal year, a separate return shall be made for the period between the close of the last calendar year for which return was made and the date designated as the close of the fiscal year. If the change is from one fiscal year to another fiscal year a separate return shall be made for the period between the close of the former fiscal year and the date designated as the close of the new fiscal year. 

(Section 47-I. R. C.) 

Under no circumstances can a return be made for an accounting period of more than twelve months. Whenever there is a change in the basis of computing net income from one taxable year to another taxable year, it is necessary to file a separate return for a fractional part of a year. Thus, if a taxpayer changes his taxable year from a calendar year ending on December 31 to a fiscal year ending on June 30, it will be necessary for him to file a separate return for the accounting period between December 

31 and June 30, or for a period of six months.

Likewise, if a taxpayer changes his taxable year from a fiscal year ending on June 30 to a calendar year ending on December 31, it will be necessary for him to file a separate return for the accounting period between June 30 and December 31, or for a period of six months.

In the event a taxpayer changes his taxable year from an accounting period of twelve months ending on June 30 to a taxable year ending on August 31, he is required to file a separate return for the accounting period between June 30 and August 31, or for a period of two months.

Computing Income on Annual Basis. (b) INCOME COMPUTED ON 

Basis of SHORT PERIOD.-Where a separate return is made under subsection (a) on account of a change in the accounting period, and in all other cases where a separate return is required or permitted, by regulations prescribed by the Commissioner with the approval of the Secretary, to be made for a fractional part of a year, then the income shall be computed on the basis of the period for which separate return is made. 

(c) INCOME PLACED ON Annual Basis.- (1) GENERAL RULE.-If a separate return is made under subsection (a) 

on account of a change in the accounting period, the net income, computed on the basis of the period for which separate return is made (referred to in this

Ch. 2] 

subsection as "the short period"), shall be placed on an annual basis by multiplying the amount thereof by twelve, and dividing by the number of months in the short period. The tax shall be such part of the tax computed on such annual basis as the number of months in the short period is of twelve months. 

(Section 47-I. R. C.) 

In the event an individual changes the basis of computing his net income from a fiscal year to a calendar year, or from a calendar year to a fiscal year, or from one fiscal year to another fiscal year, and it thereby becomes necessary for him to file a return for a period of less than twelve months, the law provides that his income must be computed on an annual basis. This is done by multiplying the amount of the net income for the period by twelve and dividing by the number of months in the period in which the income was received and for which the return is made. 

Thus, if a taxpayer is filing a return for a period of four months during which period his net income amounts to $3,000.00, it will be necessary for him to compute the income on an annual basis by multiplying by twelve and dividing by four. In this case, the annual income will be found to be $9,000.00. The tax should then be computed on this basis, but the tax to be paid will be such part of the tax computed on the annual basis as the number of months in the period is of twelve months. Thus, after computing the tax on the annual income of $9,000.00, the result should be divided by twelve and multiplied by four, which is the number of months in the period for which the return is made, in order to arrive at the amount of tax to be paid. 

Cash or Accrual Basis. An individual may report his income on either the cash basis or the accrual basis; that is, if he earns income in one year which he does not receive until the following year he may include it in the return for the year in which it is received or in the return for the year in which it is earned. For example, assume that an individual, reporting on a calendar year basis, earned a salary in December, 1944, which was not paid to him until sometime in January, 1945. If he reports on the cash basis this income should be included in his return for 1945; if he reports on the accrual basis it should be included in his return for 1944. 

If the individual in the above example elects to report the December salary on the cash basis he must use the cash basis in reporting all other income; he cannot report part of his income on one basis and the remainder on the other basis. He must also report his deductible expenses on the same basis as his income. Furthermore, it is not permissible to report on one

[Ch. 2 

basis for one year and on the other basis for the following year. 

A consistent policy must be followed from year to year. 

GENERAL RULE.-The net income shall be computed upon the basis of the taxpayer's annual accounting period (fiscal year or calendar year, as the case may be) in accordance with the method of accounting regularly employed in keeping the books of such taxpayer; but if no such method of accounting has been so employed, or if the method employed does not clearly reflect the income, the computation shall be made in accordance with such method as in the opinion of the Commissioner does clearly reflect the income. If the taxpayer's annual accounting period is other than a fiscal year as defined in section 48 or if the taxpayer has no annual accounting period or does not keep books, the net income shall be computed on the basis of the calendar year. (For use of inventories, see section 22 (c).) 

(Section 41-I. R. C.) 

A taxpayer must prepare his return on a basis of cash receipts and payments except when he keeps a set of books which clearly reflect the income on the accrual basis, or the Commissioner rules that the return must be prepared on the accrual basis in order to clearly reflect the net income. As a general rule, if proper books are kept the return must be made in accordance with the books of account. If the books of account do not show the actual income as required by law, proper adjustment must be made in the return. If a taxpayer desires to change his books from one basis to the other, he must first secure the consent of the Commissioner before computing his taxable income upon the new basis. That is, if a taxpayer has been keeping his books on the cash basis and changes his method of accounting to the accrual basis, he cannot prepare his return on the accrual basis without first obtaining the consent of the Commissioner by filing application at least thirty days before the close of the taxable period. 

Period in Which Items of Gross Income Included. (a) GENERAL 

RULE. The amount of all items of gross income shall be included in the gross income for the taxable year in which received by the taxpayer, unless, under methods of accounting permitted under section 41, any such amounts are to be properly accounted for as of a different period. In the case of the death of a taxpayer whose net income is computed upon the basis of the accrual method of accounting, amounts (except amounts includible in computing a partner's net income under section 182) accrued only by reason of the death of the taxpayer shall not be included in computing net income for the period in which falls the date of the taxpayer's death. 

(b) NONINTEREST-BEARING OBLIGATIONS ISSUED AT DISCOUNT.-If, in the case of a taxpayer owning any noninterest-bearing obligation issued at a discount and redeemable for fixed amounts increasing at stated intervals, the increase in the redemption price of such obligation occurring in the taxable year does not (under the method of accounting used in computing his net income) constitute income to him in such year, such taxpayer may, at his election made in his return for any taxable year beginning after December 31, 1940, treat such increase as income received in such taxable year. If any such election is made with respect to any such obligation, it shall apply also to all such obligations owned by the taxpayer at the beginning of the first taxable year to which it applies and to all such obligations thereafter acquired by him and shall be binding for all subsequent taxable years, unless upon application by the taxpayer the Commissioner permits him, sub-

Ch. 2] 

ject to such conditions as the Commissioner deems necessary, to change to a different method. In the case of any such obligations owned by the taxpayer at the beginning of the first taxable year to which his election applies, the increase in the redemption price of such obligations occurring between the date of acquisition and the first day of such taxable year shall also be treated as income received in such taxable year. 

(c) SHORT-TERM OBLIGATIONS ISSUED ON DISCOUNT BASIS.-In the case of any obligation of the United States or any of its possessions, or of a State or Territory, or any political subdivision thereof, or of the District of Columbia, issued on or after March 1, 1941, on a discount basis and payable without interest at a fixed maturity date not exceeding one year from the date of issue, the amount of discount at which such obligation is originally sold shall not be considered to accrue until the date on which such obligation is paid at maturity, sold, or otherwise disposed of. 

(Section 42-I. R. C.) 

Approved standard methods of accounting will ordinarily be regarded as clearly reflecting income. A method of accounting will not, however, be regarded as clearly reflecting income unless all items of gross income and all deductions are treated with reasonable consistency. When the cash basis is used all items of gross income shall be included in the gross income for the taxable year in which they are received by the taxpayer, and deductions taken accordingly, unless in order clearly to reflect income such amounts are to be properly accounted for as of a different period. For instance, in any case in which it is necessary to use an inventory, no accounting in regard to purchases and sales will correctly reflect income except the accrual method. 

A taxpayer is deemed to have received items of gross income which have been credited to or set apart for him without restriction. Interest coupons must be reported as income for the year in which they mature, even though they are not cashed until sometime during the following year. Likewise, interest credited to a savings account is constructively received on the day credited. However, dividends on corporate stock declared to be payable on December 31 which are mailed, in accordance with usual custom, so that stockholders do not receive them until 

January, are not constructively received in December.

Appreciation in value of property is not an accrual of income to a taxpayer prior to the realization of such appreciation through sale or conversion of the property. 

Period for Which Deductions and Credits Taken. The deductions and credits (other than the corporation dividends paid credit provided in section 27) provided for in this chapter shall be taken for the taxable year in which "paid or accrued" or "paid or incurred," dependent upon the method of accounting upon the basis of which the net income is computed, unless in order to clearly reflect the income the deductions or credits should be taken as of a different period. In the case of the death of a taxpayer whose net income is computed upon the basis of the accrual method of accounting, amounts (except amounts includible in computing a partner's net income under section 182) accrued as deductions and credits only by reason of the death of the taxpayer shall not be allowed in computing net income for the period in which falls the date of the taxpayer's death. 

(Section 43—I. R. C.)

[Ch. 2 

The Treasury Department recognizes that no uniform method of accounting can be prescribed for all taxpayers, and the law contemplates that each taxpayer shall adopt such forms and systems of accounting as in his judgment are best suited to his business. Each taxpayer is required by law to make a return of his true income. He must, therefore, maintain such accounting records as will enable him to do so. Among the essentials are the following: (1) In all cases in which the production, purchase, or sale of merchandise of any kind is an income-producing factor, inventories of the merchandise on hand (including finished goods, work in process, raw materials, and supplies) should be taken at the beginning and end of the year and used in computing the net income of the year. (2) Expenditures made during the year should be properly classified as between capital and revenue, that is to say, that expenditures for items of plant, equipment, etc., which have a useful life extending substantially beyond the year should be charged to a capital account and not to an expense account. (3) In any case in which the cost of capital assets is being recovered through deductions for wear and tear, depletion, or obsolescence, any expenditure (other than ordinary repairs) made to restore the property or prolong its useful life should be added to the property account, or charged against the appropriate reserve and not to current expenses. 

Allocation of Income and Deductions. In any case of two or more organizations, trades, or businesses (whether or not incorporated, whether or not organized in the United States, and whether or not affiliated) owned or controlled directly or indirectly by the same interests, the Commissioner is authorized to distribute, apportion, or allocate gross income, deductions, credits, or allowances between or among such organizations, trades, or businesses, if he determines that such distribution, apportionment, or allocation is necessary in order to prevent evasion of taxes or clearly to reflect the income of any of such organizations, trades, or businesses. 

(Section 45-I. R. C.) 

This provision of the law gives the Commissioner the right to allocate taxable income, allowable deductions, and credits between or among such organizations, trades, or businesses as may be conducted by the taxpayer. However, consolidation of the accounts of two or more businesses is not actually required. The law recognizes the fact that a taxpayer may keep but one set of books for two or more businesses or trades in which he may be engaged. In such case it may or may not be necessary to allocate the income and deductions between or among the different businesses or trades, in order clearly to reflect the income of any of

Ch. 2] 

such businesses or trades. The controlling factor is the necessity of arranging the accounts so that they will clearly reflect the true income of each business or trade conducted by the taxpayer. 

Time and Place for Filing Returns. (a) TIME FOR FILING.-(1) 

GENERAL RULE.-Returns made on the basis of the calendar year shall be made on or before the 15th day of March following the close of the calendar year. Returns made on the basis of a fiscal year shall be made on or before the 15th day of the third month following the close of the fiscal year. 

(2) EXTENSION OF TIME.-The Commissioner may grant a reasonable extension of time for filing returns, under such rules and regulations as he shall prescribe with the approval of the Secretary. Except in the case of taxpayers who are abroad, no such extension shall be for more than six months. 

(b) To WHOM Return Made.-(1) INDIVIDUALS.-Returns (other than corporation returns) shall be made to the collector for the district in which is located the legal residence or principal place of business of the person making the return, or, if he has no legal residence or principal place of business in the United States, then to the collector at Baltimore, Maryland. 

(Section 53-I. R. C.) 

When returns are filed on a basis of the calendar year, they must be filed with the collector for the district in which the taxpayer resides or in which he maintains his principal place of business on or before March 15 of the year following the period for which the return is being made. If a return is being filed on the basis of a fiscal year, the taxpayer may file his return any time on or before the fifteenth day of the third month following the close of the period for which his return is being made. This applies to all taxpayers who are required to file returns excepting nonresident aliens who are allowed a longer period of time in which to file the return. 

When the last due date for filing returns falls on Sunday or a legal holiday, the last day for filing returns will be the day following such Sunday or legal holiday. When returns are mailed to the collector of the district in which the taxpayer resides, they must be placed in the mail in ample time to reach the collector's office under ordinary handling of the mails on or before the date on which the return is required to be filed. The date appearing in the postmark on the envelope is used as a basis of determining whether or not the return was mailed in ample time to reach the collector's office on or before the due date.

Returns of Nonresident Aliens. (a) REQUIREMENT.-In the case of a nonresident alien individual with respect to whose wages, as defined in section 1621 (a), withholding under Subchapter D of Chapter 9 is not made applicable, the return, in lieu of the time prescribed in section 53 (a) (1), shall be made on or before the fifteenth day of the sixth month following the close of the fiscal year, or, if the return is made on the basis of the calendar year, then on or before the fifteenth day of June.

[Ch. 2 

 (b) EXEMPTION FROM REQUIREMENT.-Subject to such con ditions, limitations, and exceptions and under such regulations as may be prescribed by the Commissioner, with the approval of the Secretary, nonresident alien individuals subject to the tax imposed by section 211 (a) may be exempted from the requirement of filing returns of such tax. 

(Section 217-I. R. C.) 

In general, nonresident aliens are classified as follows: (a) Those whose wages are not subject to withholding at the source and who at any time within the taxable year are engaged in a trade or business within the United States. Such aliens must make or have made returns on Form 1040B. (b) Those whose wages are not subject to withholding at the source who are not engaged in a trade or business within the United States and whose gross income from fixed and determinable annual or periodical income from sources within the United States is more than $15,400. Such aliens must make or have made returns on Form 1040NB-a. (c) Those whose wages are not subject to withholding at the source who are not engaged in a trade or business within the United States and whose gross income from fixed and determinable annual or periodical income from sources within the United States is not more than $15,400 and their tax is not fully satisfied at the source. Such aliens must make or have made returns on Form 1040NB. (d) Those who are not engaged in a trade or business in the United States and whose income is not in excess of $15,400 and the tax is fully satisfied at the source. Such aliens are not required to file returns unless the amount of the tax withheld at the source is in excess of the amount of the tax actually due and a refund of the excess is desired. The form to be used by or in behalf of such aliens is 1040NB. 

The returns of nonresident aliens must be made on or before the fifteenth day of the sixth month following the close of the taxable year, regardless of whether the return is filed on a basis of the calendar year or a fiscal year. The return must be filed with the collector of internal revenue for the district in which the nonresident alien has his principal place of business in the United States, or if he has no principal place of business in the United States, then with the collector of internal revenue at 

Baltimore, Maryland.

Extension of Time for Filing Returns. Under the law, the Commissioner is authorized to allow an extension of time for filing returns for not more than six months except in the case of a taxpayer who is abroad. An extension of time will be granted

Ch. 2] 

only when good cause exists. While collectors do not have authority to extend the time for filing returns, authority has been delegated to them by the Commissioner to act in his behalf when the request for extension of time involves absence, sickness or inability to secure data within the time prescribed in the law. 

Applications for extension of time, therefore, should be submitted to the local collectors. The Commissioner has instructed collectors to grant extensions for thirty or sixty days, and in some cases, ninety days. If more than ninety days are asked for, the collector may grant a ninety-day extension and advise the taxpayer to make a new application if he is unable to file a return within the ninety-day period. Ordinarily the collectors will not grant a second extension of time for filing a return unless the taxpayer has made every reasonable effort to avoid a delay. 

A request for extension of time should be made before the date for filing returns and specific reasons should be given for requesting the extension as the collector is authorized by the Commissioner to extend the time only when the reasons given appear to be satisfactory. The extension of time for filing a return does not automatically extend the time for payment of the tax. However, the time for payment of tax may also be extended by the Commissioner.

Taxpayers usually are required to file a tentative return showing an estimate of the tax and the payment of one-fourth of the estimated amount before they will be granted an extension of time for filing the complete return. A tentative return should be made on the official form and it should be designated as a tentative return. It is only necessary to state the estimated amount of tax believed to be due. No other information is required. Tentative returns will not be accepted unless permission was obtained previous to filing. A copy of the statement granting permission to file a tentative return should be attached to it. In case the complete return when filed shows that the tax amounted to more than the estimate indicated on the tentative return, the excess bears interest at 6% per annum from the original due date to the date of expiration of the extended period even though the entire tax may be paid prior to that date. When a complete return cannot be made within the extended period, a return as complete as possible should be submitted with a request for an additional extension of time which will be granted if good and satisfactory reason be given. 

Verification of Returns. The returns of individuals need not be made under oath, but need only contain a written decla-

[Ch. 2 

ration that they are made under the penalties of perjury. The same penalties as for perjury are prescribed for individuals who willfully make and subscribe a return which they do not believe to be true and correct as to every material matter. 

Penalties for Failure to File Returns. (a) In case of any failure to make and file return required by this chapter, within the time prescribed by law or prescribed by the Commissioner in pursuance of law, unless it is shown that such failure is due to reasonable cause and not due to willful neglect, there shall be added to the tax: 5 per centum if the failure is for not more than thirty days with an additional 5 per centum for each additional thirty days or fraction thereof during which such failure continues, not exceeding 25 per centum in the aggregate. The amount so added to any tax shall be collected at the same time and in the same manner and as a part of the tax unless the tax has been paid before the discovery of the neglect, in which case the amount so added shall be collected in the same manner as the tax. 

The amount added to the tax under this section shall be in lieu of the 25 per centum addition to the tax provided in section 3612 (d) (1). 

(Section 291-I. R. C.) 

Two classes of delinquents are liable to penalty as follows: (a) Those who do not file returns and for whom returns are made by the collector or Commissioner; (b) Those who file tardy returns and are unable to show reasonable cause for the delay. A taxpayer who files a tardy return and who wishes to avoid the penalty must make a statement of all the facts alleged as a reasonable cause for failure to file the return on time in the form of an affidavit, which should be attached to the return. 

If such an affidavit is filed voluntarily with the return or is filed upon the collector's demand, the collector, unless otherwise directed by the Commissioner, will forward the affidavit with the return, and if the Commissioner determines that the delinquency was due to reasonable cause and not to willful neglect no penalty will be imposed.

If the taxpayer is unable to satisfy the Commissioner that the delay in filing his return was due to reasonable cause, the penalty prescribed by law will be imposed by adding it to the tax to be collected at the same time and in the same manner as the tax. The penalty imposed will be 5% of the tax if the failure is for not more than thirty days, with an additional 5% for each additional thirty days or fraction thereof during which such failure continues, not to exceed 25% in the aggregate. 

Additional Penalties. (a) FAILURE TO FILE RETURNS, SUBMIT IN- FORMATION, OR PAY ESTIMATED TAX OR TAX.-Any person required under this chapter to pay any estimated tax or tax, or required by law or regulations made under authority thereof to make a return or declaration, keep any records, or supply any information, for the purposes of the computation, assessment, or collection of any estimated tax or tax imposed by this chapter, who willfully fails to pay such estimated tax or tax, make such return or declaration, keep such records, or supply such information, at the time or times required by law or regulations, shall, in addition to other penalties provided by law, be guilty

Ch. 2] 

of a misdemeanor and, upon conviction thereof, be fined not more than $10,000, or imprisoned for not more than one year, or both, together with the costs of prosecution. 

(b) FAILURE to Collect anD PAY OVER Tax, or Attempt to Defeat 

OR EVADE TAX.-Any person required under this chapter to collect, account for, and pay over any tax imposed by this chapter, who willfully fails to collect or truthfully account for and pay over such tax, and any person who willfully attempts in any manner to evade or defeat any tax imposed by this chapter or the payment thereof, shall, in addition to other penalties provided by law, be guilty of a felony and, upon conviction thereof, be fined not more than $10,000, or imprisoned for not more than five years, or both, together with the costs of prosecution.
