(c) Any individual who willfully makes and subscribes a return which he does not believe to be true and correct as to every material matter, shall be guilty of a felony, and, upon conviction thereof, shall be subject to the penalties prescribed for perjury in section 125 of the Criminal Code. (d) PERSON DEFINED.-The term "person" as used in this section includes an officer or employee of a corporation or a member or employee of a partnership, who as such officer, employee, or member is under a duty to perform the act in respect of which the violation occurs. (e) CROSS REFERENCE.-For penalties for failure to file information returns with respect to foreign personal holding companies and foreign corporations, see section 340. 

(Section 145-I. R. C.) 

Penalties which consist of a percentage of the tax are known as "ad valorem" penalties. Fines and imprisonments are known as "specific" penalties. The ad valorem penalties may be assessed by the Commissioner and are collected in the same manner as the tax. The specific penalties are imposed by the courts because they are criminal in their nature. 

The specific criminal penalties are incurred when a taxpayer willfully fails to file a return when required by the law or regulations to do so, or when he willfully attempts in any manner to evade or defeat all or any part of the tax. Willful failure to file a required return is a misdemeanor punishable by a fine of not more than $10,000.00 or imprisonment for not more than one year or both, together with the costs of prosecution. A willful attempt to evade all or any part of the tax due is a felony punishable by a fine of not more than $10,000.00 or imprisonment for not more than five years or both, together with the costs of prosecution. 

These criminal penalties are in addition to the civil penalties and are enforceable only by prosecution.

Information at Source. As an aid to the enforcement of the filing provisions, persons making payments to another person are required, under certain conditions, to report the details to the Commissioner.

All persons making payment to another person of fixed or determinable income of $500 or more in any calendar year must render a return thereof for such year on or before February 15

[Ch. 2 

of the following year, except payments of the following character: (a) Payments by a broker to his customers. (b) Payments of any type made to corporations. (c) Bills paid for merchandise, telegrams, telephone, freight, storage, and similar charges. (d) Payments of rent made to real estate agents. (e) Payments made to alien employees serving in foreign countries or payments representing earned income for services rendered without the United States made to nonresident citizens entitled to the benefits of section 116(a). (f) Salaries and profits paid or distributed by a partnership to the individual partners. (g) Payments of commissions made by fire insurance companies, or other companies insuring property, to general agents, except when specifically directed by the Commissioner to be filed. (h) Payments of income upon which income tax has been withheld at the source and reported on Forms 1012, 1013, 1042, or Forms W-1, W-2, and W-3. 

(i) Amounts paid by the United States to persons in its service (civil, military, or naval), as an allowance for traveling expenses, including an allowance for meals and lodging, as, for example, a per diem allowance in lieu of subsistence, and amounts paid as reimbursements for traveling expenses. (j) Payments of interest on obligations of the United States or any agency or instrumentality thereof. (k) Payments of interest on corporate bonds, except in the case of interest on bonds, mortgages, deeds of trust, or other similar obligations issued prior to January 1, 1934, and containing a tax-free covenant.

Form 1099, accompanied by transmittal Form 1096 showing the number of returns filed should be used in reporting the information. Returns for the calendar year are required to be filed with the Commissioner on or before February 15 of the following year.

Employers who are required to withhold the tax from wages paid to their employees are required to furnish a receipt therefor. 

If duplicates of these receipts are furnished with the employer's last quarterly return of the year, due on or before January 31, they will take the place of the information returns otherwise required, hence, it will not be necessary to file information returns on Form 1099 as to the same wages.

In addition to the use of the information returns as a check

Ch. 2] 

on whether individuals required to do so have filed income tax returns the amounts reported as paid are compared with the income reported as received by the payee. 

Publicity of Returns. Although income tax returns constitute public records, they are open to public examination and inspection only to such extent as provided by law or upon the order of the President under regulations prescribed by the Secretary of the Treasury and approved by the President.

The Internal Revenue Code specifically grants to the proper officers of any state, upon the request of the governor, the right of access to corporation returns. All income tax returns are open to inspection by any state tax official, body or commission for the purpose of administration of any state tax law. Upon the request of the governor such information so secured may be made available to taxing authorities of political subdivisions of the state for purposes of tax administration. Bona fide stockholders owning 

1% or more of the outstanding stock of any corporation may inspect the annual income return of such corporation and of its subsidiaries. The Commissioner may promulgate regulations regarding the time and place for the examination of returns. 

The Code also provides for the inspection of returns by the 

Committee on Ways and Means of the House of Representatives and the Committee on Finance of the Senate, and any special committees so authorized by either the House or the Senate or by joint resolution. Officers and employees of the Treasury

Department are required to furnish information requested by such committees.

It is unlawful for any officer or employee of the United States to disclose any information contained in income returns or for any person to publish any income return, except as provided by law. Stockholders who exercise their right of examination shall not disclose any such information gained thereby. Similarly, state officers and employees who have had access to income returns are not permitted to make known any details thereof, except as provided by law. However, the Commissioner is required to prepare and make available for public inspection in each district a list containing the name and address of each person making an income return in the district. 

Should any information acquired through an inspection of the returns in accordance with the rules and regulations be divulged contrary to law, it will constitute a misdemeanor and a fine of not more than $1,000.00, or imprisonment for not more than one year, or both, may be imposed upon the offender.

[Ch. 2 

 ILLUSTRATIVE CASES 

Case 2-1. Robert Brown is a married man who has lived with his wife during the entire taxable year. He has a child four years old. His gross income for the current calendar year amounts to $1,300.00, and his net income amounts to $1,150.00. 

Is he required to file a return?

Solution. Yes, since his gross income is in excess of the minimum amount specified by law. The amount of net income is not a material factor.

Case 2-2. Assume that Robert Brown's wife has income of her own from interest and dividends amounting to $400.00. 

Is it necessary to report this income?

Solution. If Mr. and Mrs. Brown unite in filing a joint return, her separate income must be reported. If Mr. Brown files a separate return, he need not include her income in his return and she will not be required to file a return since her income is less than $500.00. 

Case 2-3. C. P. Moore is a married man living with his wife during the entire taxable year. He has gross income of $4,500.00 and net income amounting to $3,000.00. He has a son sixteen years of age who received gross income of $400.00 from securities owned by him and $300.00 as salary for personal services. Should the income of the son be included in the parent's return or should the son file a separate return?

Solution. The son's income should not be included in the return of the parent, but since his gross income is in excess of $500 he must file a separate return. 

Case 2-4. H. A. Perry is the fiduciary for a trust, the gross income of which for the taxable year amounted to $2,500.00. 

One of the beneficiaries of this trust is a nonresident alien whose share of the trust income amounted to $250.00 What returns should be filed?

Solution. The fiduciary must file a return for the trust and also a return for the nonresident alien beneficiary. 

Case 2-5. Owing to a change in the accounting period, an individual is required to file a return for a period of eight months during which time he has a net income of $1,800.00. 

What amount of annual income should he report?

Solution. The law provides that where a separate return is required for a fractional part of a year on account of a change

Ch. 2] 

in the accounting period, the net income should be computed on an annual basis by multiplying by twelve and dividing by the number of months included in the period for which the return is made. ($1,800X12÷8=$2,700 annual income.) 

Case 2-6. V. L. Brandon has a net income for the year amounting to $4,600.00. Due to willful neglect, Mr. Brandon fails to file a return until six months after the time prescribed by law. What penalties may be imposed?

Solution. In case a return is filed six months late due to 

I willful neglect, the law provides that 25% of the tax shall be added to the tax. The law also provides that if Mr. Brandon is found guilty of a misdemeanor and is convicted, he may be fined not more than $10,000.00 and imprisoned for not more than one year, or both, together with the costs of prosecution. 

Case 2-7. Assuming that it was found upon investigation that Mr. V. L. Brandon had willfully attempted to evade the tax and was convicted of a felony, what fines might be imposed?

Solution. In addition to the penalty imposed by section 

291 of the Code, he might be fined not more than $10,000.00 or be imprisoned for not more than five years, or both, together. with the costs of prosecution.

QUESTIONS AND PROBLEMS 

2-1. Under what condition are individuals required to file income tax returns? 

2-2. Do married persons have an option of filing either a joint return or separate returns? 

2-3. If one spouse dies during the year may a joint return be filed for the year in which death occurred? 

2-4. If one spouse is a nonresident alien may a joint return be filed? 

2-5. What provision is made for filing returns when a taxpayer is ill or absent during the period for filing returns? 

2-6. Under what conditions may an individual elect to let the collector determine the amount of his income tax? 

2-7. John Jones, a single man, has gross income of $590.00 and net income of $445.00 for the taxable year. Should he file a return?

[Ch. 2 

2-8. (a) George White, a married man, has gross income of $1,150.00 for the taxable year. Should he file a return? (b) If the amount of his deductions and credits are such as to make it unnecessary for him to pay a tax, should he file a return? 

2-9. Fred Smith, a married man, has gross income of $2,800.00 for the taxable year. The wife has separate gross income of $400.00. Will it be necessary for each to file a separate return or may they unite in filing a joint return? 

2-10. If a joint return may be filed by Fred Smith and his wife, what aggregate amount of gross income should be reported? 

2-11. If Fred Smith files a return and his wife does not file a separate return, should he include the separate income of the wife in his return? 

2-12. (a) Harold Pond is a widower but has three children who are dependent upon him for their support. He has gross income of $2,800.00 and net income of $750.00 for the current year. Should he file a return? (b) Provided that John, his oldest son, but still a minor, has a separate income of $400.00, should Mr. Pond include this income in his return, or should the son file a separate return? (c) If the income of John amounted to $700.00, should he file a return? 

2-13. Under what conditions are fiduciaries required to file returns? 

2-14. In the event the beneficiary of an estate is a nonresident alien, would the amount of the gross or net income be a deciding factor in determining whether or not the fiduciary should file a return? 

2-15. (a) What is a calendar year? (b) A fiscal year? (c) A taxable year? 

2-16. (a) What is meant by the "cash method" of reporting income? (b) "Accrual method"? 

2-17. What is meant by "constructive receipt" of income? 

2-18. Is it necessary for an individual to make his income tax return under oath or affirmation?

CHAPTER THREE GROSS INCOME OF INDIVIDUALS 1. Compensation (a) Salaries 

(b) Wages (c) Commissions (d) Fees 

2. Dividends (a) Domestic corporations (b) Foreign corporations 

3. Interest (a) Bank deposits, notes, and mortgages (b) Corporation bonds 

4. Income from— (a) Partnerships (b) Syndicates (c) Pools (d) Fiduciaries (e) Rents and royalties 

5. Business or Professional Income 

(a) Trades (b) Businesses 

(c) Professions 6. Profit from Sale or Exchange of Property 

The purpose of this chapter is to define and outline what constitutes gross income for income tax purposes. No attempt is made to exhaust the subject or to name all the various items of taxable income. It is hoped, however, that this discussion will provide a clear explanation of taxable income and that it will define the various sources of income and profits. 

Gross Income Defined. (a) "Gross income" includes gains, profits, and income derived from salaries, wages, or compensation for personal service (including personal service as an officer or employee of a State, or any political subdivision thereof, or any agency or instrumentality of any one or more of the foregoing), of whatever kind and in whatever form paid, or from professions, vocations, trades, businesses, commerce, or sales, or dealings in property, whether real or personal, growing out of the ownership or use of or interest in such property; also from interest, rent, dividends, securities, or the transaction of any business carried on for gain or profit, or gains or profits and income derived from any source whatever. In the case of Presidents of the United States and judges of courts of the United States taking office after June 6, 1932, the compensation received as such shall be included in gross income; and all Acts fixing the compensation of such Presidents and judges are hereby amended accordingly. In the case of judges of courts of the United 

States who took office on or before June 6, 1932, the compensation received as such shall be included in gross income. 

(Section 22-I. R. C.) 

51

[Ch. 3 

GROSS INCOME 

Income which accrued prior to March 1, 1913, but which may be realized or received in the current year need not be reported, because it does not constitute taxable income. The 

Sixteenth Amendment to the Constitution which granted Congress the right to enact laws taxing incomes did not become effective until February 25, 1913. As a matter of convenience, March 1, 1913, generally is taken as the date after which income is deemed taxable. Income specifically exempted from taxation need not be included in gross income but, unless specifically exempted from taxation, all income is taxable.* 

Gross income which individuals are required to report includes the following: (1) Compensation including salaries, wages, commissions, fees, tips, etc. (2) Dividends from domestic and foreign corporations (3) Interest on bank deposits, notes, mortgages, etc. (4) Interest on corporation bonds (5) Taxable interest on government obligations, etc. (6) Income from partnerships, syndicates, pools, etc. (7) Income from fiduciaries (8) Rents and royalties (9) Income from business or profession (10) Profit from sale or exchange of property (11) Other income

Compensation. All forms of compensation for personal services such as salaries, wages, bonuses, commissions, royalties, pensions, fees, percentage of profits, tips, etc., should be included in gross income. Commissions paid salesmen whether based on the sales price, profits, premiums as in the case of insurance, or on any other basis, should be included in gross income. Marriage fees, baptismal offerings, sums paid for saying masses for the dead, or any other contributions received by a clergyman, evangelist, or religious worker for services rendered should be included in gross income. Bonuses, when received as additional compensation for services actually rendered, are taxable income to the recipient. Pensions or retiring allowances paid by private persons are income to the recipient. *See chapter 4 for information on exempt income.

Ch. 3] 

GROSS INCOME 

Compensation may be in the form of cash or other property. 

If in property other than cash, the fair market value, if readily realizable, of the property taken in payment is the amount to be reported as income, provided that if the services were rendered at a stipulated price, such price will be presumed to be the fair value of the compensation received. If compensation is received in the form of stock, it represents income to the extent of its fair market value. If compensation is received in the form of notes or other evidences of indebtedness, it represents income to the extent of their fair market value. A taxpayer who receives a noninterest-bearing note in payment of services should treat as income the fair discounted value of the note at the time of its receipt. Premiums paid on life insurance policies on the lives of officers or employees who are themselves the beneficiaries under the policies, represent taxable income to such officers or employees. The cancellation or forgiveness of indebtedness may constitute compensation which should be included in gross income. If for example, a taxpayer performs services for a creditor, who in consideration thereof cancels a debt, income to the amount of the debt is realized by the taxpayer as compensation for his services and should be included in his gross income. 

Where living quarters, board and lodging, rent or expenses are allowed employees in lieu of a salary, the value thereof must be reported as income, except the rental value of a dwelling house furnished to a minister of the gospel as part of his compensation, or in the event living quarters such as camps are furnished to employees for the convenience of the employer. In this event, the ratable value need not be added to the cash compensation of the employee; hence need not be included in gross income. Where a person receives as compensation for services rendered, a salary and in addition thereto, living quarters, the value to such person of the quarters furnished constitutes income subject to tax. For instance, where employees of the Federal 

Government are provided with living quarters in addition to a salary, the estimated value of such quarters is taxable income. 

The value of quarters and subsistence supplied by the Government to members of the armed forces is not taxable income, however.

Compensation for Services Rendered Over a Long 

Period. If at least 80% of the total compensation for personal services covering a period of thirty-six calendar months or more is received or accrued in one taxable year, the tax attributable to any part thereof which is included in gross income shall

[Ch. 3 

GROSS INCOME 

not be greater than the aggregate of the taxes attributable to such part had it been included in the gross income of the taxpayer ratably over that part of the period which precedes the date of such receipt or accrual. A similar provision is made with respect to the gross income of an individual from a particular artistic work or invention. The purpose of these provisions is to avoid penalizing unfairly persons who may receive income in one year for services rendered over a period of three years or more or compensation for a literary, musical, or artistic composition the work on which covered a period of three years or more. 

Alimony. Periodic payments, received by a wife who is divorced or legally separated from her husband under a decree of divorce, constitute taxable income to the wife and an allowable deduction to the husband. However, any part of such periodic payments which is specifically designated as a sum payable for the support of minor children of the husband does not constitute taxable income to the wife nor an allowable deduction to the husband. Under prior law alimony payments did not constitute taxable income to the wife and was not an allowable deduction to the husband. This change has the effect of making that portion of the husband's income, which is paid to his divorced wife as alimony, taxable income to the wife instead of to the husband.

Dividends. (a) DEFINITION of Dividend.-The term "dividend" when used in this chapter (except in section 201 (c) (5), section 204 (c) (11) and section 207 (a) (2) and (b) (3) (where the reference is to dividends of insurance companies paid to policy holders)) means any distribution made by a corporation to its shareholders, whether in money or in other property, (1) out of its earnings or profits accumulated after February 28, 1913, or (2) out of the earnings or profits of the taxable year (computed as of the close of the taxable year without diminution by reason of any distributions made during the taxable year), without regard to the amount of the earnings and profits at the time the distribution was made. Such term also means any distribution (to the extent of its subchapter A net income, whether or not a dividend as defined in the preceding sentence) to its shareholders, whether in money or in other property, made by a corporation which, under the law applicable to the taxable year in which the distribution is made, is a personal holding company, or which, for the taxable year in respect of which the distribution is made under section 504 (c) or section 506 or a corresponding provision of a prior income tax law, is a personal holding company under the law applicable to such taxable year. 

(b) SOURCE OF DISTRIBUTIONS.-For the purposes of this chapter every distribution is made out of earnings or profits to the extent thereof, and from the most recently accumulated earnings or profits. Any earnings or profits accumulated, or increase in value of property accrued, before March 

1, 1913, may be distributed exempt from tax, after the earnings and profits accumulated after February 28, 1913, have been distributed, but any such tax-free distribution shall be applied against and reduce the adjusted basis of the stock provided in section 113. The preceding sentence shall not apply to a distribution which is a dividend within the meaning of the last sentence of subsection (a).

Ch. 3] 

GROSS INCOME (c) DISTRIBUTIONS IN LIQUIDATION.-Amounts distributed in complete liquidation of a corporation shall be treated as in full payment in exchange for the stock, and amounts distributed in partial liquidation of a corporation shall be treated as in part or full payment in exchange for the stock. The gain or loss to the distributee resulting from such exchange shall be determined under section III, but shall be recognized only to the extent provided in section 112. In the case of amounts distributed (whether before January 1, 1939, or on or after such date) in partial liquidation (other than a distribution to which the provisions of subsection (h) of this section are applicable) the part of such distribution which is properly chargeable to capital account shall not be considered a distribution of earnings or profits. If any distribution in partial liquidation or in complete liquidation (including any one of a series of distributions made by the corporation in complete cancellation or redemption of all its stock) is made by a foreign corporation which with respect to any taxable year beginning on or before, and ending after, August 26, 1937, was a foreign personal holding company, and with respect to which a United States group (as defined in section 331 (a) (2) ) existed after August 26, 1937, and before January 1, 1938, then, despite the foregoing provisions of this subsection, the gain recognized resulting from such distribution shall be considered as a gain from the sale or exchange of a capital asset held for not more than 6 months. 

(d) OTHER DISTRIBUTIONS FROM CAPITAL.—If any distribution made by a corporation to its shareholders is not out of increase in value of property accrued before March 1, 1913, and is not a dividend, then the amount of such distribution shall be applied against and reduce the adjusted basis of the stock provided in section 113, and if in excess of such basis, such excess shall be taxable in the same manner as a gain from the sale or exchange of property. 

This subsection shall not apply to a distribution in partial or complete liquidation or to a distribution which, under subsection (f) (1), is not treated as a dividend, whether or not otherwise a dividend. 

(e) DISTRIBUTIONS BY PERSONAL SERVICE CORPORATIONS.-Any distribu tion made by a corporation, which was classified as a personal service corporation under the provisions of the Revenue Act of 1918 or the Revenue Act of 1921, out of its earnings or profits which were taxable in accordance with the provisions of section 218 of the Revenue Act of 1918 or section 218 of the Revenue Act of 1921, shall be exempt from tax to the distributees. 

(f) STOCK DIVIDENDS. 

(1) General RULE.—A distribution made by a corporation to its share holders in its stock or in rights to acquire its stock shall not be treated as a dividend to the extent that it does not constitute income to the shareholder within the meaning of the Sixteenth Amendment to the Constitution. 

(2) ELECTION of Shareholders as to Medium of PaymenT.—When ever a distribution by a corporation is, at the election of any of the shareholders (whether exercised before or after the declaration thereof), payable either (A) in its stock or in rights to acquire its stock, of a class which if distributed without election would be exempt from tax under paragraph (1), or (B) in money or any other property (including its stock or in rights to acquire its stock, of a class which if distributed without election would not be exempt from tax under paragraph (1) ), then the distribution shall constitute a taxable dividend in the hands of all shareholders, regardless of the medium in which paid. 

(g) REDEMPTION OF STOCK.-If a corporation cancels or redeems its stock 

(whether or not such stock was issued as a stock dividend) at such time and in such manner as to make the distribution and cancellation or redemption in whole or in part essentially equivalent to the distribution of a taxable dividend, the amount so distributed in redemption or cancellation of the stock, to the extent that it represents a distribution of earnings or profits accumulated after February 28, 1913, shall be treated as a taxable dividend. 

(h) EFFECT ON EARNINGS AND PROFITS OF DISTRIBUTIONS OF STOCK.- 

The distribution (whether before January 1, 1939, or on or after such date) to a distributee by or on behalf of a corporation of its stock or securities, of

[Ch. 3 

GROSS INCOME 

stock or securities in another corporation, or of property or money, shall not be considered a distribution of earnings or profits of any corporation- (1) if no gain to such distributee from the receipt of such stock or securities, property or money, was recognized by law, or (2) if the distribution was not subject to tax in the hands of such dis tributee because it did not constitute income to him within the meaning of the Sixteenth Amendment to the Constitution or because exempt to him under section 115 (f) of the Revenue Act of 1934 or a corresponding provision of a prior Revenue Act. As used in this subsection the term "stock or securities" includes rights to acquire stock or securities. 

(i) DEFINITION OF PARTIAL LIQUIDATION.-As used in this section the term "amounts distributed in partial liquidation" means a distribution by a corporation in complete cancellation or redemption of a part of its stock, or one of a series of distributions in complete cancellation or redemption of all or a portion of its stock. 

(j) VALUATION OF DIVIDEND.-If the whole or any part of a dividend is paid to a shareholder in any medium other than money the property received other than money shall be included in gross income at its fair market value at the time as of which it becomes income to the shareholder. 

(k) CONSENT DISTRIBUTIONS.-For taxability as dividends of amounts agreed to be included in gross income by shareholders' consents, see section 28.

(Section 115-I. R. C.) 

Income from dividends may be classified as follows: (a) Dividends received from domestic corporations subject to taxation: (b) Dividends received from domestic corporations not subject to taxation; and (c) Dividends received from foreign corporations. 

In general, any distribution of profits by a domestic or foreign corporation to shareholders constitutes a dividend and must be included in the gross income of the individual receiving it. Any distribution of profits accumulated prior to March 1, 1913, is, however, exempt from the income tax. 

An exempt distribution of profits accumulated prior to 

March 1, 1913, cannot be made until all earnings or profits accumulated after February 28, 1913, have been distributed. In other words, under the regulations, a dividend is considered to be a distribution of the most recently accumulated earnings or profits and it is only after all earnings or profits accumulated since February 28, 1913, have been distributed that profits or earnings accumulated before March 1, 1913, can be distributed free from tax. Furthermore, such tax-free distributions constitute a reduction of the cost of the stock which must be taken into consideration in computing any gain or loss derived from the subsequent sale of stock.

Since the enactment of the Revenue Act of 1936, dividends received by an individual from a domestic corporation have been subject to both the normal tax and the surtax. Previous to that

Ch. 57 

GROSS INCOME 

time they were subject to the surtax only. Dividends received by an individual from foreign corporations are also taxable in full, at normal tax and surtax rates. Dividends are taxable at the rate applicable to the year in which the dividends are received regardless of the year in which the profits were earned. 

Stock Dividends. In the case of Towne vs. Eisner, the 

Supreme Court held that stock dividends are not taxable under the Revenue Act of 1913. In the case of Macomber vs. Eisner, the Supreme Court held that a dividend paid by a corporation on its common stock by issuing to its common stockholders additional common stock, was not income within the meaning of the Sixteenth Amendment, and therefore was not taxable. 

In the case of Koshland vs. Helvering, the Supreme Court held that where preferred stockholders receive a dividend in common stock they receive income which may be taxed under the Six teenth Amendment. 

From these decisions it may be concluded that dividends payable in capital stock of the same class as that already held by the stockholders on which the dividend is based cannot be taxed as income. However, if the stockholder is given the option of receiving a dividend payable in stock or cash or other property, such a dividend may be taxed as income even though the stockholder elects to receive the capital stock instead of the cash or other property. In other words, dividends paid in stock in lieu of cash which alter the proportionate interest of the stockholders in a corporation may be taxed as income. 

Liquidating Dividends. In general, liquidating dividends do not constitute taxable income. A liquidating dividend resulting from the dissolution of a corporation is not taxable unless the amount received exceeds the original cost, or other basis, of the stock. The distribution of profits among stockholders constitutes taxable income except that, if all profits accrued since 

February 28, 1913 have been distributed, any additional distribution of profits out of earnings accrued previous to March 1, 1913, is exempt from taxation.

Where a corporation redeems all or a part of its capital stock, the amount received by a stockholder in exchange for his stock is not treated as a dividend but as payment for the stock. 

Any excess in the amount received over the cost or other basis of the stock is a capital gain which must be reported for taxation. 

If the amount received is less than the cost or other basis of the stock, it constitutes a capital loss which may be claimed as a deduction, subject to the limitations provided by law. If property

[Ch. 3 

GROSS INCOME 

other than cash is received in whole or part payment of the stock, it must be valued at its fair market value and if such property is subsequently sold, the difference between its fair market value, when acquired, and the selling price represents gain or loss which should be reported in accordance with the law applicable to capital gains and losses. 

Interest on Bank Deposits, Notes, Mortgages, etc. All interest received or credited to the taxpayer's account during the taxable year on bank deposits, building and loan association deposits, notes, and mortgages, is taxable and must be reported. 

Interest on bank deposits and building and loan association accounts must be reported as income when credited to the account of the depositor whether or not it is withdrawn. 

Interest on Corporation Bonds. Interest on bonds is considered income when due and payable whether or not actually received or collected, thus in the case of coupon bonds the interest becomes taxable income when due and payable whether or not the coupons are presented for payment immediately. 

Interest accrued on bonds at the time of purchase does not constitute taxable income when received. For example, if a 6%, $1,000.00 bond is purchased at par plus accrued interest of $12.50, the subsequent receipt of the semiannual interest of $30.00 represents a return of the $12.50 interest advanced and interest income of $17.50. If the taxpayer is on the accrual basis rather than the cash basis the interest must be reported as earned rather than as received. Thus, in the above example if no interest is received in the taxable year of purchase of the bond and the total interest accrued at the close of the taxable year is assumed to be $16.75, the income to be reported is $4.25. 

Taxable Interest on Government Obligations. The interest received on all obligations of the United States issued previous to September 1, 1917, is exempt from taxation. Interest on obligations of the United States issued since September 1, 1917, is exempt from the income tax only to the extent provided by the Acts under which the issues were authorized or by any subsequent Acts.

Such interest on obligations of the United States as is subject to any tax must be reported by the recipient. If any part of the interest received is subject to the surtax but is not subject to the normal tax, it must be included in gross income but may be taken as a credit in computing the normal tax. Interest on

Ch. 3] 

GROSS INCOME 

coupon bonds must be reported in the taxable year in which the coupons mature whether or not the coupons are clipped from the bonds and cashed or deposited in a bank, unless the taxpayer keeps his books on the accrual basis, in which case the actual amount of interest accrued during the year must be reported without regard to the maturity date of the coupons. 

Taxpayers who employ the accrual method of accounting should include in their returns the increment in value of United States Savings bonds as income in each taxable year. The amount of income is based on the amounts set forth in the table of redemption values shown on the bonds. Although such income is exempt from normal tax, it is subject to the surtax imposed on individuals.

Prior to the 1941 Act individuals on the cash basis reported interest income on such bonds only when the cash was received, i.e. upon redemption. Taxpayers now have the option of reporting income on obligations of this type (noninterest-bearing, issued at a discount) on the accrual basis regardless of their method of accounting. If elected, the option applies to all obligations of this type owned or subsequently acquired. If any such bonds were owned prior to the beginning of the taxable year in which the option is elected the increase in redemption price occurring from the date of acquisition to the beginning of the taxable year is to be considered as earned in the year of election. 

For example, a taxpayer reporting on the calendar year basis, using the cash method of determining income, purchased a $375.00 U. S. Savings Bond in February, 1940 and a $750.00 bond in August, 1941. In 1942 he elects to report the interest on these bonds by the accrual method. On December 31, 1942 the bonds have redemption values of $395.00 and $760.00 respectively. The interest income to be reported for 1942 is $30.00, which is the increase in redemption value since the date the bonds were acquired. For subsequent years he must continue to report on the accrual basis. 

In the case of Federal or state short-term obligations issued on a discount basis after February 28, 1941, the discount on the original issuance is to be considered to accrue only at maturity or at the time of sale or other disposition. An obligation having a fixed maturity date of not exceeding one year from the date of issuance is considered short-term. Thus, taxpayers on the accrual basis must report income on this type of obligation according to the cash basis.

[Ch. 3 

GROSS INCOME 

Income from Partnerships, Syndicates, Pools, etc.

Income or profits from a partnership, syndicates, pools, etc., must be reported for taxation whether received or not. In the case of losses, such losses should be reported and claimed as a deduction from other income. The income or loss is to be reported net, and the name, address, and kind of business from which the income is derived or loss sustained must also be reported. This makes possible the checking of the individual taxpayer's return with the returns of the partnership, syndicates, or pools from which he derives the income or sustains the loss. 

An organization, the membership interests in which are transferable, and the business of which is conducted by trustees or directors and officers, without the active participation of all the members as such, is an association and not a partnership. 

Under the regulations, the term "partnership" includes a syndicate, group, pool, joint venture, or other unincorporated organization, through or by means of which any business, financial operation or venture is carried on, and which is not, within the meaning of the Act, a trust or estate or a corporation; and the term "partner" includes a member in such a syndicate, group, pool, joint venture, or organization.

Income from Fiduciaries. Income received from an estate or trust must be reported, and the name and address of the fiduciary given. If the taxable year, on the basis of which the return of an individual is filed, does not coincide with the annual accounting period of the partnership, syndicate, pool, or fiduciary, then the taxpayer should include in his return his distributive share of the income for such accounting period ending within his taxable year. In case of a loss the loss should be reported on a similar basis.

Rents and Royalties. Income from rents and royalties must be included in gross income. Income arising from the rent of property whether tangible or intangible is taxable. If property or crops are received in lieu of cash rent, the value of the property or crops received should be reported as income just as if the rent had been received in cash. In determining the net income received from rent or royalty, the taxpayer is permitted to deduct depreciation, obsolescence, depletion, repairs, interest, taxes, fire insurance, fuel, light, labor and other necessary expenses of this character. Thus, if a taxpayer received income in the form of rent on residential property, he would report the difference between the amount received and the sum of the expenses applicable to the upkeep of the property.

Ch. 3] 

GROSS INCOME 

Interest and taxes paid by a tenant on behalf of the landlord are income to the landlord when paid and must be included as income in his return. Ordinarily, repairs made by the tenant are not considered income for the landlord, but expenses of the tenant. The value of the use of property by the owner need not be considered income to the owner nor may it be deducted as rent paid.

The income derived from royalty received for the use of or the privilege of using patents, copyrights, secret processes and formulas, good will, trade-marks, trade brands, franchises, etc., should be included in gross income for it represents taxable income to the individual receiving it. 

Income from Business or Profession. Persons engaged in business or practicing a profession of any kind must report the income received from such business or profession. This includes income derived from the following sources: (1) Sale of merchandise or products of manufacturing, mining, construction, and agriculture. (2) Business service, such as amusements, hotel, restaurant, garage service, laundering, storage, transportation, etc. (3) Professional service, such as dentistry, law, medicine, or accountancy.

The taxable income is the net income which is determined by deducting from the total income received from the business or profession the sum of the following items: 

(a) Cost of Goods Sold (b) Other Business Deductions 

Cost of Goods Sold. The cost of goods sold includes, in the case of a trading business, or any business in which merchandise is bought and sold, the inventory at the beginning of year plus the cost of merchandise purchased for sale during the year, minus the inventory at end of year. The cost of goods sold includes, in the case of a manufacturing business, the inventory of raw material and supplies on hand at the beginning of year, plus purchases of material and supplies, and labor during year, minus the inventory of material and supplies at end of year. In other words, the prime cost of merchandise sold during the year is the amount to be deducted under the title of "cost of goods sold."

Accounting for Inventories. In every case in which the production, purchase, or sale of merchandise is an income-producing factor, inventories at the beginning and end of each taxable

[Ch. 3 

GROSS INCOME 

year are necessary in order to reflect the net income correctly. 

Where book inventories are maintained at cost in accordance with a sound accounting system, such book inventories may be used in the determination of income, providing they are verified by physical inventories at reasonable intervals and adjusted to conform therewith. Otherwise, physical inventories must be used.

The system of valuation used must conform to the best accounting practice in the trade or business, and it must clearly reflect the income. While an inventory method need not conform to arbitrary rules, the bases of valuation approved for income tax purposes and most commonly used are (a) cost and (b) cost or market, whichever is lower. Prior to the enactment of the Revenue Act of 1938, the “first-in, first-out" method was prescribed for identifying goods on hand. According to this method goods included in the inventory which have been so intermingled that they cannot be identified with specific invoices are deemed to be the goods most recently acquired. The 1938 

Act permitted tanners and certain nonferrous metal industries to use the "last-in, first-out" method. With the 1939 Act, permission to use this method was extended to other industries, effective for taxable years beginning after December 31, 1938.

In order to adopt the "last-in, first-out" method it is necessary to secure the consent of the Commissioner. It must be established to the satisfaction of the Commissioner that the taxpayer has actually used the method in ascertaining income for credit purposes or for the purpose of reports to shareholders or other proprietors. If this method is adopted, the goods in the inventory must be valued at cost. Furthermore, the taxpayer must continue to use this method unless authorized or required by the Commissioner to change to another.

The 1942 Act provided relief for taxpayers using the last-in, first-out method of inventory valuation whose normal stocks are depleted due to inability to make replacements. If the taxpayer satisfactorily establishes the involuntary nature of his inventory liquidation, and if he replaces such units at least by the end of the third year after cessation of hostilities, his taxable income may be redetermined for the year of involuntary liquidation. His taxable income for such year would be reduced or increased, depending on whether the replacement cost was greater or less than the cost of the liquidated inventory units. 

In other words, the taxpayer is treated as if subsequent replacements of involuntarily liquidated inventory units had been made in the year of liquidation.

Ch. 63 

GROSS INCOME 

In order that the taxpayer may have an opportunity to benefit from this provision of the law he must make an irrevocable election, at the time of filing his return for the year in which the involuntary liquidation of inventory occurs. The effect of the election is that an adjustment of income will be required for the year of liquidation when the inventory is eventually replaced provided replacement occurs within three years after termination of the war. In other words, an amended return will be required regardless of whether it results in an increase or a decrease in taxable income. If the inventory is replaced at lower cost it will result in an increase in the taxable income, while if the inventory is replaced at higher cost it will result in a decrease in the taxable income. 

Two additional inventory practices which are recognized as proper may be noted. A dealer in securities is permitted three alternative bases of valuation: (a) cost; (b) lower of cost or market; and (c) market value. Retail merchants, who employ what is known as the retail method of pricing inventories, may make their returns upon that basis under the following conditions: (a) provided that the use of such method is designated in the return; (b) provided that accurate accounts are kept; and (c) provided such method is consistently adhered to. Under the retail method, the total of the retail selling prices of the goods on hand must be reduced to approximate cost by deducting therefrom an amount which bears the same ratio to such total asless (a) the total of the retail selling price of the goods included in the opening inventory, plus the retail selling price of the goods purchased during the year, with proper adjustment to such selling prices for all markups and markdowns (b) the cost of the goods included in the opening inventory plus the cost of the goods purchased during the year, bears to (a).

Consistency in the use of inventory rules is of even greater importance in clearly reflecting income from year to year than the particular inventory practice adopted. In 1920 taxpayers were given an option to adopt the basis of either (a) cost or (b) cost or market, whichever is lower, in determining inventories.

The basis properly adopted for that year or any subsequent year is controlling and may not be altered without the permission of the Commissioner.

[Ch. 3 

GROSS INCOME (c) INVENTORIES.-Whenever in the opinion of the Commissioner the use of inventories is necessary in order clearly to determine the income of any taxpayer, inventories shall be taken by such taxpayer upon such basis as the Commissioner, with the approval of the Secretary, may prescribe as conforming as nearly as may be to the best accounting practice in the trade or business and as most clearly reflecting the income. 

(d) METHOD USED IN INVENTORYING GOODS.-(1) A taxpayer may use the following method (whether or not such method has been prescribed under subsection (c)), in inventorying goods specified in the application required under paragraph (2): (A) Inventory them at cost; (B) Treat those remaining on hand at the close of the taxable year as being: First, those included in the opening inventory of the taxable year (in the order of acquisition) to the extent thereof, and second, those acquired in the taxable year; and (C) Treat those included in the opening inventory of the taxable year in which such method is first used as having been acquired at the same time and determine their cost by the average cost method. (2) The method described in paragraph (1) may be used— (A) Only in inventorying goods (required under subsection (c) to be inventoried) specified in an application to use such method filed at such time and in such manner as the Commissioner may prescribe; and (B) Only if the taxpayer establishes to the satisfaction of the Commissioner that the taxpayer has used no procedure other than that specified in subparagraphs (B) and (C) of paragraph (1) in inventorying such goods to ascertain the income, profit, or loss of the first taxable year for which the method described in paragraph (1) is to be used, for the purpose of a report or statement covering such taxable year (i) to shareholders, partners, or other proprietors, or to beneficiaries, or (ii) for credit purposes. (3) The change to, and the use of, such method shall be in accordance with such regulations as the Commissioner, with the approval of the Secretary, may prescribe as necessary in order that the use of such method may clearly reflect income. (4) In determining income for the taxable year preceding the taxable year for which such method is first used, the closing inventory of such preceding year of the goods specified in such application shall be at cost. (5) If a taxpayer, having complied with paragraph (2), uses the method described in paragraph (1) for any taxable year, then such method shall be used in all subsequent taxable years unless- (A) With the approval of the Commissioner a change to a different method is authorized; or (B) The Commissioner determines that the taxpayer has used for any such subsequent taxable year some procedure other than that specified in subparagraph (B) of paragraph (1) in inventorying the goods specified in the application to ascertain the income, profit, or loss of such subsequent taxable year for the purpose of a report or statement covering such taxable year (i) to shareholders, partners, or other proprietors, or beneficiaries, or (ii) for credit purposes; and requires a change to a method different from that prescribed in paragraph (1) beginning with such subsequent taxable year or any taxable year thereafter. 

(Section 22-I. R. C.) Other Business Deductions. Other business deductions include the following: (1) Salaries not included as "labor" in the computation of the cost of goods sold, except that no deduction can be claimed for compensation of the taxpayer himself, his dependent minor children, or his wife if a joint return is filed. (2) Interest on business indebtedness to others.

Ch. 65 

GROSS INCOME 

(3) Taxes on business and business property, not including assessments for local improvements nor Federal income taxes. (4) Losses of business property caused by fire, storm, or other casualty, or theft, not compensated for by insurance or otherwise, and not made good by repairs otherwise claimed as deductions. 

(5) Bad debts arising from sales or services which become worthless within the taxable year, or a reasonable addition to a reserve for bad debts. (6) Depreciation, obsolescence, and depletion. Do not claim any deduction for depreciation on the value of a building occupied as a dwelling, or other property held for personal use, as such depreciation is not an allowable deduction from business income. (7) Rent, repairs, and other ordinary and necessary business expenses such as fire insurance premiums, traveling expenses, and the cost of electrical energy. 

In case a business or profession is conducted at a loss or deficit, such loss should be reported and claimed as a deduction from other income of the taxpayer. 

Profit from Sale or Exchange of Property. Transactions involving the sale or exchange of property must be reported whether or not any gain or loss results. There are two classes of property which may be designated as (a) ordinary property, and (b) capital assets. "Capital assets" means property held by the taxpayer (whether or not connected with his trade or business), but does not include (a) stock-in-trade, (b) property used in the trade or business of a character which is subject to depreciation, (c) obligations of the United States issued on or after March 1, 1941 on a discount basis and payable without interest not exceeding one year from the date of issue, or real property used in the trade or business of the taxpayer. Securities such as stocks and bonds and the taxpayer's personal residence are the principal types of capital assets. 

Gains or losses realized from the sale or exchange of capital assets held for not more than six months constitute short-term capital gains or losses; over six months, long-term capital gains or losses. Under prior law, eighteen months was the dividing point.

In the case of a taxpayer, other than a corporation, capital gains and losses are taken into account in computing net capital gain, net capital loss, and net income on the following bases:

[Ch. 3 

GROSS INCOME 

(a) 100% if the capital asset has been held for not more than six months. (b) 50% if the capital asset has been held for more than six months. (A further discussion of capital assets and methods of computing gains and losses arising from the sale or exchange thereof, appears in Chapter Six.)

Income from Improvements by Lessee. Under the 1942 

Act no taxable income is derived from the termination of a lease that results in the landlord acquiring buildings or other improvements thereby. The Supreme Court had held previously in the case of Helvering v. Brown that buildings or other improvements made by a lessee constitute income to a lessor to the extent of the value of such improvements at the time the lease is forfeited and the lessor secures control and possession of the property. Such exclusion from gross income of the lessor does not mean that the enhancement in value will not be ultimately taxed. The law provides that the basis of the property in the hands of the lessor shall not be affected by the reversion of the improvements to the lessor in a taxable year beginning after December 31, 1941. Thus, taxable gain may be realized when the property is ultimately disposed of by the lessor. 

In case the lessor has in prior years reported income realized either upon termination of a lease or during the term of a lease because of improvements made by the lessee, the amount of such income will be added to the basis of the property in order to provide a proper adjustment for the income so taxed. 

Gross Income of Nonresident Aliens. (a) GENERAL RULE. In the case of a nonresident alien individual gross income includes only the gross income from sources within the United States. (b) SHIPS UNDER FOREIGN FLAG.-The income of a nonresident alien individual which consists exclusively of earnings derived from the operation of a ship or ships documented under the laws of a foreign country which grants an equivalent exemption to citizens of the United States and to corporations organized in the United States shall not be included in gross income and shall be exempt from taxation under this chapter. 

(Section 212-I. R. C.) 

The taxable income of a nonresident alien individual includes that derived in full from sources within the United States, and that portion of the income which is derived partly from sources within and partly from sources without the United States which is allocated or apportioned to sources within the United States. 

The income may include interest, dividends, compensation for labor or personal services, rentals and royalties, and gains derived from the sale of real or personal property. Income derived from the purchase and sale of personal property shall be treated

Ch. 67 

GROSS INCOME 

as derived entirely from the country in which sold, except that income derived from the purchase of personal property within the United States and its sale within a possession of the United States, or from the purchase of personal property within a possession of the United States and its sale within the United States, shall be treated as income derived partly from sources within and partly from sources without the United States. Such gains may be derived from sales or exchanges.

ILLUSTRATIVE CASES 

Case 3-1. A taxpayer keeps his books on the cash basis. 

At the end of the taxable year he has a number of interest-bearing notes on hand. In preparing his return should he include in gross income the interest accrued on these notes? 

Solution. Where books are kept on the cash basis, interest on notes receivable is returnable for the year in which received regardless of the period in which earned. Thus the amount of accrued interest need not be reported in the year in which accrued if not received in the same year.

Case 3-2. A taxpayer leased a building and equipment to a corporation, agreeing to install such additional equipment as might be required. The lessee agreed to pay as rental a specified sum for each article manufactured and all charges for water, light, heat, power, taxes, etc. Does the payment of such charges constitute additional taxable income to the lessor? 

Solution. It was held that all of the payments were income to the lessor as additional rent. In other words, the lessor's taxable income is the specified rental plus whatever charges for water, light, heat, power, taxes, etc., were paid by the corporation. 

Case 3-3. A firm of public accountants employs juniors and requires them to attend a school of accounting and maintain a certain average. The tuition, cost of books, etc., are paid by the employer. Does such payment constitute taxable income to the employees?

Solution. It has been held that the amount paid by the employer for tuition, books, etc., constituted additional compensation, and therefore represented taxable income to the employees.

Case 3-4. Creditors of a taxpayer desiring to allow the debtor to continue in business, and deciding they could realize no more by composition or forced liquidation, agreed to accept a percentage of their claims. Does such cancellation of indebtedness constitute taxable income to the taxpayer?

[Ch. 3 

GROSS INCOME 

Solution. The Board of Tax Appeals has ruled on a number of cases similar to the one cited, and has held that since the cancellation of the indebtedness does not constitute compensation for services, nor income from a business entered into for profit, nor gain from capital, or labor, or both, it is not taxable income.

QUESTIONS AND PROBLEMS 

3-1. Outline briefly what constitutes "gross income" under the present income tax law. 

3-2. The Acme Machine Tool Company earned a net profit for the calendar year ending December 31. In January following the determination of the net profit for the preceding year, it was decided to give Nathan Stevens, an employee, a bonus of 1% of the profits, or $350.00, which is in addition to his salary. 

Mr. Stevens computes his taxable income on the cash basis and files his return on March 15. Does this bonus constitute taxable income to Mr. Stevens? If so, should he include the bonus in his return for the taxable year in which the bonus was earned, or in the taxable year during which the bonus was received? 

3-3. On December 31 of the current taxable year C. B. 

Zobel receives in payment of services rendered Ralph Duhme, a noninterest-bearing promissory note for $5,000.00, payable in 60 days. Zobel ascertains that the note can be discounted at his bank at 6%, but decides to hold it until maturity. If he prepares his income tax return for the year in which the note was received on the cash basis, what amount of income should be reported from this transaction?

Ch. 71 

GROSS INCOME 

3-4. J. W. Winters is indebted to B. A. Leonard to the extent of $450.00. He paints Leonard's residence whereupon 

Leonard agrees to cancel his debt. Does this result in taxable income to Winters? 

3-5. Ralph Trapp, a single man, is employed on a farm receiving a cash salary of $600.00 a year and, in addition, he is furnished with living quarters and board. In the locality in which he is employed, living quarters are estimated to be worth $12.00 per month and board $20.00 per month. 

What is the amount of Trapp's gross income? Will it be necessary for him to file a return? 

3-6. W. E. Reber keeps a personal set of books on the cash basis. He has a savings account in the Security Bank and Trust 

Company on which he receives interest at the rate of 2%. On 

December 31 of the current taxable year the bank credits his account with interest amounting to $110.00. Should this interest be reported as income in Reber's return for the year? 

3—7. R. L. Bueg leases a plot of ground to F. J. Kuehne at an annual rental of $4,000.00 plus all taxes assessed against the property. During the past year the taxes amounted to $1,250.00. 

What amount should Bueg include as gross income from the rent in preparing his income tax return? 

3-8. M. B. Wills is the author of a copyrighted book. 

The company which published the book has agreed to pay him a royalty of 10% of the amount of the gross sales. The book sells at $2.00 and during the current calendar year 24,150 copies were sold. The author receives a communication from the publishing company on January 15 advising him of the sales for the preceding year together with a check to cover the amount of his royalty. 

Assuming that Mr. Wills reports his income on the cash basis, should he include the amount of this royalty in his gross income and if so, should it be reported in the year in which it was earned or in the year in which it was received? 

3-9. J. T. Chase sells Thos. Babb, for $50,000.00, certain property which Chase purchased in 1914 for $30,000.00. Babb agrees to pay Chase, in addition to the purchase price, the income taxes which Chase may be required to pay on the profit of $20,000.00. Does this reimbursement by Babb of the taxes paid by Chase constitute additional income which Chase will be required to report in his return?

[Ch. 3 

GROSS INCOME 

3-10. The Davis Co., a domestic corporation, during the current year declares a dividend from its 1911 surplus, having previously distributed all earnings accumulated since March 1, 1913. (a) Should G. A. Ross, who is a stockholder in the corporation, include this dividend in gross income when preparing his income tax return? (b) If the dividend had represented a distribution of profits for the current taxable year and payment had been made in U. S. Treasury bonds, should G. A. Ross include his share in gross income? If so, should par value or fair market value of the bonds be used? 

3-11. During the current taxable year Charles Purdy received liquidating dividends on stock which he had purchased two years earlier. What is the status of such dividends for 

Federal income tax purposes?

CHAPTER FOUR EXEMPT INCOME OF INDIVIDUALS 1. Insurance 

(a) Life insurance contracts payable after death (b) Life insurance contracts payable before death (c) Annuities (d) Health and accident insurance 

2. Tax-exempt Property 

(a) Gifts (b) Bequests (c) Devises (d) Inheritances 

3. Tax-Free Interest 4. Compensation of State Officers and Employees 5. Rental Value of Ministers' Dwellings 6. Other Exempt Income 

The purpose of this chapter is to outline what constitutes exempt income or income free from taxation. Any income which is specifically exempted by statute should be excluded from gross income in filing the return, that is, tax-free income need not be mentioned in the return.

Exempt Income. (b) EXCLUSIONS FROM GROSS INCOME.-The follow ing items shall not be included in gross income and shall be exempt from taxation under this chapter: 

(1) LIFE INSURANCE.-Amounts received under a life insurance contract paid by reason of the death of the insured, whether in a single sum or otherwise (but if such amounts are held by the insurer under an agreement to pay interest thereon, the interest payments shall be included in gross income); 

(2) ANNUITIES, ETC.- 

(A) IN GENERAL.-Amounts received (other than amounts paid by reason of the death of the insured and interest payments on such amounts and other than amounts received as annuities) under a life insurance or endowment contract, but if such amounts (when added to amounts received before the taxable year under such contract) exceed the aggregate premiums or consideration paid (whether or not paid during the taxable year) then the excess shall be included in gross income. Amounts received as an annuity under an annuity or endowment contract shall be included in gross income; except that there shall be excluded from gross income the excess of the amount received in the taxable year over an amount equal to 3 per centum of the aggregate premiums or consideration paid for such annuity (whether or not paid during such year), until the aggregate amount excluded from gross income under this chapter or prior income tax laws in respect of such annuity equals the aggregate premiums or consideration paid for such annuity. In the case of a transfer for a valuable consideration, by assignment or otherwise, of a life insurance, endowment, or annuity contract, or any interest therein, 73

[Ch. 4 

EXEMPT INCOME 

only the actual value of such consideration and the amount of the premiums and other sums subsequently paid by the transferee shall be exempt from taxation under paragraph (1) or this paragraph. The preceding sentence shall not apply in the case of such a transfer if such contract or interest therein has a basis for determining gain or loss in the hands of a transferee determined in whole or in part by reference to such basis of such contract or interest therein in the hands of the transferor. This subparagraph and paragraph (1) shall not apply with respect to so much of a payment under a life insurance, endowment, or annuity contract, or any interest therein, as, under section 22 (k), is includible in gross income; 

(B) EMPLOYEES' ANNUITIES.-If an annuity contract is purchased by an employer for an employee under a plan with respect to which the employer's contribution is deductible under section 23 (p) (1) (B), or if an annuity contract is purchased for an employee by an employer exempt under section 101 (6), the employee shall include in his income the amounts received under such contract for the year received except that if the employee paid any of the consideration for the annuity, the annuity shall be included in his income as provided in subparagraph (a) of this paragraph, the consideration for such annuity being considered the amount contributed by the employee. In all other cases, if the employee's rights under the contract are nonforfeitable except for failure to pay future premiums, the amount contributed by the employer for such annuity contract on or after such rights become nonforfeitable shall be included in the income of the employee in the year in which the amount is contributed, which amount together with any amounts contributed by the employee shall constitute the consideration paid for the annuity contract in determining the amount of the annuity required to be included in the income of the employee under subparagraph (A) of this paragraph. 

(3) GIFTS, BEQUESTS, DEVISES, AND INHERITANCES.-The value of property acquired by gift, bequest, devise, or inheritance. There shall not be excluded from gross income under this paragraph, the income from such property, or, in case the gift, bequest, devise, or inheritance is of income from property, the amount of such income. For the purposes of this paragraph, if, under the terms of the gift, bequest, devise, or inheritance, payment, crediting, or distribution thereof is to be made at intervals, to the extent that it is paid or credited or to be distributed out of income from property, it shall be considered a gift, bequest, devise, or inheritance of income from property; 

(4) TAX-FREE INTEREST.-Interest upon (A) the obligations of a State, 

Territory, or any political subdivision thereof, or the District of Columbia; or (B) obligations of a corporation organized under Act of Congress, if such corporation is an instrumentality of the United States; or (C) the obligations of the United States or its possessions. Every person owning any of the obligations enumerated in clause (A), (B), or (C) shall, when so required by regulations prescribed by the Commissioner with the approval of the Secretary, submit in the return required by this chapter, a statement showing the number and amount of such obligations owned by him and the income received therefrom, in such form and with such information as such regulations may prescribe. In the case of obligations of the United States issued after September 1, 1917 (other than postal savings certificates of deposit to the extent they represent deposits made before March 1, 1941) and in the case of obligations of a corporation organized under Act of Congress, the interest shall be exempt only if and to the extent provided in the respective Acts authorizing the issue thereof as amended and supplemented, and shall be excluded from gross income only if and to the extent it is wholly exempt from the taxes imposed by this chapter; 

(5) COMPENSATION FOR INJURIES OR SICKNESS.-Except in the case of amounts attributable to (and not in excess of) deductions allowed under section 23 (x) in any prior taxable year, amounts received through accident or health insurance or under workmen's compensation acts, as compensation for personal injuries or sickness, plus the amount of any damages received whether by suit or agreement on account of such injuries or sickness, and amounts received as a pension, annuity, or similar allowance for personal injuries or sickness resulting from active service in the armed forces of any country.

(6) MINISTERS.-The rental value of a dwelling house and appurtenances thereof furnished to a minister of the gospel as part of his compensation; 

(7) INCOME EXEMPT Under TREATY.—Income of any kind, to the extent required by any treaty obligation of the United States;
