(13) ADDITIONAL ALLOWANCE FOR MILITARY and Naval PERSONNEL.- 

In the case of compensation received during any taxable year and before the termination of the present war as proclaimed by the President, by a member of the military or naval forces of the United States for active service in such forces during such war, or by a citizen or resident of the United States who is a member of the military or naval forces of any of the other United Nations for active service in such forces during such war, so much of such compensation as does not exceed $1,500.

NEL. 

(14) MUSTERING-OUT PAYMENTS FOR MILITARY AND NAVAL PERSON- 

Amounts received during the taxable year as mustering-out payments with respect to service in the military or naval forces of the United States. 

(Section 22-I. R. C.) 

Life Insurance. Amounts received by an estate or any beneficiary, including an individual, partnership, or a corporation, by reason of the death of the insured is exempt from all income taxes. For example, assume that a man owns life insurance policies on his own life with a face value of $20,000.00. Upon his death the $20,000.00 is paid to the beneficiaries named in the policies. The proceeds do not constitute gross income to any of the beneficiaries, regardless of relationship to the insured or of the amount of premiums paid in by the insured. It is to be observed, however, that only the amount paid solely by reason of the death of the insured is exempt. If the policy provides that the beneficiary may leave the proceeds with the company at interest or that the proceeds be converted into an annuity the increment in value is taxable. In other words, the amount payable immediately after the death of the insured, had the policy not provided for payment at a later date or dates, is the amount exempt from income taxes. 

The exemption does not apply to a beneficiary who is a transferee for a valuable consideration. The excess of the amount received over the original consideration and premiums subsequently paid must be reported as income. Thus, if the transferee beneficiary paid $2,000.00 to the insured for a $10,000.00 policy and continued to pay the premiums which aggregated $2,500.00 at the time of death of the insured, he would realize a taxable income of $5,500.00.

Amounts received under a life insurance or endowment policy other than by reason of the death of the insured are ex-

[Ch. 4 

EXEMPT INCOME 

empt from income taxes until the aggregate of the amounts so received exceeds the aggregate premiums or consideration paid, whether or not paid during the taxable year. In other words, the investment in such a contract may be received in settlement without any income tax liability being incurred, but any amount received in excess of the cost is taxable income. 

This does not apply to amounts received as annuities under any life insurance contract, but would apply in case of maturity of an endowment contract or in case of the surrender of a life insurance policy for cash.

Amounts received as an annuity under an annuity or endowment contract shall be included in gross income, except that there shall be excluded from gross income the excess of the amount received in the taxable year over an amount equal to 3% of the aggregate premiums or consideration paid for such annuity, until the aggregate amount excluded from gross income equals the aggregate premiums or consideration paid for such annuity. The operation of this provision may be illustrated as follows: Taxpayer purchased, in 1938, for $40,000.00, a life annuity, payable in annual installments of $4,000.00. For the current taxable year, as in preceding taxable years, he must include $1,200.00 (3% of $40,000.00) in gross income. The amount excluded is thus $2,800.00 ($4,000.00 minus $1,200.00).

The annual gross income from this annuity will continue to be $1,200.00 until the amount excluded, i.e. $2,800.00 per year, equals $40,000.00; thereafter all amounts received must be included in gross income. If upon maturity of an endowment contract payments are made to the insured by installments rather than in a lump sum the installments are treated as an annuity. The consideration paid for the annuity is the sum of the premiums paid on the endowment contract. 

Prior to 1934 receipts from annuity contracts were not taxable until the entire cost had been recovered. Therefore, for annuities on which installments were received prior to 1934 the entire amounts so received were excluded from gross income and must be so considered in computing the amount of current receipts which are taxable. 

If an annuity is payable in semiannual, quarterly, or monthly installments the amount of each installment to be included in gross income is determined by apportionment. 

Thus, if in the illustration above there are four quarterly installments of $1,000.00 each instead of an annual installment of $4,000.00 the amount of each installment to be included in

Ch. 4] 

EXEMPT INCOME 

gross income is $300.00 (4 of 3% of $40,000.00). The amount to be excluded from each installment is $700.00 ($1,000.00 minus $300.00).

Dividends on unmatured insurance policies which are in the nature of returns of premiums do not constitute taxable income. However, dividends received on paid up insurance policies are taxable when received by the insured, if they exceed the aggregate premiums or consideration paid. 

In one case where upon maturity of a 20-year life policy the holder had the option of (a) drawing a cash dividend and continuing the policy as paid up participating insurance payable at death, (b) converting the dividends into paid up participating additional insurance, or (c) surrendering the policy for a guaranteed reserve and the cash dividend, elected the first option, it was held that the cash dividend plus any other amounts received exceeding the aggregate premiums or consideration paid for the insurance constituted taxable income. 

The proceeds of a matured endowment contract received by the insured are exempt from the Federal income tax, except that any amount received in excess of the aggregate premiums or consideration paid, whether or not paid during the taxable year, constitutes taxable income. 

Where a life insurance policy is surrendered for cash, no taxable income is realized unless the amount received exceeds the aggregate premiums or consideration paid. Any sum received in excess of the cost of the contract represents gain which is subject to the Federal income tax. It will be seen that there is no difference in the treatment of gains derived by the insured from the surrender of a life insurance contract for cash and gains derived from settlement of an endowment contract at maturity.

The proceeds of a life insurance policy paid by reason of the death of the insured to any beneficiary other than a transferee for a valuable consideration are exempt from the Federal income tax. The proceeds may be payable directly or in trust, and it is immaterial whether the proceeds are paid to the beneficiary in a single sum or in installments, but only the amount paid solely by reason of the death of the insured is exempt from the income tax.

In no event may the estate or a named beneficiary under a life insurance contract receive more exempt income than the amount payable at the time of the death of the insured, had the

[Ch. 4 

EXEMPT INCOME 

insured or the beneficiary not elected to exercise an option to receive the proceeds of the policy or any part thereof at a later date or dates. Where the proceeds are payable in installments, any increment to the amount which would have been paid immediately after the death of the insured had the policy not provided for payment in installments is taxable. Following are the common modes of settlement and the method of determining the taxable income in each case: (1) Proceeds held by the insurer under an agreement with the insured or the beneficiary to distribute either the increment or the proceeds and increment in equal installments until both are exhausted. (2) Proceeds payable in installments for a fixed number of years, or for a fixed number of years and for continued life. (3) Proceeds payable in installments during the life of the beneficiary.

The increment so paid to the beneficiary, or so credited to the fund in each year by the insurer, is taxable and must be included in the gross income of the beneficiary. 

To find the amount of exempt income, divide the amount payable upon the death of the insured (if payment at a later date had not been provided for) by the total number of installments payable over the fixed number of years for which payment is to be made. Any additional amount received in installments is taxable income and must be included in the gross income of the beneficiary. 

Taxable income determined as in (2) except that the number of years to be used in the specified computation will be determined by the life expectancy of the beneficiary, as calculated by the table of mortality used by the particular insurance company in determining the amount of the annuity.

Where a life insurance policy has been transferred for a valuable consideration, payments on account of the death of the insured constitute income of the assignee to the extent that

Ch. 79 

EXEMPT INCOME 

they exceed the sum of the consideration paid by the assignee and the premiums and other amounts subsequently paid thereon by such assignee.

Income received through accident or health insurance or workmen's compensation acts as compensation for personal injuries or sickness, or any damages recovered by suit or agreement on account of such injuries or sickness, is exempt from taxation whether received by the insured while still living or by his estate or other beneficiary following his death. Amounts received as a pension, annuity, or similar allowance for personal injuries, or sickness resulting from active service in the armed forces of any country are exempt from taxation. 

Since June 25, 1918, no assessment of any Federal tax may be made on any allotments, family allowances, compensation, or death or disability insurance payable under the War Risk Insurance Act of September 2, 1914, as amended, even though the benefit accrued before that date. In addition to this exemption from all Federal taxes, amounts received as compensation, family allotments and allowances under the provisions of the 

War Risk Insurance Act, the Vocational Rehabilitation Act or the World War Veterans' Act, 1924, or as pensions from the United States for service of the beneficiary or another in the military or naval forces of the United States in time of war, or as a state pension for services rendered by the beneficiary or another for which the state is paying a pension, are exempt from income taxation.

Unemployment benefits paid by a state agency out of funds received from the Federal Unemployment Trust Fund are not subject to tax in the hands of the recipients. It has also been ruled that lump sum old-age benefit payments are not taxable.

Gifts and Bequests. Property of all kinds, whether real or personal, when received as a gift or received under a will or statute of descent and distribution is exempt from the income tax. 

While the principal amount received from these sources is exempt from the income tax, any income derived from the investment of such amount or gains resulting from the sale of the property received, is taxable and must be included in gross income. Periodic payments, received by a wife who is divorced or legally separated from her husband under a decree of divorce is not considered a gift, but is treated as taxable income.

[Ch. 4 

EXEMPT INCOME 

Tax-Free Interest. Interest upon the obligations of a state, territory, or any political subdivision thereof, or the District of Columbia is exempt from the income tax. This has reference to obligations issued by or on behalf of the state or territory or a duly organized political subdivision acting by constituted authorities empowered to issue such obligations. The term "political subdivision" denotes any subdivision of the state or territory made by the proper authorities thereof acting within their constitutional powers. Thus political subdivisions may include special assessment districts such as road, water, sewer, gas, light, reclamation, drainage, irrigation, levee, school, harbor, port improvement and any similar districts and divisions of a state or territory.

The interest upon obligations of the United States and its possessions issued prior to September 1, 1917, is wholly exempt from the Federal normal income tax and surtax. These obligations include 3% Conversion Bonds dated January 1, 1916, and 

1917; and Panama Canal Bonds dated June 1, 1911.

In the case of obligations issued after September 1, 1917, the interest is exempt only if and to the extent provided in the Acts authorizing their issue. 

Under the provisions of the Public Debt Act of 1941, the interest upon all obligations of the United States and its agencies and instrumentalities issued after February 28, 1941, is subject to Federal income taxes without any exemption. The interest on Treasury Bonds and U. S. Savings Bonds issued after Sep tember 1, 1917, and before March 1, 1941, is wholly exempt from normal tax and is exempt from surtax to the extent of an aggregate principal of $5,000.00. The interest on Treasury 

Notes, Treasury Bills, and Postal Savings Bonds issued after 

September 1, 1917, and before March 1, 1941, is wholly exempt from both the normal tax and the surtax. 

When interest is wholly exempt from both the normal tax and surtax it need not be included in the gross income of the taxpayer, but when it is subject to any income tax it must be included in gross income and such interest as is included in gross income but which is not subject to the normal tax may be taken as a credit in the computation of the normal tax. 

It should be noted that the reference throughout this section has been to interest on obligations. A taxable gain or deductible loss may result from the sale of such obligations. Furthermore, the transfer of such obligations is not exempt from the estate and gift taxes.

Ch. 4] 

EXEMPT INCOME 

The income derived from dividends from Federal Reserve Banks is exempt from the income tax. However, dividends paid by member banks are subject to the same income tax provisions as dividends received from ordinary corporations. 

Every person owning tax-free obligations of the United States or its possessions, obligations issued by a corporation, if such corporation is an instrumentality of the United States, or obligations of a state, territory, or any political subdivision thereof, or the District of Columbia must submit a statement with his income tax return showing the number and amount of such obligations owned and the income received therefrom. 

Compensation of State Employees. Prior to two de cisions of the Supreme Court, handed down in 1938, the compensation paid to officers and employees by a state or its political subdivisions for services rendered in connection with the exercise of an essential governmental function was generally exempt from the Federal income tax. This was not a statutory exemption but was implied from the Constitutional limitations on the power of the Federal Government to interfere with the functions of the states. In determining the taxability of compensation received for services rendered to a state it was necessary to consider, first, whether the services were rendered in connection with an essential governmental function and, second, whether the taxpayer received such compensation as an officer or employee of the state. 

The furnishing of education was held to be an essential governmental function, the furnishing of transportation was held to be a proprietary or nonessential governmental function. A person who occupied a position in the service of a state or political subdivision, the terms of which were continuous and not temporary, and the duties of which were established by law or regulations and not by agreement, was deemed to be an officer. An employee was deemed to be one whose duties consisted in the rendition of prescribed services and whose services were continuous. These considerations are no longer material in determining the taxability of compensation received by an individual from a state. 

In Helvering v. Therrell et al., (303 U. S. 218), decided February 28, 1938, the Supreme Court held that the Federal Government has the power to tax compensation received by attorneys and others in connection with the liquidation of insolvent bank and insurance corporations. In this case, the corporations paid the compensation, although the appointment of the recip-

[Ch. 4 

EXEMPT INCOME 

ients and the regulation of their duties were governed by state statutes.

The question at issue in the case of Helvering v. Gerhardt et al., (304 U. S. 405), was whether the imposition of a Federal income tax on compensation received by employees of the Port of New York Authority (a corporation created by New York and New Jersey for the performance of governmental functions) constituted an unconstitutional burden on the states of New York and New Jersey. The court held that any burden resulting from the Federal taxation of the employees' compensation which reached the state is a necessary incident to the coexistence of two taxing sovereigns (i. e. state and Federal) within the same organized government and that the Constitution presupposes the existence of such a burden. The decision was handed down on May 23, 1938.

In the case of Graves v. O'Keefe et al., (No. 478), decided on March 27, 1939, the reverse situation was involved, namely, the power of the state of New York to impose an income tax on the compensation of an employee of the Home Owners' Loan Corporation. The Supreme Court upheld the Tax Commission of the state of New York. It now appears that there is no implied immunity from income taxation of officers or employees of the Federal or State Governments or their instrumentalities. 

These recent cases cleared the way for the Public Salary Tax Act of 1939, which subjected the compensation of state officers and employees (including members of a legislature and judges and officers of a court) to the Federal income tax. The section of the Revenue Act of 1938 providing for exemption of salaries of school teachers employed by the governments of Alaska and Hawaii was also repealed by the Act. Safeguards were provided against the imposition of the income tax on compensation received prior to January 1, 1939.

Compensation of Federal Officers and Employees. In general the compensation received by Federal officers and employees is subject to the income tax. Under this rule the pay received by persons in the military and naval forces of the United States is taxable. At the time of the enactment of the 1932 Act it was provided that the salaries of the President of the United States and Federal judges then holding office were exempt from taxation. The 1934 Act provided, however, that the compensation received by the President of the United States and Federal judges taking office after June 6, 1932, be included in gross in-

Ch. 83 

EXEMPT INCOME 

come and that it be taxable in the same manner as compensation received by other persons.

The Public Salary Tax Act of 1939 contained the provision that the compensation of judges of courts of the United States, who took office on or before June 6, 1932, shall be included in gross income. Thus, for taxable years beginning after December 

31, 1938, the compensation of all judges of Federal courts is subject to the income tax irrespective of when they took office. 

Compensation of Military and Navy Personnel. Mem bers of the military or naval forces of the United States and citizens or residents of the United States, who are members of the military or naval forces of any of the other United Nations, may exclude from gross income $1,500 of the compensation received during any taxable year for active service in such forces during the present war. The amount excluded from gross income may not exceed the amount actually received as compensation in the armed forces. For example, an individual receiving less than $1,500 from November 10, the date of his enlistment, to December 31 could exclude from gross income for the calendar year only the amount actually received. Amounts received as mustering-out payment with respect to service in the military or naval forces of the United States may also be excluded from gross income.

ILLUSTRATIVE CASES 

Case 4-1. A taxpayer purchases an annuity payable at the rate of $100.00 per month beginning at age 65. The total cost of the annuity was $10,000.00. In the current taxable year the taxpayer receives a total of $1,200.00 income from the annuity. What amount of this income, if any, should be reported for taxation? 

Solution. 3% of $10,000.00 $300.00 Taxable income $1,200.00-$300.00 $900.00 Exempt income

The law provides that an amount equal to 3% of the aggregate cost of an annuity represents taxable income, the balance of the income received being exempt until such aggregate amount excluded from gross income equals the total cost of the annuity when any excess income becomes wholly taxable. 

Case 4-2. An architect contracts with several cities for special work in connection with the construction and erection of public buildings. Is his compensation exempt from taxation on the ground that he is an employee of a subdivision of a state?

[Ch. 4 

EXEMPT INCOME 

Solution. Even before the enactment of the Public Salary 

Tax Act of 1939, the compensation would have been subject to tax. The Board of Tax Appeals has ruled in this and numerous similar cases that such compensation is not exempt from taxation, on the ground that the work is not permanent or continuous in character but that the duties are prescribed and the compensation fixed by contract.

Case 4-3. In one case it was held that a hospital operated by a county under the statutes of the state of California, authorizing the county to provide for its indigent sick and dependent poor, was an instrumentality of the state engaged in the exercise of an essential governmental function. Collections from patients were negligible. Are salaries received by employees of the hospital exempt from taxation?

Solution. In accordance with the provisions of the Public 

Salary Tax Act of 1939, the salaries are subject to taxation. 

Case 4 4. A public school district issues bonds bearing interest at 42% the proceeds to be used in the construction of a new building. After erecting the building it employs a principal and several teachers. (a) If Miss Helen Burke buys one of the bonds will it be necessary for her to report the interest as taxable income assuming that she has sufficient income from other sources to make it necessary for her to file a return? (b) 

If she is subsequently employed as a teacher in the school at a salary of $1,800.00 per year, will it be necessary for her to report her salary as taxable income?

Solution. (a) The law provides that interest upon the obligations of a state, or any subdivision thereof, shall be exempt from taxation, hence it will not be necessary for Miss Burke to report the interest on the school bond which she owns as taxable income. (b) For taxable years beginning after December 31, 1938, the compensation of all state officers and employees as well as the compensation of all officers and employees of the Federal Government, is taxable.

4-5. Does it make any difference whether the payments are being made under (1) an annual premium deferred annuity after it has matured, or (2) a single premium deferred annuity after the deferred period has ended, or (3) a single premium immediate annuity? 

4-6. Will a gift annuity bought by one person (the donor) but payable to another (the donee) for the latter's life be subject to income tax? 

4-7. A bought on January 2, 1935, for $50,000.00 consideration, a life annuity, payable in annual installments of $5,000.00, the first installment being payable January 2, 1936. (a) Of the income received by A during the current calendar year, what amount will be taxable and what amount will be exempt from Federal income taxes? (b) Should A live long enough to receive an annuity of $5,000.00 during the calendar year 1950, what amount will be taxable and what amount exempt income? 

4-8. B bought an annuity on July 1 of the current calendar year, paying $96,000.00 as consideration therefor. The annuity amounts to $12,000.00 a year, payable in monthly installments of $1,000.00 each. On August I of the current calendar year, he received the first installment. (a) What amount of the income received during the current calendar year should B include in his Federal income tax return? (b)

What amount of B's income from this annuity for the current calendar year is exempt from income tax?

CHAPTER FIVE DEDUCTIONS ALLOWED INDIVIDUALS 1. Ordinary and Necessary Business Expenses 

(a) Compensation (b) Interest (c) Taxes (d) Losses (e) Bad debts (f) Depreciation, obsolescence, and depletion (g) Rent, repairs, and other expenses 

2. Personal Expenses 

(a) Interest (b) Taxes (c) Losses (d) Bad debts 

(e) Contributions 3. Net Operating Loss 4. Items Not Deductible 

This chapter is devoted to a discussion of the allowable deductions from gross income. In the preparation of an income tax return, the first step is to determine the taxable income and compute the amount of gross income. In the preceding discussion, an attempt has been made to outline the various sources of income and to distinguish between taxable income and income exempt from taxation. After the gross income has been determined, certain deductions are allowed in computing net income. 

The income tax is computed on the net income and not the gross income. The net income is determined by subtracting the allowable deductions from the gross income. 

Deductions from Gross Income. In computing net income there shall be allowed as deductions: 

(a) EXPENSES.- (1) TRADE OR BUSINESS EXPENSES.- 

(A) In General.-All the ordinary and necessary expenses paid or incurred during the taxable year in carrying on any trade or business, including a reasonable allowance for salaries or other compensation for personal services actually rendered; traveling expenses (including the entire amount expended for meals and lodging) while away from home in the pursuit of a trade or business; and rentals or other payments required to be made as a condition to the continued use or possession, for purposes of the trade or business, of property to which the taxpayer has not taken or is not taking title or in which he has no equity. 

(2) NON-TRADE OR NON-BUSINESS EXPENSES.-In the case of an indi vidual, all the ordinary and necessary expenses paid or incurred during the taxable year for the production or collection of income, or for the management, conservation, or maintenance of property held for the production of income. 87

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

(b) INTEREST.-All interest paid or accrued within the taxable year on indebtedness, except on indebtedness incurred or continued to purchase or carry obligations (other than obligations of the United States issued after September 24, 1917, and originally subscribed for by the taxpayer) the interest upon which is wholly exempt from the taxes imposed by this chapter. 

(c) TAXES GENERALLY.— 

(1) ALLOWANCE in General.-Taxes paid or accrued within the taxable year, except- (A) Federal income taxes; (B) war-profits and excess-profits taxes imposed by Title II of the 

Revenue Act of 1917, Title III of the Revenue Act of 1918, Title III of the Revenue Act of 1929, section 216 of the National Industrial Recovery Act, section 702 of the Revenue Act of 1934, or Subchapter E of Chapter 2, or by any such provisions as amended or supplemented; (C) income, war-profits, and excess-profits taxes imposed by the authority of any foreign country or possession of the United States, if the taxpayer chooses to take to any extent the benefits of section 131. (D) estate, inheritance, legacy, succession, and gift taxes. (E) taxes assessed against local benefits of a kind tending to increase the value of the property assessed; but this paragraph shall not exclude the allowance as a deduction of so much of such taxes as is properly allocable to maintenance or interest charges; and (F) Federal import duties, and Federal excise and stamp taxes (not described in subparagraph (A), (B), (D), or (E)), but this subsection shall not prevent such duties and taxes from being deducted under subsction (a). (3) RETAIL SALES TAX.-In the case of a tax imposed by any State, Territory, District, or possession of the United States, or any political subdivision thereof, upon persons engaged in selling tangible personal property at retail, which is measured by the gross sales price or the gross receipts from the sale or which is a stated sum per unit of such property sold, or upon persons engaged in furnishing services at retail, which is measured by the gross receipts for furnishing such services, if the amount of such tax is separately stated, then to the extent that the amount so stated is paid by the purchaser (otherwise than in connection with the purchaser's trade or business) to such person such amount shall be allowed as a deduction in computing the net income of such purchaser as if such amount constituted a tax imposed upon and paid by such purchaser. 

(e) LOSSES BY INDIVIDUALS.-In the case of an individual, losses sus tained during the taxable year and not compensated for by insurance or otherwise- (1) if incurred in trade or business; or (2) if incurred in any transaction entered into for profit, though not connected with the trade or business; or (3) of property not connected with the trade or business, if the loss arises from fires, storms, shipwreck, or other casualty, or from theft. No loss shall be allowed as a deduction under this paragraph if at the time of the filing of the return such loss has been claimed as a deduction for estate tax purposes in the estate tax return. 

(g) CAPITAL LOSSES.- (1) LIMITATION.-Losses from sales or exchanges of capital assets shall be allowed only to the extent provided in section 117.

(2) SECURITIES BECOMING Worthless.—If any securities (as defined in paragraph (3) of this subsection) become worthless during the taxable year and are capital assets, the loss resulting therefrom shall, for the purposes of this chapter, be considered as a loss from the sale or exchange, on the last day of such taxable year, of capital assets. 

(3) DEFINITION OF SECURITIES.-As used in this paragraph (2) of sub section the term "securities" means (A) shares of stock in a corporation, and (B) rights to subscribe for or to receive such shares. 

(4) STOCK IN AFFILIATED CORPORATION. For the purposes of para graph (2) stock in a corporation affiliated with the taxpayer shall not be

Ch. 5] 

DEDUCTIONS 

deemed a capital asset. For the purposes of this paragraph a corporation shall be deemed to be affiliated with the taxpayer only if: (A) at least 95 per centum of each class of its stock is owned directly by the taxpayer; and (B) more than 90 per centum of the aggregate of its gross incomes for all taxable years has been from sources other than royalties, rents (except rents derived from rental of properties to employees of the company in the ordinary course of its operating business), dividends, interest (except interest received on deferred purchase price of operating assets sold), annuities, or gains from sales or exchanges of stocks and securities; and (C) the taxpayer is a domestic corporation. 

(h) WAGERING LOSSES.-Losses from wagering transactions shall be al lowed only to the extent of the gains from such transactions.

(i) BASIS FOR DETERMINING LOSS.-The basis for determining the amount of deduction for losses sustained, to be allowed under subsection (e) or (f), and for bad debts, to be allowed under subsection (k), shall be the adjusted basis provided in section 113 (b) for determining the loss from the sale or other disposition of property. 

(j) Loss ON WASH SALES OF STOCK OR SECURITIES.-For disallowance of loss deduction in the case of sales of stock or securities where within thirty days before or after the date of the sale the taxpayer has acquired substantially identical property, see section 118. 

(k) BAD DEBTS.— 

(1) GENERAL RULE.-Debts which become worthless within the taxable year; or (in the discretion of the Commissioner) a reasonable addition to a reserve for bad debts; and when satisfied that a debt is recoverable only in part, the Commissioner may allow such debt, in an amount not in excess of the part charged off within the taxable year, as a deduction. This paragraph shall not apply in the case of a taxpayer, other than a bank, as defined in section 104, with respect to a debt evidenced by a security as defined in paragraph (3) of this subsection. This paragraph shall not apply in the case of a taxpayer, other than a corporation, with respect to a non-business debt, as defined in paragraph (4) of this subsection. 

(2) SECURITIES BECOMING WORTHLESS.-If any securities (as defined in paragraph (3) of this subsection) become worthless within the taxable year and are capital assets, the loss resulting therefrom shall, in the case of a taxpayer other than a bank, as defined in section 104, for the purposes of this chapter, be considered as a loss from the sale or exchange, on the last day of such taxable year, of capital assets. 

(3) DEFINITION OF SECURITIES.-As used in paragraphs (1), (2), and 

(4) of this subsection the term "securities" means bonds, debentures, notes, or certificates, or other evidences of indebtedness, issued by any corporation (including those issued by a government or political subdivision thereof), with interest coupons or in registered form. (4) NON-BUSINESS DEBTS.-In the case of a taxpayer, other than a corporation, if a non-business debt becomes worthless within the taxable year, the loss resulting therefrom shall be considered a loss from the sale or exchange, during the taxable year, of a capital asset held for not more than 6 months. The term "non-business debt" means a debt other than a debt evidenced by a security as defined in paragraph (3) and other than a debt the loss from the worthlessness of which is incurred in the taxpayer's trade or business. 

(1) DEPRECIATION.-A reasonable allowance for the exhaustion, wear and tear (including a reasonable allowance for obsolescence)— (1) of property used in the trade or business, or (2) of property held for the production of income. 

In the case of property held by one person for life with remainder to another person, the deduction shall be computed as if the life tenant were the absolute owner of the property and shall be allowed to the life tenant. In the case of property held in trust the allowable deduction shall be appor-

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

tioned between the income beneficiaries and the trustee in accordance with the pertinent provisions of the instrument creating the trust, or, in the ab sence of such provisions, on the basis of the trust income allocable to each. (m) DEPLETION.-In the case of mines, oil and gas wells, other natural deposits, and timber, a reasonable allowance for depletion and for depreciation of improvements, according to the peculiar conditions in each case; such reasonable allowance in all cases to be made under rules and regulations to be prescribed by the Commissioner, with the approval of the Secretary. In any case in which it is ascertained as a result of operations or of development work that the recoverable units are greater or less than the prior estimate thereof, then such prior estimate (but not the basis for depletion) shall be revised and the allowance under this subsection for subsequent taxable years shall be based upon such revised estimate. In the case of leases the deduction shall be equitably apportioned between the lessor and lessee. In the case of property held by one person for life with remainder to another person, the deduction shall be computed as if the life tenant were the absolute owner of the property and shall be allowed to the life tenant. In the case of property held in trust the allowable deduction shall be apportioned between the income beneficiaries and the trustee in accordance with the pertinent provisions of the instrument creating the trust, or, in the absence of such provisions, on the basis of the trust income allocable to each. For percentage depletion allowable under this subsection, see section 114 (b), (3) and (4). 

(n) BASIS FOR DEPRECIATION AND DEPLETION.-The basis upon which depletion, exhaustion, wear and tear, and obsolescence are to be allowed in respect of any property shall be as provided in section 114. 

(0) CHARITABLE AND OTHER CONTRIBUTIONS.-In the case of an indi vidual, contributions or gifts payment of which is made within the taxable year to or for the use of: (1) The United States, any State, Territory, or any political subdivision thereof or the District of Columbia, or any possession of the United States, for exclusively public purposes; (2) A corporation, trust, or community chest, fund, or foundation, created or organized in the United States or in any possession thereof or under the law of the United States or of any State or Territory or of any possession of the United States, organized and operated exclusively for religious, charitable, scientific, literary, or educational purposes, or for the prevention of cruelty to children or animals, no part of the net earnings of which inures to the benefit of any private shareholder or individual, and no substantial part of the activi. ties of which is carrying on propaganda, or otherwise attempting, to influence legislation; (3) the special fund for vocational rehabilitation authorized by section 12 

of the World War Veterans' Act, 1924, 43 Stat. 611 (U. S. C., Title 38 8440); 

(4) posts or organizations of war veterans, or auxiliary units or societies of any such posts or organizations, if such posts, organizations, units, or societies are organized in the United States or any of its possessions, and if no part of their net earnings inures to the benefit of any private shareholder or individual; or (5) a domestic fraternal society, order, or association, operating under the lodge system, but only if such contributions or gifts are to be used exclusively for religious, charitable, scientific, literary, or educational purposes, or for the prevention of cruelty to children or animals; to an amount which in all the above cases combined does not exceed 15 per centum of the taxpayer's adjusted gross income. Such contributions or gifts shall be allowable as deductions only if verified under rules and regulations prescribed by the Commissioner, with the approval of the Secretary. For unlimited deduction if contributions and gifts exceed 90 per centum of the net income, see section 120.

DEDUCTIONS (s) NET OPERATING Loss DEDUCTION. For any taxable year beginning after December 31, 1939, the net operating loss deduction computed under section 122. 

(u) ALIMONY, ETC., PAYMENTS.—In the case of a husband described in section 22 (k), amounts includible under section 22 (k) in the gross income of his wife, payment of which is made within the husband's taxable year. If the amount of any such payment is, under section 22 (k) or section 171, stated to be not includible in such husband's gross income, no deduction shall be allowed with respect to such payment under this subsection. (v) BOND PREMIUM DEDUCTION.-In the case of a bondholder, the deduction for amortizable bond premium provided in section 125. 

(w) DEDUCTIONS OF ESTATE, ETC., ON ACCOUNT OF DECEDent's De- DUCTIONS.- 

(1) In the case of a person described in section 126 (b), the amount of the deductions in respect of a decedent to the extent allowed by such subsection. (2) In the case of a person described in section 126 (a), the amount of the deductions in respect of a decedent to the extent allowed by section 126 (c). 

(x) MEDICAL, DENTAL, ETC., EXPENSES.-Except as limited under paragraph (1) or (2), expenses paid during the taxable year, not compensated for by insurance or otherwise, for medical care of the taxpayer, his spouse, or a dependent specified in section 25 (b) (3), to the extent that such expenses exceed 5 per centum of the adjusted gross income. If only one surtax exemption is allowed under section 25 (b) for the taxable year, the maximum deduction for the taxable year shall be not in excess of $1,250. If more than one surtax exemption is so allowed, the maximum deduction shall be not in excess of $2,500. The term "medical care," as used in this subsection, shall include amounts paid for the diagnosis, cure, mitigation, treatment, or prevention of disease, or for the purpose of affecting any structure or function of the body (including amounts paid for accident or health insurance). (1) A husband and wife who file a joint return may deduct only such expenses as exceed 5 per centum of the aggregate net income of such husband and wife, computed without the benefit of this subsection, and the maximum deduction for the taxable year shall be not in excess of $2,500 in the case of such husband and wife. (2) An individual who files a separate return may deduct only such expenses as exceed 5 per centum of the net income of the taxpayer, computed without the benefit of this subsection, and the maximum deduction for the taxable year shall be not in excess of $2,500 in the case of the head of a family, and not in excess of $1,250 in the case of all other such individuals. 

(Section 23-I. R. C.) 

Trade or Business Expenses. The net income from a business or profession is the gross income, less the sum of (a) the cost of goods sold, and (b) other business deductions. A copy of the schedule which every individual deriving income from a business or profession is required to submit as a part of his return is shown at the top of the following page: 

If the taxpayer is engaged in a trade or business in which the production, manufacture, purchase, and sale of merchandise is an income-producing factor, inventories of merchandise on hand should be taken at the beginning and end of the taxable year and should be valued at (a) cost, or (b) cost or market, whichever is lower. This was discussed in detail in Chapter Three.
