Salaries. In reporting salaries, the taxpayer must not include compensation for services of himself, or of his wife if a joint return is filed, as such compensation is not deductible. In other words, the net profit or loss from a business or profession must be computed without any deduction for services paid to the taxpayer, or his wife if a joint return is filed. Where separate returns are filed by husband and wife, compensation paid to either for services actually rendered may be claimed as a deduction in computing the net profit or loss derived by the other from a business profession in which he is engaged. 

It will be noted that a distinction is made between labor and salaries. Labor incurred in the manufacture of goods is to be included in the "Cost of Goods Sold." All other wages or salaries paid, with the exceptions noted above, should be included in "Other Business Expenses."

Ch. 5] 

DEDUCTIONS 

Interest. The only interest which may be treated as a business deduction, is interest on business indebtednesses to others. Interest paid to the taxpayer himself on capital invested in or advanced to the business, is not deductible. 

Taxes. Taxes imposed on business property, or for the privilege of carrying on business are deductible as a business expense. However, taxes assessed against local benefits of a kind tending to increase the value of the property assessed are not deductible.

Losses. Losses incurred in the trade or business are deductible if not compensated for by insurance or otherwise. Losses arising from fire, storm, shipwreck, or other casualty, or from theft are also deductible. It is necessary to explain and itemize losses claimed, setting forth a description of the property and giving the following additional information: (a) Date acquired (b) Cost or value as of March 1, 1913, whichever is the proper basis (c) Subsequent improvements (d) Depreciation allowable since acquisition (e) Insurance (f) Salvage value (g) Deductible loss.

Bad Debts. Debts arising from sales or services that have been reflected in income, which become worthless within the year, or such reasonable amount as has been added to a Reserve for Bad Debts within the year, are deductible. The taxpayer is given an option of using either the direct method or reserve method of accounting for bad debts. In 1921 taxpayers were given an option of selecting either the direct or reserve method of accounting for bad debts. The method used in the return for 1921 must be used in returns for subsequent years unless permission is granted by the Commissioner to change to the other method.

A taxpayer filing a first return of income may select either of the two methods subject to approval by the Commissioner upon examination of the return. If the method used in the first return is approved it must be followed in all subsequent returns unless permission is obtained from the Commissioner to change

94 [Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

to the other method. Application for permission to change the method of treating bad debts must be filed at least thirty days prior to the close of the taxable year for which the change is to be effective. In using the direct method, only such debts as become worthless during the year shall be allowed as a deduction. Before a taxpayer may deduct a debt in part, he must ascertain and be able to demonstrate with a reasonable degree of certainty the amount thereof which is uncollectible. Where the surrounding circumstances indicate that a debt is worthless and uncollectible, and that legal action to enforce payment would in all probability not result in the satisfaction or execution of a judgment, a showing of these facts will be sufficient evidence of the worthlessness of the debt for the purpose of deduction. 

The following factors must be considered in determining the deductibility of a bad debt: (1) The debt must be worthless and uncollectible in whole or in part. (2) Deduction must be claimed in the year in which the debt becomes worthless. (3) Legal expenses incurred in an effort to collect accounts receivable are a proper business deduction. (4) Accrued interest on accounts or notes receivable may be included as a part of the deduction only when it has previously been reported as income.

Under the reserve method of accounting for bad debts the taxpayer is required to make a statement in his return showing the volume of his charge sales or other business transactions for the year, and the percentage of the reserve to such amount, the total amount of notes and accounts receivable at the beginning and close of the taxable year, and the amount of debts which have been ascertained to be wholly or partially worthless and charged against the reserve account during the taxable year. 

What constitutes a reasonable addition to a Reserve for Bad Debts must be determined in the light of the facts and will vary as between classes of business and with conditions of business prosperity. Accountants and business men generally recognize the soundness of the reserve method of accounting for bad debts and it probably is safe to state that the reserve method is used in the returns filed by most business men. Properly applied it will result in a more uniform deduction with a corresponding uniformity of net profit. The amount of worthless debts charged off from year to year may vary the same as when the direct

Ch. 95 

DEDUCTIONS 

method is used, but the annual addition to the Reserve for Bad Debts usually will be fairly uniform over a period of years. 

Where the direct method of charging off bad debts is used, amounts subsequently collected on account of a bad debt previously deducted, must be included in gross income for the taxable year in which received unless the prior deduction did not result in an income tax benefit for the year of the deduction. 

However, where the reserve method is used it will be satisfactory to credit the amount subsequently collected to the reserve account, provided that the amount so credited is taken into consideration in computing the amount to be added to the reserve for that year.

Bad debt and partial bad debt deductions are denied a taxpayer, other than a bank, when the debt is evidenced by securities which classify as capital assets. Such securities include bonds, debentures, notes or certificates, or other evidences of indebtedness issued by any corporation, with interest coupons or in registered form. Such losses are subject to the capital loss provisions and deduction cannot be claimed, therefore, as ordinary losses. 

Depreciation. The amount claimed as depreciation caused by exhaustion, wear and tear on property used in trade or business, or as obsolescence, or depletion, should be treated as a business deduction. If obsolescence is claimed, it is necessary to explain why the useful life of the property is less than the actual life. It is also necessary to explain the amount claimed as a deduction for depreciation in a separate schedule, the following information being required: (1) Kind of property (2) Date acquired (3) Cost or other basis (4) Assets fully depreciated in use at end of year (5) Depreciation allowed (or allowable) in prior years (6) Remaining cost of other basis to be recovered (7) Life used in accumulating depreciation (8) Estimated remaining life from beginning of year (9) Depreciation allowable this year

The amount of depreciation on property acquired by purchase should be determined upon the basis of the original cost of the property and the probable number of years remaining of its useful life, except that if the property was purchased prior to March 1, 1913, the depreciation should be computed on a basis of the fair market value of such property as of that date,

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

or its original cost adjusted, whichever is greater. If the original cost is used it must be adjusted for any depreciation sustained between date of acquisition and March 1, 1913.

No depreciation can be claimed on land, stock, bonds, or securities, or on a building occupied by the taxpayer as a dwelling. 

Only business property and property held for the production of income is subject to depreciation for income tax purposes. 

The regulations do not prescribe any definite method to be used in computing the depreciation allowance. Whatever plan or method of apportionment is adopted must be reasonable and must have due regard for operating conditions during the taxable period. The reasonableness of any claim for depreciation shall be determined upon the conditions known to exist at the end of the period for which the return is made. Where the cost or other basis of property has been recovered through depreciation or other allowances, no further deduction for depreciation shall be allowed. The deduction for depreciation in respect of any depreciable property for any taxable year, shall be limited to such ratable amount as may reasonably be considered necessary to recover during the remaining useful life of the property, the unrecovered cost or other basis. The burden of proof will rest upon the taxpayer to sustain the deduction claimed. 

Under Treasury Decision 4422 approved by the Secretary of the Treasury February 28, 1934, taxpayers must furnish full and complete information with respect to the cost or other basis of assets in respect of which depreciation is claimed; their age, condition and remaining useful life, the portion of their cost or other basis which has been recovered through depreciation allowances for prior taxable years, and such other information as the Commissioner may require in substantiation of the deduction claimed.

A professional man is entitled to deduct a reasonable allowance for depreciation sustained on a library which is necessary and used wholly in the pursuit of his profession, taking as a basis for such allowance the cost or other allowable basis. The cost of professional magazines and books purchased by a business or professional man and having a temporary value should be deducted as an expense of doing business, but the cost of books which have a more permanent value to the business or profession should be capitalized and made the subject of depreciation allowances.

If obsolescence is claimed, it is necessary to explain why the useful life of the property is less than the actual life. No

Ch. 5] 

DEDUCTIONS 

deduction for obsolescence will be permitted merely because, in the opinion of a taxpayer, the property may become obsolete at some later time. This allowance will be confined to such portion of the property on which obsolescence is definitely shown to be sustained and cannot be held applicable to an entire property unless all portions thereof are affected by the condition to which obsolescence is found to be due. Where a taxpayer can show that the effect of economic conditions on physical property is such that depreciation deductions alone are insufficient to return the cost or other basis at the end of its economic term of usefulness, a reasonable deduction for obsolescence, in addition to depreciation, may be allowed in accordance with the facts obtaining with respect to each item of property concerning which a claim for obsolescence is made. 

If depletion is claimed as a deduction, it will be necessary to compute it in accordance with section 114 of the Internal Revenue Code. (See Chapter Six for a discussion of depletion.) 

Rent, Repairs, and Other Expenses. Rent of business property in which the taxpayer has no equity, ordinary repairs to keep the property in a usable condition, and other necessary business expenses not previously claimed as a deduction, such as, heat, light, and fire insurance, should be treated as business deductions. Rent for a dwelling occupied by the taxpayer for residential purposes is not deductible, neither is the cost of business equipment or furniture, expenditures for replacements or permanent improvements to property, nor personal, living, or family expenses deductible.

If after deducting from gross income the cost of goods sold and all other deductible business expenses, the result is a net loss or deficit, the amount should be preceded by a minus sign, or written with red ink, as an indication that it is to be deducted from other income of the taxpayer in arriving at the total income. 

There are certain tests which may be applied in determining what expenses may be deducted in computing the net income of a business or profession: (1) Only ordinary and necessary expenses paid or incurred during the taxable year in carrying on the trade or business may be deducted. (2) Any necessary expense not actually a capital investment incurred in good faith in a particular business is to be considered an ordinary expense of that business. (3) Capital expenditures are not deductible but should be treated as permanent investments to be added to the cost of the

886 [Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

property to which they apply. Depreciation of the property based upon the cost, adjusted for capital expenditures, is a proper deduction. (4) Personal, living, or family expenses are not deductible except in the case of medical and dental expenses in excess of 5% of adjusted gross income. (5) Expenses incurred in the earning of wholly tax-exempt income are not deductible except in the case of expenses incurred in earning tax-exempt interest. 

Adjusted Gross Income. The term "adjusted gross income" means, in general, gross income less business expenses. 

The term is used primarily in connection with the optional tax table. The table can be used only by taxpayers whose adjusted gross income is less than $5,000 and the tax set forth in the table is based on adjusted gross income. 

Specifically, adjusted gross income means gross income less the sum of the following deductions: 

(1) Deductions which are attributable to a trade or business carried on by the taxpayer, not consisting of services performed as an employee. (2) Deductions which constitute expenses of travel, meals, and lodging paid or incurred by the taxpayer while away from home in connection with the performance by him of services as an employee. (3) Deductions, other than expenses of travel, meals, and lodging paid or incurred while away from home, which constitute expenses paid or incurred in connection with the performance of services as an employee under a reimbursement or other expense allowance arrangement with the employer. (4) Deductions which are attributable to rent or royalties. (5) Deductions for depreciation and depletion to a life tenant of property or to an income beneficiary of property held in trust. (6) Deductions allowed as losses from the sale or exchange of property.

Since expenses incurred by an employee for travel, meals, and lodging while away from home or expenses for which he is reimbursed by his employer are deductible in computing adjusted gross income, it is obvious that any amounts received as reimbursement should be included in gross income. This is true

Ch. 5] 

DEDUCTIONS 

regardless of whether an employee receives a per diem allowance for expenses or is reimbursed for the expenses he incurs in behalf of his employer.

For taxable years beginning after December 31, 1943, adjusted gross income is the base for the limitation of the deduction. allowed for charitable contributions. Individuals are now allowed to deduct contributions not to exceed 15% of their adjusted gross income rather than 15% of their net income as provided under prior law. The effect of this change is to enlarge the amount of the tax benefit which may be received by an individual who makes large gifts to charity. 

For taxable years beginning after December 31, 1943, adjusted gross income is also made the basis for determining the amount of the deduction allowed for medical expenses. Individuals are now allowed to deduct medical expenses incurred in excess of 5% of their adjusted gross income rather than 5% of their net income as provided under prior law. The effect of this change is to decrease the amount of medical expenses which may be claimed as a deduction. 

Non-trade or Non-business Deductions. Under prior law non-trade or non-business expenses were not deductible although non-trade or non-business income was fully taxable. 

This inequity is now corrected by a change in the law which allows a deduction for the ordinary and necessary expenses of an individual paid or incurred during the taxable year for the production and collection of income or for the management, conservation, or maintenance of property held by the taxpayer for the production of income, whether or not such expenses are paid or incurred in carrying on a trade or business. 

A deduction is also allowed for depreciation (including a reasonable amount for obsolescence) of property held for the production of income, whether or not such property is used by the taxpayer in a trade or business. Expenses incurred in managing or conserving property held for investment may be deductible under this provision even though there is no likelihood that the property will be sold at a profit or will otherwise be productive of income, and even though the property is held merely to minimize a loss with respect thereto. The expenses, however, of carrying on a transaction which does not constitute a trade or business of the taxpayer and is not carried on for the production of income or for the management, conservation, or maintenance of property, but which is carried on primarily as a

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

sport, hobby, or recreation are not allowable as non-trade or non-business expenses.

Farmers. If the taxpayer is a farmer who keeps no books of account or who keeps his books on the cash basis, he must obtain from the collector and attach to his return Form 1040F, which is a schedule of farm income and expenses. If the farm books of account are kept on the accrual basis the filing of Form 1040F is optional.

Installment Method. Where the installment method of accounting for sales is used, there must be attached to the return a schedule showing separately for each year, including the current taxable year and the three preceding taxable years, the following information: (a) Gross sales (b) Cost of goods sold (c) Gross profits (d) Percentage of profits to gross sales (e) Amount collected (f) Gross profit on amount collected (For a detailed discussion of the installment method of accounting for sales of real and personal property, see Chapter Six.)

Rents and Royalties. Expenses chargeable against income derived from rents and royalties should be deducted in computing the net income as explained in Chapter Three. Such expenses may include insurance, depreciation, repairs, taxes, and all other ordinary and necessary expenses applicable to the property from which the income is derived. Thus if a taxpayer owned an apartment house all of which was rented, he would be required to report the entire rental income less such expenses as insurance premiums (fire, windstorm, plate glass, etc.); property taxes (not including assessments for improvements); interest on money borrowed with which to purchase the building or to make improvements, but not the cost of improvements otherwise; depreciation applicable to the building; and ordinary repairs, fuel, electrical energy, and wages paid to employees such as for elevator operator, gardener, etc.

Other Deductions. An individual is entitled to claim the following additional deductions from his gross income in the computation of his net income: 

(a) Contributions 

(b) Interest

Ch. 5] 101 

DEDUCTIONS 

(c) Taxes (d) Losses by fire, storm, etc. (e) Bad debts (f) Other deductions authorized by law. 

Contributions or Gifts. Contributions or gifts to a cor poration, trust, community chest, fund, or foundation which conform to the following requirements are deductible: (a) The recipient must have been created or organized in the United States or in any possession thereof or under the law of the United States or of any state or territory or of any possession of the United States. (b) It must be organized and operated exclusively for religious, charitable, scientific, literary, or educational purposes, or for the prevention of cruelty to children or animals. (c) No part of the net earnings must inure to the benefit of any private shareholder or individual.. (d) No substantial part of the activities of the organization must be devoted to carrying on propaganda, or otherwise attempting, to influence legislation. 

Contributions to the United States and other governmental units thereof are deductible if made for exclusively public purposes. Contributions to the special vocational rehabilitation fund and to organizations of war veterans or auxiliary organizations are also deductible. Contributions to domestic fraternal societies are deductible if made for exclusively religious, charitable, etc. purposes. "Contributions or gifts" means gifts of property or money. 

The value of personal services rendered is not an allowable deduction. Where the gift is in property rather than money, the fair market value at the time of the contribution is to be taken as the amount of the gift. The contributions are allowable deductions in the year in which they are actually paid, regardless of when pledged or of the accounting method employed by the taxpayer.

The deductibility of contributions is limited to 15% of the taxpayer's adjusted gross income. The limitation does not apply, however, if in the taxable year and each of the ten preceding taxable years the sum of the contributions and income. war-profits or excess-profits taxes paid exceeds 90% of the taxpayer's net income for each such year.

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

If the husband and wife file a joint return they are treated as one person in applying the 15% limitation, i.e. their aggregate contributions may not exceed 15% of their aggregate adjusted gross income. The same rule applies in determining their right to the unlimited deductibility of contributions. 

UNLIMITED DEDUCTION FOR Charitable and Other CONTRIBUTIONS.- 

In the case of an individual if in the taxable year and in each of the ten preceding taxable years the amount of the contributions or gifts described in section 23 (0) (or corresponding provisions of prior revenue Acts) plus the amount of income, war-profits, or excess-profits taxes paid during such year in respect of preceding taxable years, exceeds 90 per centum of the taxpayer's net income for each such year, as computed without the benefit of the applicable subsection, then the 15 per centum limit imposed by section 23 (0) shall not be applicable. 

(Section 120-I. R. C.) 

Apparently contributions to a charity fund to be distributed by a civic organization, such as a Rotary Club, or a Kiwanis Club, are not deductible, unless such club is incorporated as a religious, charitable, scientific, or educational organization exclusively. In one case it was held that contributions to a community club, the activities of which are civic and recreational, rather than strictly charitable, were not deductible. 

Interest. In addition to the interest claimed as a deduction from the income derived from a business or profession, or from rents and royalties, an individual is entitled to claim as a deduction from his gross income, interest paid or accrued within the taxable year on indebtedness, except indebtedness incurred or continued to purchase or carry obligations (other than obligations of the United States issued after September 24, 1917, and originally subscribed for by the taxpayer) the interest upon which is wholly exempt from the income tax. 

While interest generally is deductible whether it relates to business or personal indebtedness, it is to be noted that interest on money borrowed to purchase certain tax-exempt securities is not deductible. For instance, the interest on money borrowed to purchase municipal bonds, or obligations of the United States issued prior to September 24, 1917, the interest upon which is wholly exempt from tax, is not deductible. In the case of obligations of the United States issued after September 24, 1917, the interest upon which is not wholly exempt from all income taxes including both the normal tax and the surtax, interest on money borrowed to purchase such securities is deductible in accordance with the general rule.

It is interesting to note in a study of income taxation, that the treatment of interest is not consistent with the treatment of

Ch. 103 

DEDUCTIONS 

expenses. Under the law, a distinction is made between business expenses and personal, living, or family expenses. All ordinary and necessary business expenses are deductible, but personal, living, or family expenses are not deductible. All interest, except that relating to obligations incurred to purchase tax-exempt securities, is deductible; whether it applies to business indebtedness, or personal, or family indebtedness. For instance, interest paid on money borrowed with which to operate a business, or to carry on a profession, is deductible from the business or professional income.

Interest on money borrowed by the taxpayer with which to purchase an automobile for personal use, or to build a home for residential purposes, is also deductible. At one time the Commissioner of Internal Revenue ruled that the law referred only to interest paid on the indebtedness of the taxpayer incurred in the conduct of his business. This decision, however, was later overruled and at the present time interest on both personal indebtedness and business indebtedness can be deducted.

Under existing Federal tax laws, the taxpayer may claim as a deduction from gross income, interest paid or accrued for the following purposes relating to insurance: (1) Interest on indebtedness incurred to purchase life insurance or annuity contracts, except that interest paid or accrued on indebtedness incurred or continued to purchase a single premium life insurance or endowment contract is not deductible. (2) Interest on indebtedness incurred to carry life insurance or annuity contracts. (3) Interest on indebtedness arising from loans involving the pledging of life insurance or annuity contracts. 

It is immaterial as to whom the interest is paid. Funds may be borrowed from anyone for the purpose of purchasing or paying subsequent premiums on insurance or annuity contracts and such interest is wholly deductible. Money may be borrowed from insurance companies, banks, or anyone else, life insurance or annuity contracts being pledged as security, and such interest will be wholly deductible. No distinction is made between individuals and corporations as taxpayers in the laws which permit of the deduction of interest on indebtedness incurred in connection with life insurance or annuity contracts.

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

The loss resulting from inability to collect interest accrued on securities may be included as a deduction only when such interest has been included in the gross income. It is usually best to ignore it entirely for nothing is gained in including such interest in gross income and then claiming it as a deduction. 

Interest on capital of the taxpayer invested in the business is not deductible. Interest, like other expenses, may be reported on either the cash or accrual basis but, of course, the method employed must be the same for all expenses. However, under certain conditions, interest accrued on business indebtedness and not paid within the taxable year or within 22 months after the close thereof, may not be deductible. 

Taxes. Individuals may deduct taxes paid or accrued except (a) income taxes levied by the United States, (b) estate, inheritance, legacy, succession, and gift taxes, whether levied by a state or the United States, (c) Federal import duties and Federal excise and stamp taxes, and (d) assessments levied for local benefits. Examples of the latter kind are assessments paid by the property owner for the paving or extension of streets in a city, or by the landowner for irrigation or drainage in agricultural districts.

Taxes paid on property used as a residence may be treated as a deduction. It will be noted that though insurance on a residence and rent paid for a residence are not deductible, the taxes paid on a residence or interest paid on indebtedness, incurred in order to purchase a residence, are deductible.

Federal import duties, while not deductible as a tax, may, however, be added to the cost of merchandise purchased or may be made a part of the expenses of doing business. Postage is not considered a tax, hence cannot be deducted as such. However, postage paid in connection with the operation of a business constitutes a business expense which is deductible. 

License fees on automobiles levied under state laws are deductible because this is a tax levied against the owner of the automobile and may be deducted by him. 

Taxes are deemed to be paid by or accrued to those persons on whom they are imposed. These persons are entitled to claim such taxes as a deduction from gross income in computing the tax.

In addition to certain Federal taxes there are numerous taxes imposed by the states and their subdivisions, which may be deducted from gross income in the Federal tax return.

Ch 105 

DEDUCTIONS 

Taxes applicable to a business or profession should be deducted from the income derived from the business or profession. 

Taxes chargeable against rents and royalties should be deducted from the income from rents and royalties. All other taxes which are deductible should be deducted from the gross income of the taxpayer.

The payroll taxes imposed on employers under the Federal Social Security Act and the contributions required of employers under the state unemployment compensation acts, represent excise taxes which are deductible from gross income in Federal income tax returns. However, the taxes imposed on employees under the Federal Social Security Act for the purposes of oldage benefits or insurance are not deductible.

Retail Sales Tax. Under prior law state and local retail sales taxes actually imposed on the consumer were deductible, but in many states such taxes were imposed on the dealer rather than the consumer, hence could not be deducted by the consumer even though actually paid by him. This inequity is now corrected by permitting the deduction to the purchaser of state and local retail sales taxes paid by such purchaser even though not imposed by law directly on the purchaser. To be deductible such taxes must be stated separately rather than merely be included in the price of the article or service purchased. It is not necessary, however, that a purchaser be furnished with a bill or invoice upon which the tax is listed separately. All that is required is that it clearly appear that the tax was paid as such and not merely as a component of the price of the article or service purchased. This will have the effect of treating retail sales taxes more uniformly in all states. 

Losses. Under the law, losses are classified as follows: (a) Losses incurred in trade or business (b) Losses incurred in transactions entered into for profit but not connected with trade or business (c) Losses incurred through casualty or theft.

Losses of the first class have already been discussed. Such losses are deductible from business or professional income. 

Losses incurred through casualty or theft affecting business property are also deductible from business or professional income. 

Losses incurred through casualty or theft of property not connected with trade, business, or profession may be deducted from gross income in arriving at net income. For instance, if an individual suffers a loss to his residence because of a storm or a fire,

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

if not compensated for by insurance, such loss though a personal loss, may be deducted from gross income in the computation of net income.

Losses incurred in transactions entered into for profit but not connected with trade or business, are deductible from gross income. Such a loss may arise from the sale of a dwelling which an individual may construct or purchase with a view to sale at a profit. In the case of a loss from the sale of residential property constructed or purchased by a taxpayer for use as his personal residence and so used by him up to the time of the sale, such loss is not deductible. The point is that losses of a personal nature are deductible only when they relate to transactions entered into for profit. When one constructs or purchases a residence for his own use, it does not constitute a transaction entered into for profit, but when one constructs or purchases a residence to rent or which he hopes to sell at a profit, it is a transaction entered into for profit and, in the latter case, should a loss result from the sale of the property, it is a deductible loss. 

Losses must usually be evidenced by closed and completed transactions and must not be compensated for by insurance or otherwise. The loss must be sustained during the taxable year in which it is claimed as a deduction. Proper adjustment must be made in each case for any expenditure, receipt, loss, or other item properly chargeable to capital accounts, and for depreciation, obsolescence, amortization, or depletion. The loss must be reduced by the amount of any insurance or other compensation received and by the salvage value, if any, of the property. A loss occasioned by damage to an automobile maintained for pleasure, where such damage results from the faulty driving of the taxpayer or other person operating the automobile, but is not due to willful act or willful negligence of the taxpayer, is a deductible loss. Where damage to a taxpayer's automobile results from the faulty driving of the operator of another automobile with which the automobile of the taxpayer collides, the loss occasioned to the taxpayer by such damage is likewise a deductible loss. 

Wagering losses are deductible only to the extent of similar gains. Gains from wagering transactions are wholly taxable, but the deduction of wagering losses is limited to the extent of wagering gains.

Losses from illegal transactions are generally not allowable as deductions from gross income.

Ch. 5] 107 

DEDUCTIONS 

Bad Debts. The deductibility of bad debts has been discussed previously. Losses on account of bad debts incurred in connection with a trade, business, or profession, should be de ducted from business or professional income. Other deductible losses from bad debts incurred outside of the trade, business, or profession, may be deductible from gross income. For taxable years beginning after December 31, 1942, however, they are to be treated as short-term capital losses and are to be subject to the limitations applicable to the deductibility of capital losses. 

Bad debts representing income earned but not collected are not allowed as a deduction unless the income has been included in the return for the year in which the deduction as a bad debt is sought to be made, or in a previous year. Illustrations of the deductible bad debts arising from personal transactions not related to a trade, business, or profession, include the following: (a) Personal loans to relatives and friends which prove to be worthless. (b) Loss to depositors resulting from the failure of a bank. 

Following are some important factors to be considered in determining the deductibility of losses arising from bad debts: (1) The debt must represent a legal obligation. The voluntary assumption by the taxpayer of a debt of another person is not a deductible loss but is a gift. (2) Worthlessness of the bad debt must be established in the taxable year in which it is claimed as a deduction. A creditor may not elect the year in which he shall claim the deduction of a bad debt if worthlessness is ascertained in a given year. (3) The debt must not be evidenced by securities such as bonds, debentures, notes, or certificates, or other evidences of indebtedness, issued by any corporation, with interest coupons or in registered form. Such losses are to be claimed as losses arising from worthless securities and come under the capital loss classification rather than as a loss from bad debts. In the case of debts of individuals, the capital loss limitation does not apply. This is also true in the case of debts of corporations which are not evidenced by notes, bonds, or other securities. (4) In the case of non-business debts of an individual, the loss resulting from their becoming entirely worthless in the taxable year is to be considered a loss from the sale or exchange of a capital asset held for not more than six months. This has the effect of treating such bad debts in the same manner as short-term capital losses. There is a similar change in the treat-

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

ment of securities that become worthless. Such securities are deductible in the taxable year in which they become worthless regardless of when worthlessness is ascertained and regardless of when they are charged off. It is not always a simple matter to establish the worthlessness of bad debts for the purposes of claiming deductibility. The Commissioner has specifically stated that worthlessness is evidenced when (1) legal action to enforce payment would result in an uncollectible judgment, or (2) upon a settlement in bankruptcy, although worthlessness may sometimes be determined after bankruptcy and before settlement. 

Medical and Dental Expenses. A deduction may be made for expenses paid during the taxable year for the medical care of the taxpayer, his spouse, or a dependent of the taxpayer. The deduction is limited to such expenses as exceed 5% of the taxpayer's adjusted gross income. If only one surtax exemption is allowed the taxpayer, the maximum deduction for the taxable year is $1,250. If more than one surtax exemption is allowed, the maximum deduction is $2,500. The term medical care is broadly defined to include amounts paid for the diagnosis, cure, mitigation, treatment, or prevention of disease, or for the purpose of affecting any structure or function of the body. While such expenses as are compensated for by insurance or otherwise may not be deducted, amounts paid for accident or health insurance may be included in the deduction. Apparently, also payments for hospitalization insurance or for membership in an association furnishing co-operative medical service or group hospitalization and clinical care may be included in the deduction. 

Alimony Payments. Amounts paid by a husband to his divorced wife as alimony may be treated as a deduction from his gross income. However, no deduction may be made for any portion of such payments which is designated as support for a minor child of the husband.

Bond Premium Amortization. The amount of any pre mium paid for bonds may be amortized over the life of the bonds. 

The amount of the bond premium to be amortized each year may be determined either (1) by a method of amortization which is regularly employed by the taxpayer and which is reasonable or (2) by a method prescribed by the Commissioner under authorized regulations. Under prior law it was necessary to treat any premium paid for bonds as capital loss sustained by the owner of the bond at the time of the disposition or maturity of the bond.

Ch. 109 

DEDUCTIONS 

Net Operating Loss Deduction. A net operating loss for any taxable year beginning after December 31, 1941, may be carried back to the two preceding taxable years (but not to any year beginning before January 1, 1941) and may be included in computing the net operating loss deduction for such preceding taxable year. Any portion of the net operating loss which is not used as a carry-back may be carried forward to the two succeeding taxable years. There are limitations on the amount of the loss which may be carried back or forward. The intent of these limitations is to prevent the deduction of losses which are absorbed by untaxed income. 

Computation of Net Operating Loss. The net operating loss is the excess of allowable deductions over statutory gross income in any taxable year, computed with the following exceptions and limitations: (a) The deduction for depletion shall not exceed the amount allowable if computed without reference to discovery value or percentage depletion. (b) Wholly exempt interest must be included in gross income, decreased by the amount of interest paid which is not deductible because related to obligations incurred or continued in order to purchase or carry tax exempt securities. (c) The net operating loss of a preceding or a succeeding year cannot be included as a deduction in order to create a net operating loss for the current year. (d) 100% of all gains and losses from sales or exchanges of capital assets must be taken into account. Capital losses may be deducted only to the extent of capital gains. (e) Nonbusiness deductions are allowed only to the extent of nonbusiness gross income. 

The operation of the foregoing limitations and exceptions may be illustrated by the following example: A, an individual reporting on the calendar year basis, has gross income of $200,000.00 and deductions (exclusive of a net operating loss deduction) of $300,000.00 for 1944. Included in gross income are business long-term capital gains of $20,000.00 on capital assets held for more than 6 months (actual gains $30,000.00) and nonbusiness income of $4,000.00. A also received $3,500.00 interest on municipal bonds which was excluded from gross income. 

Interest was paid in the amount of $1,200.00 on obligations incurred to carry these bonds.

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

Included among the deductions are a business long-term capital loss of $25,000.00 on assets held more than 6 months (actual loss $50,000.00); percentage depletion in the amount of $22,000.00 (depletion based on cost, $14,000.00); nonbusiness fire loss of $7,000.00; and other nonbusiness deductions amounting to $1,500.00. A has no other items of gross income or deductions to which the limitations are applicable. 

On the basis of these facts, A's net operating loss deduction may be computed as follows: 

(1) Deduction for 1944, Exclusive of Long-term Capital 

Loss ($300,000.00 minus $25,000.00)... (2) Plus: Actual Capital Loss ($50,000.00) not to Exceed 

Actual Capital Gains ($30,000.00).. (3) Sum of (1) and (2). (4) Less: Excess of Percentage Depletion over 

Depletion Based on Cost ($22,000.00 minus $14,000.00)... 

(5) Less: Excess of Nonbusiness Deductions over Nonbusiness Income ($7,000.00 plus $1,500.00 minus $4,000.00). (6) Sum of (4) and (5)…………. $275,000.00 30,000.00 $305,000.00 $8,000.00 4,500.00 12,500.00 (7) Deductions as Adjusted (item (3) minus item (6)).... $292,500.00 (8) Gross Income for 1944, Exclusive of Long-term Capital 

Gains ($200,000.00 minus $20,000.00)... 

(9) Plus: Actual Capital Gains.. 

(10) Plus: Excess of Tax Exempt Interest Received Over 

Interest Paid ($3,500.00 minus $1,200.00).. $180,000.00 30,000.00 2,300.00 (11) Gross Income as Adjusted (sum of items (8), (9) & (10)) $212,300.00 (12) Net Operating Loss for 1944 (item (7) minus item (11)) $ 80,200.00

Computation of Net Operating Loss Deduction. The net operating loss deduction for any taxable year is the sum of the carry-backs and the carry-overs to such taxable year. In the foregoing example A had a net operating loss of $80,200.00 for the taxable year 1944. This may be carried back to the two preceding taxable years and be claimed as a deduction from the net income of those years. To secure the benefits of the carry back the taxpayer should then file a claim for refund of the excess taxes already paid. If the net operating loss for 1944 is not carried back and used as a deduction in the two preceding taxable years, it may be carried forward to the two succeeding taxable years. The net operating loss deduction for any taxable year is the sum of the carry-backs and carry-overs to such taxable year. The following examples were used in the Senate 

Finance Committee Report to illustrate the benefits to be derived from the net operating loss deduction provisions:

Ch. 111 

DEDUCTIONS 

1. The taxpayer has $600.00 net income for 1941 and $900.00 net income for 1942. In 1943 he has a net operating loss of $1,000.00. The $1,000.00 may be taken as a net operating loss deduction in 1941, and will therefore reduce the net income for 1941 to zero. In 1942 the net operating loss may be taken as a deduction to the extent of $400.00 (the net operating loss of $1,000.00 less the net income of $600.00 for 1941 against which the net operating loss has been applied). The net income for 

1942 is therefore reduced to $500.00. The net operating loss for 

1943 has been completely offset by the net income for 1941 ($600.00) and 1942 ($900.00), and therefore no part of such net operating loss may be taken as a deduction in 1944 or 1945. 

2. In 1944 the taxpayer has a net operating loss of $1,600.00.

This net operating loss may be taken as a deduction in 1942 and will reduce the remaining $500.00 of net income for 1942 to zero. Since the net income for 1943 was zero (there being a loss for such year), none of the net operating loss for 1944 is used to offset net income for 1943. In 1945, the taxpayer has $300.00 net income, and in 1946 $1,400.00 net income. The net operating loss of $1,600.00 for 1944 has been offset to the extent of $500.00 by the net income for 1942 (the $900.00 net income for 1942, less the $400.00 carry-back from 1943), and may therefore be taken as a deduction in 1945 to the extent of $1,100.00 ($1,600.00 less $500.00). It will therefore reduce the net income for 1945 to zero. Since $300.00 of the $1,100.00 deduction is used to offset the $300.00 net income for 1945, the remaining $800.00 may be taken as a deduction against the $1,400.00 net income for 1946. It will therefore reduce the net income for 1946 to $600.00. 

3. In 1947 the taxpayer has a net operating loss of $1,500.00. 

This net operating loss may be taken as a deduction in 1945. 

Since the net income for 1945 has been completely offset by the net operating losses for years prior to 1947 (the $300.00 net income for such year less the $1,100.00 carry-over from 1944), the net operating loss is not offset by any net income for 1945, and may be taken in full as a deduction in 1946. It will thus offset the remaining $600.00 net income for 1946 (the $1,400.00 net income for 1946 less the $800.00 carry-over from 1944) and will reduce such net income to zero. The remaining $900.00 of the net operating loss (the $1,500.00 net operating loss less the sum of zero, the $300.00 net income for 1945 reduced by the $1,100 carry-over to 1945 from 1944, and $600.00, the $1,400.00 net income for 1946 reduced by the $800.00 carry-over to 1946

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

from 1944) may be taken as a deduction in 1948 and 1949 in the same manner as the net operating loss for 1944 was taken as a deduction in 1945 and 1946. 

Every taxpayer claiming a net operating loss deduction must file with his return a statement setting forth the amount of the deduction claimed and all pertinent facts, together with a schedule showing the computations of the net operating loss deduction. 

The net operating loss deduction is allowed individuals engaged in trade or business; estates and trusts, if engaged in trade or business; participants in common trust funds; members of partnerships; insurance companies and corporations. 

Optional Standard Deduction. Certain individual tax payers may elect to take a standard deduction in lieu of certain deductions and credits. If a taxpayer has an adjusted gross income of $5,000 or more, the standard deduction is $500. If a taxpayer's adjusted gross income is less than $5,000, the standard deduction is 10% of the adjusted gross income. The standard deduction in the latter case is available only through the use of the optional tax table which is so constructed that it automatically allows the taxpayer a standard deduction of 10% of his ad usted gross income.

The standard deduction, if elected by the taxpayer, is taken in lieu of all deductions other than those which may be subtracted from gross income in computing adjusted gross income. 

When the standard deduction is elected no credit may be claimed for taxes paid to foreign countries or to possessions of the United States, or for taxes withheld at the source on tax-free covenant bonds, or for credits against net income in respect to interest on tax exempt government bonds.

The taxpayer may avail himself of the standard deduction for the taxable year only if he so elects and such an election is irrevocable for such year. If the taxpayer's adjusted gross income is less than $5,000, the standard deduction is allowed only if he elects to pay the tax imposed by the optional tax table. If the taxpayer's adjusted gross income is $5,000 or more, the standard deduction is allowed only if he signifies in his return his election to take such standard deductions in lieu of his actual deductions. 

In the case of a husband and wife living together, if the net income of one spouse is determined without regard to the standard deduction, the other spouse is not permitted to elect the standard deduction. For example, if a husband, whose adjusted

Ch. 5] 

113 

DEDUCTIONS 

gross income is $6,000, does not elect to take the standard deduction of $500, his wife with an adjusted gross income of $3,500 is precluded from using the optional tax table in ascertaining the amount of her tax.

The standard deduction is not permitted where a separate return is made for a period of less than 12 months because of a change in the accounting period of the taxpayer..

Items Not Deductible. (a) GENERAL RULE.-In computing net in come no deduction shall in any case be allowed in respect of— (1) Personal, living, or family expenses, except extraordinary medical expenses deductible under section 23 (x); (2) Any amount paid out for new buildings or for permanent improvements or betterments made to increase the value of any property or estate; (3) Any amount expended in restoring property or in making good the exhaustion thereof for which an allowance is or has been made; (4) Premiums paid on any life insurance policy covering the life of any officer or employee, or of any person financially interested in any trade or business carried on by the taxpayer, when the taxpayer is directly or indirectly a beneficiary under such policy; (5) Any amount otherwise allowable as a deduction which is allocable to one or more classes of income other than interest (whether or not any amount of income of that class or classes is received or accrued) wholly exempt from the taxes imposed by this chapter, or any amount otherwise allowable under section 23 (a) (2) which is allocable to interest (whether or not any amount of such interest is received or accrued) wholly exempt from the taxes imposed by this chapter; (6) Any amount paid or accrued on indebtedness incurred or continued to purchase a single premium life insurance or endowment contract. For the purposes of this paragraph, if substantially all the premiums on a life insurance or endowment contract are paid within a period of four years from the date on which such contract is purchased, such contract shall be considered a single premium life insurance or endowment contract; or (7) Amounts paid or accrued for such taxes and carrying charges as, under regulations prescribed by the Commissioner with the approval of the Secretary, are chargeable to capital account with respect to property, if the taxpayer elects, in accordance with such regulations, to treat such taxes or charges as so chargeable. 

(b) Losses From Sales or EXCHANGES OF Property.- 

(1) LOSSES DISALLOWED.-In computing net income no deduction shall in any case be allowed in respect of losses from sales or exchanges of property, 

(A) Between members of a family, as defined in paragraph (2) (D); (B) Except in the case of distributions in liquidation, between an individual and a corporation more than 50 per centum in value of the outstanding stock of which is owned, directly or indirectly, by or for such individual; (C) Except in the case of distributions in liquidation, between two corporations more than 50 per centum in value of the outstanding stock of each of which is owned, directly or indirectly, by or for the same individual, if either one of such corporations, with respect to the taxable year of the corporation preceding the date of the sale or exchange was, under the law applicable to such taxable year, a personal holding company or a foreign personal holding company; (D) Between a grantor and a fiduciary of any trust;

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

(E) Between the fiduciary of a trust and the fiduciary of another trust, if the same person is a grantor with respect to each trust; or (F) Between the fiduciary of a trust and a beneficiary of such trust. 

(2) STOCK OWNERSHIP, FAMILY, AND PARTNERSHIP RULE.-For the purposes of determining, in applying paragraph (1), the ownership of stock- (A) Stock owned, directly or indirectly, by or for a corporation, partnership, estate, or trust, shall be considered as being owned proportionately by or for its shareholders, partners, or beneficiaries; (B) An individual shall be considered as owning the stock owned, directly or indirectly, by or for his family; (C) An individual owning (otherwise than by the application of subparagraph (B)) any stock in a corporation shall be considered as owning the stock owned, directly or indirectly, by or for his partner; (D) The family of an individual shall include only his brothers and sisters (whether by the whole or half blood), spouse, ancestors, and lineal descendants; and 

(E) Constructive Ownership as Actual Ownership.-Stock constructively owned by a person by reason of the application of subparagraph (A) shall, for the purpose of applying subparagraph (A), (B), or (C), be treated as actually owned by such person, but stock constructively owned by an individual by reason of the application of subparagraph (B) or (C) shall not be treated as owned by him for the purpose of again applying either of such subparagraphs in order to make another the constructive owner of such stock. (c) UNPAID EXPENSES AND INTEREST.-In computing net income no deduction shall be allowed under section 23(a), relating to expenses incurred, or under section 23(b), relating to interest accrued- (1) If such expenses or interest are not paid within the taxable year or within two and one-half months after the close thereof; and (2) If, by reason of the method of accounting of the person to whom the payment is to be made, the amount thereof is not, unless paid, includible in the gross income of such person for the taxable year in which or with which the taxable year of the taxpayer ends; and (3) If, at the close of the taxable year of the taxpayer or at any time within two and one-half months thereafter, both the taxpayer and the person to whom the payment is to be made are persons between whom losses would be disallowed under section 24 (b). 

(d) HOLDERS OF LIFE OR TERMINABLE INTEREST.-Amounts paid under the laws of any State, Territory, District of Columbia, possession of the United States, or foreign country as income to the holder of a life or terminable interest acquired by gift, bequest, or inheritance shall not be reduced or diminished by any deduction for shrinkage (by whatever name called) in the value of such interest due to the lapse of time, nor by any deduction allowed by this chapter (except the deductions provided for in subsections (1) and (m) of section 23) for the purpose of computing the net income of an estate or trust but not allowed under the laws of such State, Territory, District of Columbia, possession of the United States, or foreign country for the purpose of computing the income to which such holder is entitled. 

(Section 24-I. R. C.) 

Interest. Any amount paid or accrued on an indebtedness incurred or continued to purchase a single premium life insurance or endowment contract may not be treated as an allowable deduction. Any contract shall be considered a single premium life insurance or endowment contract if substantially all the premiums on such contract are paid within a period of four

Ch. 5] 115 

DEDUCTIONS 

years from the date on which the contract is purchased. This provision is somewhat similar to the provision in prior law which disallows a deduction for interest paid or accrued on an indebtedness incurred or continued to purchase or carry tax exempt securities.

Losses. For many years, it has been the policy of the Treasury Department to disallow deduction of losses resulting from fictitious sales to members of the same family or to close business associates. The Revenue Act of 1934 contained a provision upholding the action of the Treasury Department by specifically disallowing deduction of losses arising from sales or exchanges of property directly or indirectly between members of a family or between a corporation and a stockholder where the stockholder, including his family, owned more than 50 per cent in value of the outstanding stock. Beginning with the 1938 Act, losses have been disallowed on sales or exchanges between (a) two corporations if more than 50 per cent in value of the outstanding stock is owned, directly or indirectly by or for the same individual, and if either one of the corporations was a personal holding company for the preceding taxable year; (b) between an individual and a corporation, if the individual and his partner together own more than 50 per cent in value of the outstanding stock; (c) between the grantor and the fiduciary of a trust; (d) between fiduciaries of trusts created by the same grantor; and (e) between a fiduciary and any beneficiary of a trust. 

For the purpose of determining stock ownership in a corporation, the following rules apply: (1) Stock held by a corporation, partnership, estate, or trust is considered to be owned proportionately by its shareholders, partners, or beneficiaries; and (2) Stock held by an individual's partner is considered to be owned by such individual if such individual also owns stock in the corporation.

Another limiting provision of the Code which dates back to the 1938 Act disallows deductions for unpaid expenses and interest of a debtor where the creditor, by reason of his method of accounting, is not required to include in his gross income the amount of the expense or interest until received. However, if the debtor pays the expense or interest within two and onehalf months after the close of such taxable year, he may deduct it in such taxable year. The purpose of this provision is to prevent the deduction of artificially created expenses.

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS The operation of the provision may be illustrated as follows: 

A corporation whose stock is closely held, reporting on the accrual basis, allows salaries to officer stockholders which are in excess of the amounts paid to such officers during the taxable year. Officers report on the cash basis. Portions of such salaries which are not paid within two and one-half months after the close of the corporation's taxable year are not allowed as deductions for that year.

Premiums paid on life insurance contracts of all kinds do not constitute an allowable deduction from gross income in Federal income tax returns when such premiums constitute personal, living, or family expenses. Individuals paying premiums on life insurance contracts based on their own lives or the lives of members of their families cannot claim the premiums paid as an allowable deduction. 

Premiums paid by a taxpayer on an insurance policy on the life of an officer, employee, or other individual financially interested in the taxpayer's business, for the purpose of protecting the taxpayer from loss in the event of the death of the insured are not deductible from the taxpayer's gross income. 

When an employer can show that the premiums paid on life insurance of an officer or employee are (1) in the nature of additional compensation, (2) that the total compensation of the employee is reasonable, and (3) that the employer is not directly or indirectly a beneficiary under the policy, such premiums may be claimed as an allowable deduction on the ground that such premiums represent additional compensation and are an ordinary and necessary business expense. When such premiums are allowable as a deduction in the employer's return, the amount of the premium paid represents taxable income to the employee and must be reported as taxable income. On the other hand, if the premiums paid by an employer are not deductible, they do not constitute taxable income to the employee. 

Premiums paid by an employer on group life insurance covering the lives of employees, the beneficiaries of which are designated by the employees, are not income to the employees. 

However, such premiums are deductible by the employer, but such portion of the premium as may be paid by the employees is not an allowable deduction, for the reason that it represents a personal expense.

Deductions Allowed Nonresident Alien Individuals. (a) GENERAL 

RULE. In the case of a nonresident alien individual the deductions shall be allowed only if and to the extent that they are connected with income from

Ch. 117 

DEDUCTIONS 

sources within the United States; and the proper apportionment and allocation of the deductions with respect to sources of income within and without the United States shall be determined as provided in section 119, under rules and regulations prescribed by the Commissioner with the approval of the Secretary. 

(b) LOSSES.- 

(1) The deduction, for losses not connected with the trade or business if incurred in transactions entered into for profit, allowed by section 23 (e) (2) shall be allowed whether or not connected with income from sources within the United States, but only if the profit, if such transaction had resulted in a profit, would be taxable under this chapter. (2) The deduction for losses of property not connected with the trade or business if arising from certain casualties or theft, allowed by section 23 (e) (3), shall be allowed whether or not connected with income from sources within the United States, but only if the loss is of property within the United States. 

(c) CHARITABLE, ETC., CONTRIBUTIONS.-The so-called "charitable contribution" deduction allowed by section 23 (0) shall be allowed whether or not connected with income from sources within the United States, but only as to contributions or gifts made to domestic corporations, or to com. munity chests, funds, or foundations, created in the United States, or to the vocational rehabilitation fund. 

(d) STANDARD DEDUCTION.-The standard deduction provided in section 

23 (aa) shall not be allowed.

(Section 213-I. R. C.) 

In general, nonresident alien individuals are entitled to the same deductions as citizens of the United States provided, however, that the deductions shall be apportioned and allocated to the income derived from sources both within and without the United States. Losses not connected with the trade or business but arising from transactions entered into for profit are deductible only in case the profit, had the transaction resulted in a profit, would be taxable under the law. 

ILLUSTRATIVE CASES 

Case 5-1. Robert Klein buys ten shares of the capital stock of a corporation which was organized for the purpose of operating a moving picture theater. The assets of the company consist of a lease for two years of premises where the moving picture theater is operated. He pays $900.00 for his stock. During the first two years the earnings of the company average $100.00 per month, but at the end of that time when the lease expired, the stock became worthless owing to the fact that the company was unable to secure an extension of the lease or to secure other suitable quarters. It was, therefore, decided to discontinue the business and dissolve the company. Mr. Klein received nothing for his stock. What amount is he entitled to claim as a deduction on account of this stock becoming worthless?

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

Solution. The law provides that a loss incurred in any transaction entered into for profit, though not connected with the trade or business, shall be allowed as a deduction. There is no question that the purchase of the stock by Mr. Klein represented a transaction entered into for profit. Therefore, the loss resulting from the stock becoming worthless, is deductible. 

Assuming that the stock was purchased since February 28, 1913, the cost represents the basis for computing the amount of the loss.

A loss from worthless securities is a capital loss. For the purpose of determining the period held the loss is considered to have occurred on the last day of the taxable year in which the stock became worthless. The loss, of course, is subject to the capital loss limitations.

Case 5-2. Howard Nolan has a gross income of $5,800.00 derived from salary and commissions. His allowable deductions amount to $1,400.00 not including a contribution of $850.00 to the Community Chest. Compute his net taxable income. 

Solution. Assuming that Mr. Nolan had no business deductions his adjusted gross income would be the same as his gross income and he would be entitled to claim a deduction for contributions not exceeding 15% of his adjusted gross income, or $870. Since his actual contributions amounted to only $850, the entire amount may be claimed as a deduction. His net income may, therefore, be computed as follows: 

Gross income..

Allowable deductions. 

Net income..... $5,800.00 2,250.00 $3,550.00

Mr. Nolan would not be entitled to the unlimited deduction of contributions unless, in the current year and in each of the preceding ten years, his contributions and income taxes paid were equal to 90% of his net income. 

Case 5-3. Mr. A. W. Watson is regularly engaged in the business of buying oil leases and in the producing and marketing of oil. During the current taxable year he has a net business income of $14,000.00 before making any deduction on account of losses amounting to $5,000.00 arising from drilling dry holes. He also receives dividends from domestic oil corporations amounting to $10,000.00. What amount of net income should he report for taxation?

Ch. 

119 

DEDUCTIONS Solution. 

Business income... 

Less business losses.

Dividends from domestic corporations. 

Total net income…… 

$14,000.00 5,000.00 $9,000.00 10,000.00 $19,000.00

5-9. A corporation contracts with an insurance company for a group policy on the one-year renewal term plan with premiums payable annually to insure the lives of the employees. Under the terms of the contract, the employer and employees contribute jointly toward the payment of the premiums, the employees contributing not more than 60 cents per month for each $1,000.00 of insurance. In addition to providing for death benefits, the contract provides for disability benefits in event of total and permanent disability before age 60. It is also provided that annually during the continuance of the contract the proportion of the divisible surplus accruing on the contract shall be ascertained and apportioned by the Board of Directors and credited to the contract at the end of the policy year as a dividend. Such dividend shall be (1) paid to the employer in cash, or (2) at the option of the employer applied to the reduction of the premium then due, if any; or upon written request of the employer it may be (3) left to accumulate at the rate of 32% plus such additional interest as the company may declare on such funds and withdrawable at any time.

The premium paid during the current taxable year amounted to $2,910.00, of which 60 cents per $1,000.00 of insurance was paid by the employees and approximately 20 cents per $1,000.00 of insurance was paid by the employer. (a) May the portion of the premium paid by the employer on this contract be claimed as a deduction in the Federal income tax return for the taxable year? (b) May the portion of the premium paid by employees on this contract be claimed as an allowable deduction in their individual returns for the taxable year? (c) One of the employees of the company became totally and permanently disabled, and the insurance company, in addition to waiving the premiums, agreed to pay the amount of insurance on the life of such person in sixty monthly installments each installment amounting to $36.30. Does the receipt of such disability benefit constitute taxable income to the employee? (d) One of the employees who participated in the group contract to the extent of $3,000.00 designated his wife as the beneficiary. After paying two annual premiums at the rate of 

60 cents per month for each $1,000.00 of insurance, the insured

[Ch. 5 

DEDUCTIONS 

died suddenly and the insurance company paid the beneficiary $3,000.00. Would the beneficiary realize any taxable income from this transaction? (e) If the insured corporation elected to receive dividends declared on the contract in cash, would such dividends constitute taxable income?
