6-14. C. C. Calhoun constructed a factory building for his own use at a cost of $200,000. After charging off depreciation at the rate of 22% a year for four years, the building was completely destroyed by fire. At the time of the fire the building was insured for $190,000 and the insurance company settled by paying the full amount of insurance. The money received from the insurance company was immediately invested in the construction of a new factory building similar to the old building. 

After charging off depreciation on the new building at the rate of 22% annually for six years, the building was sold in the current year for $145,000. (a) Compute the amount of any gain realized or loss sustained in the year the old building was destroyed by fire. (b) What amount of capital gain or loss resulted from the sale of the new building in the current year? 

Note: Section 112 (f) of the Code (see page 125) provides that no gain shall be recognized if property, as a result of its destruction in whole or in part, is compulsorily or involuntarily converted into money which is forthwith, in good faith, expended in the acquisition of other property similar to the property so converted. Since no gain was recognized in the year of fire, the basis for computing any gain realized or loss sustained in the year of sale is the original cost of the old building adjusted for the total amount of depreciation charged off during the ten-year period.

CHAPTER SEVEN COMPUTATION OF INDIVIDUAL TAXES 1. Credits Against Net Income 

(a) For normal tax only (b) For surtax only 

2. The Federal Income Tax 

(a) Normal tax 

(b) Surtax 3. Credits Against Tax 

4. Nonresident Aliens 

5. Optional Tax 

In computing the income taxes imposed on individuals it is necessary to make separate computations of the normal tax and the surtax. Certain credits may be deducted from the net income in arriving at the basis for the computations of the normal tax and the surtax. In lieu of using the percentage rate method in computing the tax certain taxpayers are given an option of using a table in ascertaining their total tax liability. 

Credits of Individual Against Net Income. (a) CREDITS FOR NOR- 

MAL TAX ONLY.-There shall be allowed for the purpose of the normal tax, but not for the surtax, the following credits against the net income: 

(1) INTEREST ON UNITED STATES OBLIGATIONS.-The amount received as interest upon obligations of the United States, if such interest is included in gross income under section 22, and if, under the Act authorizing the issue of such obligations, as amended and supplemented, such interest is exempt from normal tax. 

(2) INTEREST ON OBLIGATIONS OF INSTRUMENTALITIES OF THE UNITED 

STATES. The amount received as interest on obligations of a corporation organized under Act of Congress, if (A) such corporation is an instrumentality of the United States; and (B) such interest is included in gross income under section 22; and (C) under the Act authorizing the issue thereof, as amended and supplemented, such interest is exempt from normal tax. (For reduction of credit under paragraph (1) or (2) on account of amortizable bond premium, see section 125). 

(3) NORMAL-TAX EXEMPTION. A normal-tax exemption of $500. In the case of a joint return by husband and wife under section 51, the normaltax exemption shall be $1,000, except that if the adjusted gross income of one spouse is less than $500, the normal-tax exemption shall be $500 plus the adjusted gross income of such spouse. 

(b) CREDITS FOR SURTAX ONLY.- 

(1) CREDITS.-There shall be allowed for the purpose of the surtax, but not for the normal tax, the following credits against net income: (A) A surtax exemption of $500 for the taxpayer; (B) A surtax exemption of $500 for the spouse of the taxpayer if— (i) a joint return is made by the taxpayer and his spouse under section 51, in which case the surtax exemption of the spouses under subparagraph (A) and this subparagraph shall be only $1,000 in the aggregate, or 159

[Ch. 7 

(ii) a separate return is made by the taxpayer, and his spouse has no gross income for the calendar year in which the taxable year of the taxpayer begins and is not the dependent of another taxpayer; (C) A surtax exemption of $500 for each dependent whose gross income for the calendar year in which the taxable year of the taxpayer begins is less than $500, except that if such dependent is married the exemption in respect of such dependent shall not be allowed if such dependent has made a joint return with the other spouse under section 51 for a taxable year beginning in such calendar year. 

(2) DETERMINATION OF STATUS.-The determination of whether an in dividual is married shall be made as of the last day of the taxable year, unless his spouse dies during the taxable year, in which case such determination shall be made as of the date of his spouse's death. 

(3) DEFINITION OF DEPENDENT.-As used in this chapter the term 

"dependent" means any of the following persons over half of whose support, for the calendar year in which the taxable year of the taxpayer begins, was received from the taxpayer: (A) a son or daughter of the taxpayer, or a descendant of either, (B) a stepson or stepdaughter of the taxpayer, (C) a brother, sister, stepbrother, or stepsister of the taxpayer, (D) the father or mother of the taxpayer, or an ancestor of either, (E) a stepfather or stepmother of the taxpayer, (F) a son or daughter of a brother or sister of the taxpayer, (G) a brother or sister of the father or mother of the taxpayer, (H) a son-in-law, daughter-in-law, father-in-law, mother-in-law, brotherin-law, or sister-in-law of the taxpayer. 

As used in this paragraph, the terms "brother" and "sister" include a brother or sister by the half-blood. For the purposes of determining whether any of the foregoing relationships exist, a legally adopted child of a person shall bẹ considered a child of such person by blood. The term "dependent" does not include any individual who is a citizen or subject of a foreign country unless such individual is a resident of the United States or of a country contiguous to the United States. A payment to a wife which is includible under section 

22 (k) or section 171 in the gross income of such wife shall not be considered a payment by her husband for the support of any dependent. 

(Section 25-I. R. C.) 

Although net income is in general the basis for computing the income tax, it will be observed that there are two specific bases against which the rates are applied; the normal tax rate is applied to one base and the surtax rates are applied to another base. The procedure may be set forth as follows: 

(a) Net income. 

Less normal tax credits.

Normal tax net income. . . . (b) Net income..... 

Less surtax credits.

Surtax net income.. 

XXX.XX XX. XX XXX.XX XXX.XX XX. XX XXX. XX Normal Tax Credits. Credits allowed for the purpose of the normal tax include the following: (a) Interest on obligations of the United States or its instrumentalities. (b) The normal-tax exemption.

Ch. 7] 

161 

Exempt Interest. Interest received on any obligations that is wholly exempt from the income tax may be excluded from gross income in which case it should not be claimed as a credit in computing the tax. Interest on obligations of the United 

States or its instrumentalities that is subject to the surtax but is not subject to the normal tax must be included in gross income and, therefore, it may be claimed as a credit in computing the normal tax.

The Normal-Tax Exemption. Every taxpayer is entitled to a normal-tax exemption of at least $500. If married, and a joint return is filed by husband and wife, a normal-tax exemption of $1,000 may be claimed provided the adjusted gross income of each spouse is at least $500. If the adjusted gross income of one spouse is less than $500, the normal-tax exemption is $500 plus the adjusted gross income of such spouse. 

For example, if A and B are married and A has net income amounting to $3,000 and B has net income amounting to $300, but B's adjusted gross income amounts to $400, their normal-tax exemption in a joint return should be determined as follows: 

A's normal-tax exemption. 

B's normal-tax exemption.

Total exemption..... 

$500.00 400.00 $900.00

Surtax Credits. Credits allowed for the purpose of the surtax include the following: (a) A surtax exemption of $500 for the taxpayer. (b) A surtax exemption of $500 for the spouse. (c) A surtax exemption of $500 for each dependent. 

Every taxpayer is entitled to a surtax exemption of at least $500. If married and a joint return is filed by husband and wife, a surtax exemption of $1,000 in the aggregate may be claimed. 

If married and a separate return is filed by the taxpayer, and his spouse has no gross income and is not a dependent of another person, a surtax exemption of $500 may be claimed for such spouse. If married and separate returns are filed by husband and wife, both having gross income, each may claim a surtax exemption of $500 for himself, but neither spouse may claim a surtax exemption for the other spouse. 

A surtax exemption of $500 may be claimed for each dependent whose gross income is less than $500. However, if a dependent is married, a surtax exemption for such dependent will not be allowed if such dependent has made a joint return with the other spouse.

[Ch. 7 

The status of a taxpayer as to marriage is determined by his status on the last day of the taxable year unless his spouse dies during the taxable year. Thus, any taxpayer who files his return on the basis of a calendar year must be married on Decenber 31 to be entitled to claim a surtax exemption of $500 in behalf of his spouse. If his spouse dies during the taxable year, his status is determined as of the date of his spouse's death. 

Thus, any taxpayer whose spouse died any time during the taxable year may claim a surtax exemption of $500 for such spouse. 

Every taxpayer is entitled to claim a surtax exemption of $500 for each person, whose gross income is less than $500, over half of whose support was received from the taxpayer. The age or marital status of the dependent is not a material factor. It is no longer necessary for a dependent to be incapable of selfsupport. There is no status determination date for dependents. 

The sole question is whether or not the dependent has received over half of his support from the taxpayer for the calendar year in which the taxable year of the taxpayer begins. Under the 

Individual Income Tax Act of 1944, dependents are limited to the following relatives of the taxpayer: (a) A descendant, including a legally adopted child. (b) A stepchild. (c) A brother, sister, stepbrother, or stepsister, including a brother or sister by the half-blood. (d) An ancestor. (e) A stepparent. (f) A nephew or niece (but not if related only by marriage). (g) An uncle or aunt (but not if related only by marriage). (h) An in-law.

An individual who is a citizen or subject of a foreign country cannot be classified as a dependent unless such individual is a resident of the United States or of a country adjacent to the 

United States.

NORMAL TAX ON INDIVIDUALS.-There shall be levied, collected, and paid for each taxable year upon the net income of every individual a normal tax of 3 per centum of the amount of the net income in excess of the credits against net income provided in section 25(a). For alternative tax which may be elected if adjusted gross income is less than $5,000, see Supplement T. 

(Section II- -I. R. C.) 

For taxable years beginning after December 31, 1943, the normal tax rate applicable to individuals is 3%. The basis for computing the normal tax is the normal tax net income, that is, net income less the normal tax credits.

Ch. 7]

Credits Against Tax. TAXes of Foreign Countries AND POSSESSIONS 

OF UNITED STATES.-The amount of income, war-profits, and excess-profits taxes imposed by foreign countries or possessions of the United States shall be allowed as a credit against the tax, to the extent provided in section 131. 

(Section 31-I. R. C.) 

TAXES WITHHeld at SourcE.-The amount of tax withheld at the source under section 143 or 144 shall be allowed as a credit against the tax. 

(Section 32-I. R. C.) CREDIT FOR OVERPAYMENTS.-For credit against the tax of overpay. 

ments of taxes imposed by this chapter for other taxable years, see section 322.

(Section 33-I. R. C.) CREDIT FOR TAX WITHHELD ON WAGES.-The amount deducted and withheld as tax under Subchapter D of Chapter 9 during any calendar year upon the wages of any individual shall be allowed as a credit to the recipient of the income against the tax imposed by this chapter for the taxable year beginning in such calendar year. If more than one taxable year begins in any such calendar year such amount shall be allowed as a credit against the tax for the last taxable year so beginning. 

(Section 35-I. R. C.) 

In addition to the normal tax credits and surtax credits allowed in computing the tax, certain credits are allowable against the total income tax. These credits may be classified as follows: 

(a) Income taxes imposed by foreign countries and possessions of the United States. (b) Taxes withheld at the source by corporations issuing tax-free covenant bonds. (c) Overpayments of income taxes in prior years.

Ch. 7] 

165 

(d) Taxes withheld by employers from wages earned during the calendar year.

Taxes of Foreign Countries and Possessions of the United States. A citizen of the United States who has received income from foreign countries and paid an income tax thereon, is entitled to deduct the amount of the tax paid from the income tax computed on his entire net income. There are two limitations on the amount of the credit: (1) the cred for taxes paid to any one country may not exceed that proportion of the income tax which the net income from such country bears to the total net income; and (2) the credit for taxes paid to all foreign countries may not exceed that proportion of the income tax which the net income from such countries bears to the total net in come. The following case illustrates the first limitation: 

Taxpayer, a citizen, single, with no dependents has a $4,000.00 net income from sources within foreign country A and a $6,000.00 net income from sources within the United 

States. The income tax paid to foreign country A is $1,150.00. 

Net Income from sources within foreign country A... $ 4,000.00 

Net Income from sources within the United States... 

Total net income... 

Total Federal income tax on $10,000.00...

Limitation on credit for tax paid to foreign country A ($4,000/$10,000X$2,755.00)... 

The foreign tax is allowed as a credit to the extent of $1,102.00 and the balance of the tax is $1,653.00 ($2,755.00 minus $1,120.00) 6,000.00 $10,000.00 $2,755.00 .$ 1,102.00

Taxes Withheld at Source. In the past corporations have at times issued bonds and similar obligations which contained an agreement on the part of the corporation to reimburse the bondholders for the income taxes required to be paid on the interest earned, In the case of such obligations, called "tax-free covenant" bonds, which were issued before January 1, 1934, the corporation is required to withhold from the bondholder and pay to the government a tax of 2% of the interest on the bonds. 

The bondholder may in turn deduct the amount from the total of his income tax. Thus, a taxpayer who received $300.00 during the taxable year as interest on tax-free covenant bonds issued in 1930 would include the entire amount as gross income and then deduct 2% of $300.00, or $6.00, as a credit against his income tax. No credit is allowable on such bonds issued on or after 

January 1, 1934 and the interest thereon is treated in exactly the same manner as interest on other corporation bonds.

[Ch. 7 

Tax Withheld on Wages. Since 1943 employers have been authorized by law to withhold a percentage of the wages earned by employees for income tax purposes. The amounts withheld by the employer during any calendar year may be claimed as a credit against the income tax of the taxpayer for the taxable year beginning in that calendar year, even though all or any part of the amounts withheld have not been paid to the government by the employer. For all wage earners who use the calendar year basis in filing their income tax returns, the taxes withheld by their employers during the calendar year 1944 should be claimed as a credit against their income tax on the return to be filed on or before March 15, 1945. If the total amount withheld by the employer is in excess of the actual tax for the year, the excess is considered an overpayment which may be refunded to the taxpayer or may be applied as a credit against any tax then due or any tax that will be payable in the future. 

Nonresident Aliens. Special provisions regarding the tax rates on nonresident alien individuals are contained in section 211 of the Internal Revenue Code. If the aggregate amount of dividends, rents, salaries, wages, premiums, annuities, compensations, remunerations, emoluments, gains, profits, and income from sources within the United States, received by a nonresident alien individual who is not engaged in trade or business within the United States, is not in excess of $15,400.00, the tax rate is 

30%. This tax is in lieu of the regular normal tax and surtax. 

If the aggregate income from sources within the United States is in excess of $15,400.00, the rates applicable to citizens and residents apply except that the aggregate of the normal tax and surtax shall not be less than 30% of such income. The special taxing provisions do not apply to a resident of any country in 

North, Central, or South America, or in the West Indies, or of

Newfoundland, if there is in effect a treaty with such country which provides otherwise. At present Canada is the only country which has such a treaty with the United States; it was ratified on August 13, 1937.

CREDITS AGAINST INCOME.-In the case of a nonresident alien individual who is not a resident of a contiguous country, the normal tax exemption allowed by section 25 (a) (3) shall be only $500 and the surtax exemptions allowed by section 25 (b) (1) (B) and (C) shall not be allowed. 

(Section 214-I. R. C.) 

A nonresident alien individual who is not a resident of a contiguous country is entitled to but one exemption in computing both his normal tax and his surtax. This exemption amounts to

Ch. 7] 167 

$500 regardless of his marital status, or age, or whether or not he has any dependents.

CREDITS AGAINST TAX.-A nonresident alien individual shall not be allowed the credits against the tax for taxes of foreign countries and possessions of the United States allowed by section 131. A nonresident alien individual shall be allowed as a credit against his tax the amount required by section 396 to be paid by the personal service corporation of which he is a shareholder with respect to his tax liability under Supplement S. 

(Section 216-I. R. C.) 

While nonresident alien individuals are not entitled to any credit against the tax on account of the taxes imposed by foreign countries and possessions of the United States, they are entitled to credit against the tax on account of taxes withheld at the source.

Optional Tax. For taxable years beginning after December 31, 1943, individuals with an adjusted gross income of less than $5,000 may elect to use an optional tax table in determining their tax liability in lieu of computing the tax by applying the normal and surtax rates. The official table is reproduced on pages 168 and 169.

The optional tax table is available to all qualified taxpayers regardless of the source of income. Persons ineligible to use the optional tax table include the following: (a) Nonresident aliens. (b) Estates. (c) Trusts. (d) Individuals making returns for a period of less than twelve months due to a change of accounting period. (e) Married persons, under certain conditions. 

If husband and wife each has less than $5,000 adjusted gross income, both must use the optional table or neither one may do so. If one spouse has less than $5,000 adjusted gross income, but the other has $5,000 or over, the latter must elect to take the optional standard deduction of $500 or the former may not use the optional tax table. In the case of death of one spouse, the survivor may use the optional table only if— (a) the decedent has adjusted gross income of less than $5,000 and the optional table was used in his last return; or (b) the decedent has adjusted gross income of $5,000 or over and the standard deduction of $500 was used in his last return.

84 84 86 86 5588487888888FNRPFFRIRL 64 64 66 67 68 76 77 82 83 86 90 92 94 95 96 98 360 99 99 470 370 270 170 100 100 480 380 280 180 102 102 122 107 132 109 Normal tax exemption: In joint return of husband and wife, tax is to be reduced by 3% of the smaller adjusted gross income, but not by more than $15.

[Ch. 7 

The optional tax table may be used by married persons who unite in filing a joint return provided their aggregate adjusted gross income is less than $5,000. However, a special computation is required in determining their tax liability. If the adjusted gross income of each spouse is $500 or over, 3% of $500 or $15 should be deducted from the tax shown in the table. If the adjusted gross income of either spouse is less than $500, 3% of the smaller adjusted gross income should be deducted from the tax shown in the table.

The number of surtax exemptions to which the taxpayer is entitled determines the proper column of the table to be used in ascertaining the tax. Thus, a single taxpayer with no dependents should use Column No. I because he is entitled to one surtax exemption. A married taxpayer with no dependents should use Column No. 2 because he is entitled to two surtax exemptions—one for himself and one for his spouse. A married taxpayer with two dependent childred should use Column No. 4 because he is entitled to four surtax exemptions-one for himself, one for his spouse, and one for each child. 

The tax shown in the optional table is arrived at by taking the average adjusted gross income in each bracket, reducing it by 10% as an allowance in lieu of certain deductions and credits, and applying the normal tax and surtax rates. No credit may be claimed for income taxes imposed by foreign countries or possessions of the U. S. or for taxes withheld at the source on taxfree covenant bonds.

The advisability of electing to use the optional tax table in lieu of the normal and surtax rates can only be determined by actual computation of the tax under both methods. However, where the allowable deductions that may not be subtracted in computing adjusted gross income do not amount to more than 10% of the adjusted gross income, it will usually be satisfactory to use the optional tax table. Such deductions include property taxes on the taxpayer's residence, state sales taxes on personal expenditures, interest on personal indebtedness, charitable contributions, medical expenses, and investment expenses. Business expenses are not to be considered as they are deductible in computing adjusted gross income.

Ch. 7] 

171 

 ILLUSTRATIVE CASES Case 7-1. W. E. Lindsey is married and is the sole support of one dependent minor child and his aged father and mother.

His wife has no income of her own. If they unite in filing a single return jointly what amount of (a) normal tax exemption and (b) surtax exemption may be claimed as credits in computing the tax?

Solution. Since Mrs. Lindsey had no income of her own a normal-tax exemption of $500 may be claimed. However, in computing the surtax an exemption of $500 each may be claimed for (a) Mr. Lindsey, (b) Mrs. Lindsey, (c) the child, and (d) each of Mr. Lindsey's parents making a total surtax exemption' of $2,500.

Case 7-2. What would be the effect on the normal-tax exemption if Mrs. Lindsey's adjusted gross income amounted to (a) $200 or (b) $600? 

Solution. (a) $700. (b) $1,000.

In (a) the normal-tax exemption is $500 for Mr. Lindsey plus the adjusted gross income of Mrs. Lindsey. 

In (b) the normal-tax exemption is $500 for each spouse, or a total of $1,000.

Case 7-6. Compute the total tax liability, assuming that 

Mr. and Mrs. Lindsey file separate returns both using the optional tax table instead of the percentage rate method. Mr. 

Lindsey's deductions consist of property taxes on his residence, $110; interest on personal indebtedness, $60; and charitable contributions, $30.

Solution. 

Mr. Lindsey's Return 

Adjusted gross income.... $4,200.00

Number of surtax exemptions. 

Amount of tax per table.. $ 460.00

Mr. Lindsey's adjusted gross income is the same as his gross income for the reason that he has no business deductions. 

It will be noted that the use of the optional tax table results in a tax of only $460 as compared with $505 under the percentage rate method. The difference is largely accounted for by the fact that his actual deductions amount to only $200 whereas the optional tax table automatically allows him a deduction of 10% of his adjusted gross income, or $420. If his allowable deductions actually amounted to $420 his tax, using the percentage rate method, would amount to only $454.40 or $5.60 less than the tax computed by the optional table method. This difference is accounted for by the fact that his adjusted gross income of

[Ch. 7 

$4,200 is the minimum amount in the bracket whereas the tax is arrived at by taking the average amount in the bracket. In other words, his tax under the optional table method would be no greater if his adjusted gross income amounted to $4,249.99. 

Mrs. Lindsey's Return

Adjusted gross income... 

Number of surtax exemptions.

It will be noted that the use of the optional tax table by Mrs. Lindsey results in a tax of only $12 as compared with $23 under the percentage rate method. The difference is accounted for by the fact that her only deductions are business deductions which are allowable in computing adjusted gross income. The table automatically allows her an additional deduction of 10% from adjusted gross income or $60. If she had actual allowable deductions amounting to $60 in excess of her business deductions her tax, using the percentage rate method, would amount to only $9.20 or $2.80 less than the tax computed by the optional table method.

When husband and wife file a joint return and use the optional table method of ascertaining the tax, it is necessary to determine the tax under the table and to reduce it by 3% of the smaller adjusted gross income, but not by more than $15 in order to find the actual tax liability. 

It will be noted that if Mr. and Mrs. Lindsey unite in filing a joint return and use the optional table method in ascertaining the tax, the total tax liability will amount to only $469 as compared with $472 ($460 + $12) on separate returns. After exercising all of the options that are permissible under the law, it

Ch. 7] 175 

has been found that it is best for Mr. and Mrs. Lindsey to unite in filing a joint return and to use the optional table method in computing their tax.

Following is a comparison of the tax under the different methods of reporting that are permissible: (a) Joint return using percentage rate method.....$530.00 (b) Separate returns using percentage rate method. 528.00 (c) Separate returns using optional table method.. 472.00 (d) Joint return using optional table method...... 469.00

Case 7-8. Roy E. Cooper has a net income of $90,000 for the current taxable year exclusive of capital gains. He also has gains of $100,000 derived from the sale of capital assets held more than six months. He is entitled to the following credits in computing the tax:

QUESTIONS AND PROBLEMS 

7-1. Distinguish between allowable deductions and credits against net income and explain how each should be classified in the return for an individual. 

7-2. An analysis of the accounts kept by Henry Cameron shows that he has received dividends during the taxable year as follows: 

(a) A cash dividend received from the American Steel Company, a domestic corporation, which is itself subject to the income tax... (b) A stock dividend received from the 

Wheeler Manufacturing Company, a domestic corporation, which is itself subject to the income tax... (c) A cash dividend received from the Canadian Milling Company, a foreign corporation....... $1,400.00 800.00 1,750.00 

How should each of these dividends be classified on 

Mr. Cameron's income tax return?

Ch. 7] 

177 

7-3. Under what conditions is a taxpayer entitled to the following normal-tax exemptions: (a) An exemption of $500. (b) An exemption of $1,000. 

7-4. A, a widower with two dependent children, married 

B on August 20 of the current taxable year. He continued to support his children even though one child became 18 years of age on November II of the taxable year. Assuming that both 

A and B have adjusted gross incomes of more than $500 each, what amounts of normal-tax exemption and surtax exemption will A and B be entitled to if- (a) separate returns are filed, or (b) a joint return is filed. 

7-5. Charles Springer is a single man. His father, who is totally blind, is dependent upon him for support. Anna Springer, his fifteen year old sister, is also dependent upon him. Both live with him. What exemptions can Charles Springer claim in his income tax return? 

7-6. If H. C. Hewitt changes from a fiscal to a calendar year basis with the approval of the Commissioner and has a net income of $1,600.00 for a period of nine months, what will be the amount of his annual income? 

7-7. Irwin Bell is a married man with three dependent children. His adjusted gross income for the current calendar year amounted to $5,400 from which $609.60 was withheld by his employer for income tax purposes. His allowable personal deductions amount to $400. His wife had no separate income or deductions. (a) Is it necessary for Mr. Bell to file a return for the taxable year? If so, when? (b) May he elect to let the collector compute his tax? (c) May Mr. and Mrs. Bell unite in filing a joint return or must Mr. Bell file a separate return? (d) If a joint return may be filed should Mr. Bell elect to take the optional standard deduction of $500 or should he take his actual deductions? (e) How many surtax exemptions may be claimed in a joint return? (f) Compute Mr. Bell's income tax liability assuming that he files his return on the most favorable basis.

[Ch. 7 

7-8. George Vine had gross income for the calendar year amounting to $2,942.50 derived from wages from which his employer withheld a total of $494 during the year for income tax purposes.

His personal deductions amount to $190. His wife had gross income entirely from salary amounting to $1,850 from which her employer withheld a total of $265.20 during the year for income tax purposes. Her personal deductions amounted to $30. Advise them as to whether they should file separate returns or a joint return and compute their tax in the most favorable manner.

If you advise them to file separate returns and let the collector compute their tax, when must their returns be filed and when will they be required to pay any balance due? If you advise them to file separate returns and compute their own tax, when must their returns be filed and when will they be required to pay any balance due?

Samuel Black. Mr. Black is the chief support of his aged parents, neither of whom has income amounting to as much as $500. 

Mr. Black:

Note. Obviously, it will be to the advantage of Mr. and Mrs. Black to file separate returns because of the operation of the graduated surtax rates. 

It will also be to their advantage to elect to take the optional standard deduction in lieu of their actual deductions. In order to get the benefit of this option it will be necessary for Mr. Black to elect the $500 standard deduction and for Mrs. Black to use the optional tax table under which she will automatically get the benefit of a standard deduction of 10% of her adjusted gross income.

Ch. 7] 179

(b) Assuming that he files a declaration and pays the initial installment on March 15 of the taxable year, when will it be necessary for him to pay additional installments and what will be the amount of each additional installment?
